#2945. Doug Christie provides honest explanation about being on hot seat for Kings

Doug Christie provides honest explanation about being on hot seat for Kings originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

SACRAMENTO — The more the Kings lose, the more the big-picture questions arise.

The Kings fell to 1-11 in a 120-107 loss to the Portland Trail Blazers on Friday at Golden 1 Center, extending their franchise-record home winless streak to seven games to begin the 2026-27 NBA season.

To be sure, expectations weren't high surrounding Doug Christie's team coming in. General manager Scott Perry has made it clear his team is in early stages of a multi-season rebuilding project around promising rookie Darius Acuff Jr.

But Sacramento has the second-worst record in the NBA –– only the 1-12 Utah Jazz and Milwaukee Bucks are worse –– and currently is on pace to finish with seven wins, worse than the 1972-73 Philadelphia 76ers, who had the worst 82-game record in NBA history at 9-73.

That futility has led to a swelling conversation about the immediate future of Kings coach Doug Christie, who is 23-70 since having the interim tag removed from his title before last season.

Christie was asked Friday about that conversation and if he feels the growing pressure as the Kings dropped their fifth straight.

"I think the seat is hot, always," Christie said. "The nature of this business, it's all about winning. So that's where you think the seat is always hot."

Christie's answer reflected a sense of urgency that any NBA coach could relate to. But he also knows he's the Kings' eighth head coach since the current ownership group headed by Vivek Ranadivé assumed control in 2013.

Christie had a front-row seat to the firing of Mike Brown in December 2024, just less than two seasons after being voted as the unanimous coach of the year and helping end the Kings' 17-year NBA playoff drought, the longest in league history. Brown, of course, won a championship last season with the New York Knicks.

Christie was an assistant on Brown's staff, and leaped a handful of Brown assistants for the head job after Brown was fired while on the way to the airport to travel to a road game against the Los Angeles Lakers. Christie and Perry remade the staff the following offseason when Perry came on as general manager and made Christie the full-time head coach.

The Kings went 22-60 in the Christie's first full season. Landing Acuff with the No. 7 pick in the 2026 NBA Draft served as the silver lining in what proved to be a lost season while Domantas Sabonis, Keegan Murray and Zach LaVine missed most of it with injuries.

Christie indicated he understands why he's in the crosshairs of some Kings fans unhappy with the product on the court. But he's trying not to focus on that, he said.

"What's for me is going to be for me," Christie said. "I'm always going to do my best, continue to improve in every aspect of whatever it is I do. No one's going to outwork me when it comes to that. So that's not something I worry about.

"In moments, do you feel like, 'ugh'? Yeah, of course," he continued. "But then I go find my center and understand that I'm a leader of men and an organization, and I have to come with the proper energy, the proper messaging, all those things to continue to lead them forward."

Christie pointed to Friday's performance as a positive despite the final score. The Kings had good energy throughout the game, Christie said. They lost by getting outscored at the 3-point line by 18 points and the free-throw line by 11 more.

They lost while outscoring the Blazers in points in the paint (46-38), second-chance points (12-6), and fast-break points (20-10). It wasn't a blowout that has become too common early this season.

Sabonis rebounded from his scoreless performance on Tuesday with 21 points and 12 rebounds, while Murray had his best game of the year with 16 points, seven rebounds and four assists in his return to the starting lineup for the first time since the second game of the season.

The Kings were led in scoring by DeAndre Hunter's 24, while Acuff had his streak of 20-plus points snapped at seven games by finishing with 17 on 6-of-19 shooting. Acuff had just seven points through three quarters but scored 10 in the fourth.

Still, those developments were far from enough to overcome Portland. And there's little proof the Kings will bring the energy they brought Friday consistently as the season continues.

The schedule remains tough with the next game Sunday against the Oklahoma City Thunder, Wednesday versus the Phoenix Suns before a back-to-back on the road against the Lakers and home to the Boston Celtics next Friday and Saturday.

"But I'm always searching because leadership is something that I take seriously when it comes to (the players) and trying to find a pathway for them to be successful," Christie said. "…And if you've ever been through anything in life, you don't look at the totality of it. You look at it in bite sizes.

"A lot of people can be overwhelmed by that. I'm just not built that way. So therefore, I won't let my players be built that way, and that's how we get through."

#2944. John Harbaugh breaks down key — and controversial — moments from Giants loss

Cam Skattebo didn't just forget to push the tush.

Giants head coach John Harbaugh explained Friday that the failed third-and-1 quarterback sneak by Jameis Winston late in the fourth quarter was not a traditional Tush Push and thus it was not detrimental that it took Skattebo nearly two seconds to fire out of his pre-snap stance.

"That was a different kind of play, that was a normal quarterback sneak," Harbaugh said. "It had a certain check involved in it that is part of the play so he wasn't supposed to be up there pushing."

Perhaps the clearest evidence that it is not a cover-up is that fullback Patrick Ricard was lined up as a tight end and not pushing Winston, either.

New York Giants running back Cam Skattebo reacts on the bench during the second quarter against the Washington Commanders, Thursday, Nov. 12, 2026. Charles Wenzelberg/NY Post

The failed third-down conversion led to the now-infamous "Who let the dogs out?" cadence on fourth-and-1 at the 11-yard line.

"It's not about what you say, exactly," Harbaugh said. "While it was, I think, probably fun on his part, the way the cadence sounds was an attempt to draw him offsides. That they would hear something different. They are going to jump based on the fact that they are too antsy and as soon as they hear the word and anticipate the snap, they are going to jump. They are not trying to define the word you said."

New York Giants quarterback Jameis Winston (19) warms up before an NFL football game against the Washington Commanders in East Rutherford, N.J., Thursday, Nov. 12, 2026. Getty Images

Harbaugh said that in hindsight he wouldn't have wasted time with the trickery and taking a delay of game penalty that backed up punter Jordan Stout.

"If I had to do it again, I wouldn't even try to do it," Harbaugh said. "It didn't end up hurting us in the end. They are probably not going to jump there. [The Commanders] are a team that's jumped offside a bunch in those situations. They probably have been emphasizing it to a high degree, and they weren't budging."

"You say, 'Who let the dogs out?' and then you snap the ball," Harbaugh said. "Maybe that would've been a better strategy."

Snap the ball at the 11-yard line while protecting a four-point lead? That's Lions head coach Dan Campbell-levels of gutsy.

"I'm kind of kicking myself," Harbaugh said. "Maybe we should've gone for that one. You can kind of get as aggressive as you want. We're not afraid. We've done that before [in Baltimore]. History would tell you that."

New York Giants head coach John Harbaugh speaks during a press conference after an NFL football game in East Rutherford, N.J., Thursday, Nov. 12, 2026. AP Photo/Seth Wenig

All six of kicker Dominic Zvada's missed field goals this season have come from the right hash.

"There is a technique issue there with his plant foot that we're looking at," Harbaugh said. "He needs to clean that up from a consistency standpoint. There is an issue on the right hash, no question."

The Giants punted three times across midfield: Fourth-and-12 at the 42-yard line, fourth-and-3 at the 46 and fourth-and-5 at the 41.

"When we were talking about them during the game, we just felt like they were out of range to go for it," Harbaugh said. "A lot of it was the analytics. But even feeling comfortable about doing it. We may be more aggressive in the future than we were."

#2942. The Blockchain Boom Is Still Happening Behind the Scenes. These 3 Growth Stocks Will Benefit.

Blockchain is being used silently in key products and services that people use. It's good for these three stocks.

Blockchain technology is one of the most innovative technologies that can compound wealth for savvy investors, but blockchain won't feel as mainstream as Bitcoin.

One of the main takeaways from the Assets on Blockchain Conference is that the next 1 billion blockchain users won't be onboarded into blockchain. Instead, they will be onboarded to blockchain-based products. For instance, people do not need to know how Apple Pay's technology works to use the service.

Speakers at the event highlighted how blockchain can make the financial grid more efficient and bring greater transparency to alternative assets such as real estate. You don't have to create blockchain technology to ride this trend. These three growth stocks are well-positioned to reward investors seeking greater exposure to blockchain.

Block (XYZ +2.82%), formerly known as Square, has been at the forefront of blockchain technology for many years. It was a pioneer in enabling crypto transactions for everyday products and services. The company's Cash App makes it easier for people to spend, save, and invest, and it uses blockchain technology for some of its services.

Blockchain technology helps Block support cryptocurrencies and stablecoins on its app. The fintech company has been expanding beyond Bitcoin to give its users more flexibility.

Block also continues to gain market share in its small-business services, which helps explain the company's aim for a 69% year-over-year boost in adjusted operating income in fiscal 2027.

IBM (IBM +1.00%) reinvented itself in recent years. Cloud computing and enterprise AI solutions have put the dividend stock back on the map. Its shares more than doubled over the past year despite a sluggish year-to-date performance.

Its software, consulting, and infrastructure segments all trended upward in the third quarter, resulting in 5% year-over-year overall revenue growth. IBM CEO Arvind Krishna noted in the press release that the company's artificial intelligence was driving transformations across multiple businesses.

On the blockchain side, IBM Blockchain Platform is an enterprise-grade blockchain-as-a-service software that helps financial institutions, logistics companies, retailers, and other corporations track the movements of products and services more accurately.

For instance, IBM's blockchain has reduced food waste and can pinpoint bad batches during an E. coli outbreak. That way, healthy food doesn't have to be thrown out.

It shows that blockchain already improves people's lives without it being a front-and-center part of market news like AI stocks.

Although Amazon (AMZN -0.29%) rolled back its Amazon Managed Blockchain service, its cloud platform remains the backbone of most blockchain platforms. Amazon Web Services offers the compute, storage, and developer tools that companies need to create and maintain public blockchains.

Amazon's cloud segment has been reaccelerating over multiple quarters, and it does not look like growth is slowing. AWS sales jumped by 39% year over year, propelling overall revenue up by 12% year over year. It was the highest growth rate for AWS in nearly five years, and the cloud platform now generates $184 billion in annualized revenue.

Amazon's cloud platform also has higher margins than its e-commerce business, translating into meaningful profit expansion. Companies that use Amazon Web Services must continue to pay monthly fees and upgrade their plans as their storage and compute needs evolve.

Grandview Research projects a substantial 88.2% CAGR for blockchain technology through 2033. That build-out will require large investments in cloud computing, and Amazon is one of the biggest winners of that trade.

AWS is part of a broader business model that includes opportunities such as AI agents, online advertising, robotics, AI chips, and other segments. The tech stock has rewarded investors with a 17% year-to-date return.

#2940. Why Advanced Micro Devices Stock Surged This Week

It was a bumpy week for chip stocks and AI-related names.

A major host-infrastructure deal with Google Cloud, alongside expanding data center demand, sent Advanced Micro Devices (AMD -8.75%) soaring 19% this week.

Advanced Micro Devices stock changed hands at $534.06 as of Friday's close, adding about 19% from Friday, Nov. 6's close. Over the same period, the S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.40%) rose 2.5%, and the Nasdaq-100 was up 2.4%.

The rally gained traction after AI cloud provider CoreWeave reported strong quarterly results and raised its 2026 capital expenditure guidance to $37 billion to $39 billion, increasing the lower end of the range. That massive infrastructure spending plan reassures investors that tech companies continue buying data center processors at a rapid pace. At the same time, Google Cloud announced that AMD's 5th Gen EPYC server processors will power the host infrastructure for its new confidential computing systems, reinforcing AMD's market share gains in enterprise computing.

Advanced Micro Devices designs computer processors and graphics chips and sells data center servers and PC components to cloud providers and computer manufacturers that build high-performance systems. The company's EPYC line of server microprocessors powers cloud data centers, while its Instinct graphics processors handle complex artificial intelligence workloads. Winning major cloud deployments expands AMD's addressable market beyond traditional personal computers and positions its hardware at the foundation of modern cloud infrastructure.

In its last reported quarter, which ended Sept. 26, 2026, AMD generated $13.4 billion in revenue, up 45% year over year, while data center sales nearly doubled to $8.5 billion. Fresh data center deals and competitive shifts invert concerns over market share losses, establishing AMD as an indispensable alternative supplier for large-scale artificial intelligence infrastructure.

AMD stock trades at a trailing price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio of 117.6. That rich valuation reflects high market expectations for sustained earnings growth as cloud providers ramp up processor orders.

Investors will closely watch Nvidia's upcoming quarterly report and AMD's next earnings release on Feb. 2, 2027, to confirm whether data center hardware demand maintains its trajectory. Any slowdown in hyperscaler capital budgets could quickly compress AMD's elevated valuation multiple.

Investors tracking the AI sector were shocked this week by revelations about sector leader Nvidia and its former CEO, Jensen Huang. The tumultuous news impacted chip stocks like AMD all week. AMD could be viewed as a beneficiary of Nvidia's problems, and AMD stock was up as much as 30% at its peak this week.

But company-specific fundamentals also drove the results this week, and that's where long-term investors should focus. The AI build-out should ultimately survive the turbulence driven by Nvidia this week, and investors will be listening closely to what the company says when it reports its quarterly results next week.

If investors expect data center momentum to continue, AMD should remain a stock to own as an aggressive holding in a portfolio. While its valuation is high, the sector's growth rate could still justify it. And the company could also benefit from Nvidia's internal woes.

Operational missteps will always be a risk, but AMD appears well positioned to capitalize on growth in the AI sector now more than ever.

#2936. Cisco Just Raised Its AI Forecast Again. Is it a Buy?

The networking giant's AI business is growing much faster than its valuation suggests.

Cisco Systems (CSCO -6.24%) probably isn't the first name that comes to mind for most investors when they hear the term "AI stocks." Many investors think of it as a legacy tech company. But its latest earnings report suggests it belongs on the list.

Cisco's revenue rose 24% year over year to $18.5 billion in its fiscal first quarter, and adjusted earnings per share jumped 40% to $1.40. Both beat analysts' expectations. Networking revenue, which includes the switches and routers that connect AI data centers, grew 43%.

The biggest number was the forecast. Cisco now expects $9 billion in AI infrastructure revenue from hyperscalers in fiscal 2027, up from the $7.5 billion it forecast in August and more than double the roughly $4 billion it delivered in fiscal 2026. CEO Chuck Robbins said the company is "still early in the networking super cycle driven by AI deployments with enterprise adoption just beginning."

Management also raised its full-year guidance to $74.8 billion-$75.8 billion in revenue and $5.47-$5.53 in adjusted EPS. Both of these were above analyst expectations.

Despite the strong report, Cisco shares fell more than 6% the following day. And to be fair, the report had some weak spots.

For one thing, higher memory costs and a shift toward hardware pushed its adjusted gross margin down 2.5 percentage points to 65.6%. Product order growth slowed to 21% from 35% the prior quarter. After a roughly 30% gain year-to-date heading into the report, it's fair to say that expectations were high.

Even after this year's run, Cisco trades for about 17 times the midpoint of its fiscal 2027 earnings guidance, based on its share price of about $96 as of this writing. That's an attractive valuation for a company whose AI revenue is set to more than double and whose overall top line is growing at over 20%. Cisco also pays a quarterly dividend of $0.42 per share (a roughly 1.8% yield), and it bought back $2 billion of stock last quarter. Both of these should help drive total returns for investors going forward.

To be sure, Cisco's margins are worth watching. If memory costs stay high, Cisco's earnings growth could lag its revenue growth. But I think the market still values Cisco as a slow-growing hardware company, and that's no longer the full picture. For investors who want AI exposure without paying a sky-high multiple, Cisco deserves a closer look.

#2932. Remembering a Random Packers Game: The Minnesota Vikings

The last time I wrote about a random game between the Green Bay Packers and Minnesota Vikings, I covered a 1999 matchup the Packers had won. The 2026 Packers proceeded to lose in spectacular fashion to open the season, and I think it's because I jinxed them. Following that same logic, I'm using my voodoo magic to conjure up a win for Week 10. This week's random Packers vs. Vikings game is the 90th meeting between the two teams, when the Packers lost 20-17 on November 21st, 2005.

The 2005 Packers were…pretty bad! They lost WR Javon Walker to a torn ACL in Week 1, and both RBs Ahman Green and Najeh Davenport were placed on IR with leg injuries. With a 2-7 record coming into their matchup against the 4-5 Vikings, the Packers had turned the ball over 20 times already. This was a far cry from the team who had won the NFC North at 10-6 the previous year while having two WRs with 1,200+ yards (Javon Walker and Donald Driver) AND a 1,100 yard rusher (Ahman Green). The team had already lost to Minnesota by 3 points in Week 7—it was time to right the ship.

On the Vikings' side, they had recently lost starting QB Daunte Culpepper to a catastrophic knee injury. Taking his place was veteran Brad Johnson, who had the Vikings on a bit of a hot streak after two straight wins. Losing Culpepper certainly wasn't ideal, but the Vikings were finding ways to win.

After a missed 49-yard field goal attempt by the Vikings on their opening drive, QB Brett Favre and the Packers offense responded by going 3 and out. The Vikings offense proceeded to do exactly the same thing. The first score of the game came on the Packers' next drive after a couple of big plays, including a 30 yard pass to RB Samkon Gado, and then a 15 yard pass to WR Donald Driver in the endzone.

The Vikings' next drive ended with an interception by S Nick Collins. Things were looking up for the Packers! Just kidding. Both offenses proceeded to combine for 6 straight punts. Favre then decided enough was enough—someone needed to score! So he threw a 51 yard pick-six to CB Dovonte Edwards.

With about 30 seconds left in the 2nd quarter, the gunslinger rattled off a 53-yard yard pass to Donald Driver, and the Packers found themselves with a 14-7 lead at halftime.

After more unsavory play by both offenses, the Vikings tied it up 14-14 in the 3rd quarter after RB Ciatrick Fason rushed for a 1 yard TD, capping off a nearly 9 minute drive. This would be the final touchdown scored by either team. The teams proceeded to trade punts and field goals, and with 3 seconds left, Vikings K Paul Edinger nailed the 27 yard dagger. Packers lose, 20-17.

Brett Favre finished the day 20 of 33 for 227 yards, 2 TDs, and 2 INTs. Tony Fisher led the team on the ground, finishing with a whopping 14 yards on 4 carries, while Donald Driver finished with 5 catches on 9 targets for 84 yards and 2 TDs.

All the big moments from this game can be found here.

The Packers fell to 2-8 on the season, and would go on to win just 2 more games all year, finishing 4-12 for the team's first losing record since 1991 (also 4-12). The 2005 Packers turned the ball over 45 times, while scoring just 31 TDs on offense. HC Mike Sherman was fired less than a year after receiving a contract extension, and the team replaced him with Another Mike™, Mike McCarthy. Their 4-12 record earned them the 5th overall pick in the 2006 NFL Draft, which they used to select LB A.J. Hawk.

The Green Bay Packers and Minnesota Vikings will meet for the 133rd time on Sunday, during the day's early slate of games at 1pm ET/12pm CT.

#2931. Lakers stars Luka Doncic, Austin Reaves earn different status for battle with Suns

The Lakers received positive injury news following multiple scares in Wednesday's 123-112 win over the Bucks.

Lakers coach JJ Redick said Austin Reaves suffered a left heel bruise in the third quarter of that game and is listed as day-to-day ahead of Sunday's contest against the Suns.

The Lakers' medical staff and Reaves jointly determined that he didn't need further imaging after seeing how he felt Thursday morning. Reaves had X-rays Wednesday evening and they came back clean.

When asked if Reaves' injury was minor, Redick didn't hesitate.

"I would assume a day-to-day injury is minor, yeah," Redick said.

Austin Reaves #15 of the Los Angeles Lakers shoots a free throw against the Milwaukee Bucks on Wednesday, Nov. 11, 2026, at Fiserv Forum in Milwaukee. NBAE via Getty Images

As for Luka Doncic, who tweaked his right ankle in the fourth quarter of Wednesday's game?

"Luka is fine," Redick said. "I think the expectation would be he's available."

Collin Sexton is also listed as day-to-day after receiving multiple stitches to close a deep cut over his right eye after he and Bucks guard Ryan Rollins bumped heads in the fourth quarter.

Redick said a concussion was ruled out for Sexton.

Meanwhile, Quentin Grimes, who missed the Lakers' last two games because of a sprained right pinkie, participated in the live portion of the "stay ready game" at Friday's practice.

Los Angeles Lakers' Collin Sexton (10) dribbles against Milwaukee Bucks' Ryan Rollins (13) during a game in Milwaukee on Wednesday, Nov. 11, 2026. Getty Images

Grimes, who had his pinkie taped, said the swelling has gone down and his finger is "basically back to normal."

"I definitely expect to play, for sure," Grimes said of Sunday.

It's all positive news for the Lakers, who have won seven straight games and have the best record in the league at 11-2.

While Doncic spoke after Wednesday's game saying his ankle was "painful a little bit but I'll be fine," there was significantly more worry around Reaves, who did not speak to the media after the Lakers' win and was expected to receive more imaging upon the team's return to Los Angeles — until it was deemed unnecessary.

When Redick was asked if Doncic advised Reaves about the heel injury that he suffered in 2024, the coach said he wasn't sure.

Los Angeles Lakers' Luka Doncic looks at his ankle during an NBA basketball game against the Milwaukee Bucks in Milwaukee, Wednesday, Nov. 11, 2026. AP Photo/Aaron Gash

"I know those guys text 47 times a day, so maybe there's a chance," Redick said. "Austin said he woke up yesterday at 7 a.m. and he had four texts from Luka, so maybe one of those was the heel text. I don't know."

Following a brutal stretch of three road games in four days, including a back-to-back, the Lakers had a much-needed three days to regroup before playing the Suns.

Redick and his staff spent the last three days auditing the Lakers, who are the top-rated team in the league on offense but are No. 18 on defense.

His analytics team prepared a 21-page presentation.

"We know we want to be a top-10 defense," Redick said. "We know we are not going to be able to do that unless we become better than a bottom-10 transition team and better than a bottom 10-defensive rebounding team."

#2930. Top Wall Street analysts are upbeat about the prospects for these 3 stocks

September ended on a mixed note, with the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average falling 0.5% and 4.3%, respectively, while the Nasdaq Composite climbed 1.9%. Markets remained on edge with higher oil prices and Treasury yields weighing on investor sentiment and triggering fears of additional interest rate hikes.

In this climate, investors with a long-term horizon can track recommendations from top Wall Street analysts to find stocks with solid growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Neocloud company CoreWeave (CRWV) is benefiting from robust AI-driven demand for cloud computing.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Samik Chatterjee upgraded CoreWeave to buy from hold, raising his price target to $125 from $120. The analyst cited favorable pricing, driven by solid demand, for the upgrade.

Chatterjee said CoreWeave is now more willing to sign premium priced, shorter-term contracts, which lift his revenue and margin estimates. CRWV stock performance has been underwhelming as investors worry about high capital spending, the analyst noted. That said, he believes that higher prices and increased margins will more than offset the rise in debt to fund capital spending, improving the stock's valuation.

Chatterjee noted the 25% price hike in July across CoreWeave's product offerings and frequent price increases by rivals like Nebius (NBIS), adding that some peers are charging nearly three times more for short-term compute contracts than what CoreWeave charges on longer-term deals.

"CoreWeave's leverage of the opportunity [is] evident in its recent press release highlighting contracts signed in F3Q at ~$40M/MW," said Chatterjee. JPMorgan said higher pricing is already contributing 5-10 percentage points to contract margins versus earlier deals.

Chatterjee ranks No. 4 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 75% of the time, delivering an average return of 55.3%. See CoreWeave Statistics on TipRanks.

Palo Alto Networks (PANW) is a provider of cybersecurity solutions across network, cloud, security operations, AI and identity. Following a discussion with management, BTIG analyst Gray Powell reiterated a buy rating on PANW and raised his price target to $425 from $404.

After a review of Palo Alto's product-level growth catalysts, Powell sees clear upside to both revenue and next-generation security (NGS) annual recurring revenue (ARR) in fiscal 2027. He expects PANW to deliver pro forma revenue growth of more than 17%, above the consensus estimate of 14.6%, and NGS ARR growth of more than 26% versus the Street's expectation of 24.5%.

Over the past five years, the Palo Alto business mix, has moved to higher-growth product areas and new markets. from what was earlier viewed as a hardware-centric network security company, Powell said. The 5-star analyst estimates that hardware firewalls and attached subscriptions now account for less than 50% of revenue.

Meanwhile, high-growth solutions, including Prisma SASE, software firewalls, Cortex XSIAM, Chronosphere and Idira now represent more than 35% of PANW's revenue. "Investors may not fully realize the growth potential within PANW's platform story," Powell said.

BTIG is optimistic about the prospects of Chronosphere in observability, CyberArk in identity and software firewalls and Prisma AIRS in the network and AI security business.

Powell ranks No. 320 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.3%. See Palo Alto Networks KPIs on TipRanks.

In a research note on September 30, Rosenblatt analyst Scott Devitt reiterated a buy rating on e-commerce and cloud computing platform Amazon (AMZN), raising his price target to $360 from $335. Fears that agentic commerce poses a major threat to Amazon's advertising business seem "overstated," he said.

Devitt noted that the growing adoption of personal agents and AI shopping assistants has sparked concern about displacement of Amazon's retail media business. But the 5-star analyst remains bullish, given Amazon's ability to adapt to changing consumer behavior, as it's done in the past.

Additionally, Devitt argues that even if an AI agent helps a shopper discover a product, Amazon can still handle the purchase and delivery, keeping much of the transaction value. He highlighted that management believes that existing Amazon shoppers will likely continue to start their shopping journey on the platform because it has better personalization, accurate pricing and inventory information that third-party agents still lack.

Overall, Devitt expects Amazon's retail business to prove a net beneficiary of agentic commerce. Fewer clicks would not hurt the company if the value of each remaining click and overall volumes keep rising, he said. The argument is that even if Amazon loses some sponsored ad revenue over the next few years, it would likely be only a small part of the company's total business and could be offset by growth in other areas.

"Consistent with our AI framework, we continue to view Amazon as one of the most resilient leaders in our coverage, combining owned compute infrastructure with a physical fulfillment moat," said Devitt. His bullish view is supported, Devitt said, by expectations that Amazon Web Services (AWS) will exit the year at a more than 45% growth rate with continued margin improvement.

Devitt ranks No. 765 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 49% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Amazon Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2928. A Realistic View of What Kirk Cousins 2.0 Brings to the Vikings

The Minnesota Vikings made the move they had to make after a half-season of underwhelming quarterback performance in trading for Kirk Cousins. He will be the starter as soon as possible, which is likely to be this Sunday against the Packers. His arrival has come with much fanfare as the savior of the Vikings offense who, combined with the Vikings dominant defense and good special teams, could position the Vikings for a deep run in the post-season.

But a quarterback switching teams in mid-season is not without its difficulties, even with Cousins' familiarity with Kevin O'Connell's scheme and some of the key players around him from Justin Jefferson, Jordan Addison, and T.J. Hockenson to Brian O'Neill, Blake Brandel, and Christian Darrisaw. It's also an advantage that Cousins has been playing the entire season, so he doesn't have to get acclimated to playing again, as a backup quarterback who hasn't played would need to do.

If Cousins plays on Sunday (as expected at this point), there could be some hiccups as he works out timing and other rapport issues in his first game back after three years. But Cousins has been playing in the same type of offense under Klint Kubiak in Las Vegas, and is probably the most well-versed quarterback in Kevin O'Connell's scheme in the league- and one of the most well-suited to execute it.

The first graphic compares Cousins' rate and EPA efficiency in the play-action passing game, which is central to KOC's offense. He's been one of the more effective quarterbacks out of play-action for most of his career, and has run it at a relatively high rate for most of his 15-year career. He's also played at a relatively high rate from under center, which KOC also likes to do to make the play-action game more effective. Cousins' career passer rating from under center is 107.2, and in play-action it is 110.0. This season, Cousins' passer rating with play-action is 123.4.

The Vikings have used play-action more than the Raiders have so far this season. Part of that is game situation- both down-and-distance and score/time remaining. You would think that with the Vikings dominant defense, they would continue to be able to run play action at a high rate to maximize Cousins' strength in that area.

The second graphic represents Cousins' passing metrics to each part of the field. The big takeaway is that the lion's share of Cousins' passing yards and attempts are between the numbers. He's also had good success throwing at intermediate depth (10-19 yards) this season. That's another important element of KOC's scheme-targeting the middle of the field. Both Kyler Murray and Carson Wentz have been reluctant to target the middle of the field, which compromises the effectiveness of O'Connell's scheme. One of the staples of O'Connell's scheme is the dagger route combination, which has largely disappeared from run or targeted routes this season. It's also one of the most effective route combinations for Justin Jefferson and Jordan Addison.

Bottom line, having Kirk Cousins at quarterback should open up the entirety of KOC's playbook, but it may take some time for Cousins to re-familiarize himself with the playbook and get on the same page again with receivers. Still, I don't anticipate Cousins hesitating to get the ball out to open receivers as Murray and Wentz have done this season.

Since the last time Cousins played for the Vikings- three years ago- he had his Achilles tendon repaired and spent most of his time in Atlanta recovering from that. At that point it looked like he was done. His passes lacked velocity and his movement was slower than it had been. Apparently, he couldn't really push off of his foot with the repaired Achilles and that was negatively affecting his game. But after signing with the Raiders, it appears that Cousins has fully recovered from the Achilles injury and no longer has the issues that plagued him in Atlanta. The velocity is back and while Cousins has never been particularly mobile, he is able to move like he used to before the Achilles injury. According to Albert Breer, when the Vikings were looking into trading for Cousins, after, "meshing the scouts' and coaches' analysis, they saw Cousins as still possessing a live arm and deadly accuracy and having returned to the baseline of athleticism he set before tearing his Achilles in 2024, evidenced on tape in the keeper game that's essential to Kubiak's scheme."

I've watched all of Cousins' throws with the Raiders this year, and it is reminiscent of his previous stint with the Vikings. I don't know that his arm strength has declined much- it doesn't look like it- but there is a caveat. He can make all the throws, as you would expect from a starting NFL quarterback, but he needs to step-in to deeper throws for them to have the needed velocity. That may have been the case before as well but it is something that stood out when watching his tape this season. He can't launch balls down field with velocity off his back foot like Jordan Love or Caleb Williams. This is the impact of being his being 38 years old. Additionally, he has not become any more mobile over the last three years, and the scampering of Kyler Murray to pick up first downs here and there is definitely not in Cousins' bag, if that wasn't obvious. He has run for five-yard gains on a couple occasions this season, but that's about it. He is able to maneuver within the pocket effectively and does fine on bootlegs, but he's not going to outrun many pass rushers for very long. He's also not afraid to deliver the ball downfield in the face of oncoming pressure- and pay the price for it. He's also not afraid of throwing into tight windows and does so with regularity.

Cousins has 8 interceptions so far this season, three of which came last weekend against the 49ers. A few of them came on throws left a bit short, a few in tight window throws where ball placement and/or route were not ideal. One his receiver fell down. And the last one came with 4 minutes left in the 49ers game and he was just trying make something happen down multiple scores. But for the most part Cousins' interceptions came while being aggressive and playing on time making throws that Murray and Wentz have generally avoided this season.

Cousins hasn't been sacked much so far this season- just 11 times in 9 games. He's also the only quarterback who has not been sacked on a blitz so far this season. So, he's generally done an excellent job of avoiding the rush and getting the ball out- maybe only one of sacks can be blamed on him. And whereas Murray or Wentz may look to scramble to buy time, Cousins is mainly focused on getting the ball out. He's very much a pocket passer in that respect, with his only move typically being to step up in the pocket to avoid the edge rush.

The Raiders offense has different strengths than the Vikings, and that has influenced Cousins' play. Their offensive line is similar strength as the Vikings, anchored by All-Pro center Tyler Linderbaum. But the strength of their receiver group is their tight ends- Brock Bowers and Michael Mayer- and they run double tight-end sets at the highest rate in the league. Their receivers are Tre Tucker and former Viking Jalen Nailor. The Raiders running game is terrible- last in the league- averaging just 2.9 yards per carry. Ashton Jeanty hasn't done much as a running back, but he is a good receiving back. So, roughly 60% of Cousins' targets with the Raiders were to tight ends or running backs- mainly Jeanty.

Cousins has the third highest PFF passing grade (91.6) when targeting intermediate (10-19 yard) routes this season among starting quarterbacks and has done so at the fifth-highest rate (24.8%). Most of those targets were to tight ends between the numbers. Cousins is likely to target wide receivers more with the Vikings- as he did in 2022-23- as that is the strength of the Vikings receiver group. But the route concepts the Vikings employ will likely have him continue to target intermediate routes over the middle at a fairly high clip, complemented by outside zone runs that can spread the defense out and make linebackers bite on play action.

In addition to having a poor running game, the Raiders also have the last ranked defense in both points and yards allowed. They've allowed at least 30 points in each of their last six games, which has put Cousins under pressure to keep pace. That has affected the game situation and play calling for the Raiders, which pass just over 60% of the time- one of the higher rates in the league.

Still, despite a poor supporting cast when it comes to the running game and defense, Cousins has managed a top ten ranking (7th) across a composite of key quarterback metrics this season. That's leaps and bounds higher than anything the Vikings have had so far this season.

By most metrics, Cousins' performance this season has been pretty close to his season average across five and a half seasons with the Vikings previously, so from an age standpoint there doesn't seem to be an impediment there to his achieving similar results with the Vikings this season compared to what he did with the Raiders and compared to what he did previously in Minnesota. The schemes in Las Vegas and Minnesota are similar- both from the Shanahan tree- and Cousins' spent a year and a half in O'Connell's system already and three and a half seasons with Justin Jefferson, a half season with Jordan Addison and a year and a half with T.J. Hockenson. It's really a question of how fast everything comes back to him. It wouldn't be surprising if there is a ramp up period, but from all accounts he's hit the ground running since he arrived on Tuesday. My guess is there will be hiccups on Sunday as he gets back into the swing of the Vikings offense- and maybe here and there for a few games- but I also expect him to make more throws than we've been seeing from Murray and Wentz this season. Whether that's enough to beat the Packers and 49ers remains to be seen, and once again the Vikings' defense will need to show up and be dominant, but Cousins should be able to allow the Vikings offense to possess the ball and put them in position to score more points than they have so far this season- even with a more limited playbook and kind of re-learning on the field as far as rapport with receivers.

How quickly Cousins can get up to speed with the Vikings offense could mean the difference between a division championship and potential first seed in the playoffs and a wild card spot, assuming the Vikings beat the weaker teams remaining on their schedule. But the next two games are crucial- the Packers and 49ers- in terms of keeping pace within the division and competing for the top seed in the postseason. If the Vikings can win them both, they move to 8-2 and have a much easier schedule the rest of the season and would hold a key tie-breaker against the 49ers. If they lose both games, they fall to 6-4 and if they can regroup from there to gain a wild card spot they will play on the road throughout the playoffs in all likelihood. That's a tough road to make a deep run. But if the Vikings and Cousins ramp up to play their best football in December- and beyond- they will be as formidable a team as there is in the league and not a team any division winner is looking forward to playing.

But a premium is on winning in November- beginning on Sunday- to best position the team for a deep run. Hopefully Cousins- and the rest of the team- is up to the task.

#2926. Giants’ daunting schedule will reveal if they’re legitimate playoff contenders

The Giants didn't lose their grip on first place in the NFC East by falling 24-21 to the Commanders, but they did squander a chance to add breathing room against the second-worst opponent on the schedule between this week and New Year's Day.

Considering the Giants have built their 5-3-1 record by beating teams with a combined .317 winning percentage, the jump over the next six games to a .607 level of competition sounds daunting.

"We don't live in that scared world," head coach John Harbaugh said. "I said it was an important game before the game for that very reason, but now we don't sit back and go, 'We have to beat a heavyweight.'"

New York Giants head coach John Harbaugh speaks during a press conference after an NFL game in East Rutherford, N.J., Thursday, Nov. 12, 2026. AP Photo/Seth Wenig

And the first game of 2027 might be the most important of all if the Cowboys can get enough defensive stops and wins that Week 17 in Dallas is what decides the NFC East.

"We want all the smoke with all those teams," edge Abdul Carter said. "We're not ducking no smoke. Everybody's going to get our best. There's no team that we're looking at like, 'Oh, my goodness.' No. We're going to play every team the same way."

How do the Giants avoid seeing their season go up in flames when their probability to reach the playoffs dropped from a potential 78 percent by beating the Commanders down to 50 percent with the loss?

"I think we'll respond fine," left tackle Andrew Thomas said, "but if we want to go where we want to, we have to win games like this."

Here's a look at the gauntlet ahead that will either prove right or wrong the critics who believe the Giants are phonies propped up by a bad division and soft schedule:

Jaguars (6-2): Ranked No. 4 in scoring offense and No. 2 in scoring defense and No. 3 in both most takeaways and fewest giveaways, the Jaguars started the week with three all-in trades to fuel Super Bowl aspirations.

"Every one of these games is going to be hard to win," Harbaugh said. "Starting with the Jacksonville game, that will be our focus. We are going to expect to win the game."

Jacksonville Jaguars quarterback Trevor Lawrence (16) looks on during the first quarter of an NFL football matchup at EverBank Stadium on Sunday, Nov. 1, 2026, in Jacksonville, Fla. The Jaguars defeated the Colts 42-7. USA TODAY Network via Reuters Connect

At Colts (2-7): A must-win given that the Colts are tanking after a trade-deadline fire sale.

The complications are that it will be Daniel Jones' first game against the team that unceremoniously released him and Jonathan Taylor's memory will be fresh that the Giants didn't want to trade a first-round pick to acquire him.

The Giants need to be able to score at will against a defense ranked No. 30 in points allowed and rushing yards allowed.

San Francisco 49ers quarterback Brock Purdy (13) looks to pass against the Las Vegas Raiders on Sunday, Nov. 8, 2026, in Santa Clara, Calif. Getty Images

49ers (7-1): Kyle Shanahan hates when the injury-cursed 49ers play the MetLife Stadium turf monster. But he defies the myth that west-to-east travel is a killer, as the 49ers are 14-7 in Eastern Standard Time since 2021 (0-1 this season).

Brock Purdy is building a quiet MVP case.

At Seahawks (5-4): Despite owning the third-worst record in the NFL since 2020, the Giants are 2-1 in Seattle, which is regarded as one of the toughest places to play.

It's only tougher when defending Super Bowl champions need every win just to stay in the wild card hunt and the defense (No. 1 in yards allowed and passing yards allowed) is the NFL's stingiest.

The season has been disrupted by quarterback Sam Darnold's multiple injuries. Will Drew Lock still be filling in by then?

Deshaun Watson #4 of the Cleveland Browns gets shaken up during a game against the Pittsburgh Steelers in Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania, Sunday, Nov. 1, 2026. Getty Images

Browns (5-4): If not for the Giants, Todd Monken's Browns would be the biggest surprise in the NFL.

Who has the edge in Harbaugh against his protégé Monken, who nearly followed him to the Giants as offensive coordinator?

The Browns rank No. 29 in points scored but still are sticking with Deshaun Watson at quarterback … for now. Attention, Carter: No one has taken more sacks than Watson.

At Lions (6-2): Jamhyr Gibbs shredded the Giants for 264 yards and three touchdowns on turf last season. This year's defense is faster and more aggressive.

The Giants might have to un-handcuff J.J. McCarthy to keep pace with the Lions and take advantage of their last-ranked passing defense (18 touchdowns allowed). A 27-sack pass rush is a nightmare for the Giants offensive line.

At Cowboys (4-5): If the Cowboys win and cancel out the Giants' head-to-head tiebreaker, a tie between them will go to the better division record (made easy by the Giants' tie).

The Giants ended a 13-game losing streak in Philadelphia. The next hovering curse is a nine-game skid in Dallas.

The Cowboys can't stop offenses through the air. Coordinator Christian Parker seems to be hanging by a thread.

Eagles (2-7): How they'd love to play spoiler, unless it hurts general manager Howie Roseman's grand draft plans.

The Eagles won't come riding in on a 14-game losing streak then, right? It could depend on how many more players shut it down.

#2920. Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market's riskiest debt. It isn't time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.

High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.

The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.

Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.

The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody's. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.

Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.

It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.

Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.

"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.

He's in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.

That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors' psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.

"There's a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn't overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."

While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.

In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.

"We've had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.

The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it's no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.

The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it's too early to say that it's spreading to the broad credit market."

In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.

The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.

"It is isolated," she said. "I'm not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn't tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."

For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.

"We're looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we're leaning into that," she said.

Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.

But there's scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn't been in a straight line.

"We're going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that's what we'll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."

It's also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.

"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn't expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.

High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.

"That's when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."

#2918. Steelers could be forced to bus to Cincinnati after FAA grounds flights

The Pittsburgh Steelers are set to take on the Cincinnati Bengals on Sunday Night Football for sole possession of first place in the AFC North. However, travel plans could have to change drastically for the Steelers over the course of the next 24 hours.

Just a few hours ago, the FAA grounded all flights nationwide after what they are calling a communications problem. At the time of this writing, Homeland Security does not believe that this was a nefarious attack against the U.S., but rather just an outage. This is the first nationwide ground stoppage since January 2023.

Steelers senior director of communications Burt Lauten told WPXI's Shelby Cassesse that the team is "monitoring the situation" as air travel comes to a halt.

It's difficult to say how long this ground stoppage could last, and it could effect sporting events all across the nation throughout the weekend. Luckily for the Steelers, the drive to Cincinnati would be something they could quickly pivot to.

Since the team was not scheduled to fly out until Saturday, they are in a better situation than most right now. While a flight is quicker and more convenient for the team, a bus ride shouldn't have a major effect on their preparation plans, as it may for other teams.

#2913. Why the Suns should avoid trading Jalen Green for Dejounte Murray

Would I or wouldn't I? That is the question. Ever since reports surfaced earlier today linking the Phoenix Suns to disgruntled New Orleans Pelicans guard Dejounte Murray, I've had plenty of friends reaching out to ask whether I'd pull the trigger on a potential deal involving Jalen Green. And I understand why they're asking. If there's anybody who's been more vocal about the belief that Jalen Green doesn't belong in the Suns' starting five, I'd certainly be a contender for that title.

So a straight-up swap of Green for Murray makes sense, right? Not so fast.

I understand the appeal. Bringing in Murray would theoretically provide Phoenix with a more traditional point guard, allowing the team to reestablish some semblance of positional clarity and better define the responsibilities of the players surrounding Devin Booker. And who knows? Maybe it would work. A straight-up deal would also save the Suns some money, which is certainly beneficial from a salary cap perspective, although it doesn't exactly put any additional cash in my pocket.

Murray is somebody I've been thinking about since last summer. We've discussed potential scenarios involving him for quite some time, and no matter how many times I revisit the possibility, I keep arriving at the same conclusion: I'll pass on that, thank you.

My hesitation begins with Murray's health. He suffered an Achilles injury in February 2025, and there's still uncertainty surrounding whether he can return to the player he was before that injury. That's a significant concern when you're discussing acquiring a guard whose effectiveness has historically relied on his mobility, quickness, and ability to operate on both ends of the floor.

And even before the injury, was Murray really a game-altering player? He was a serviceable guard with a defensive reputation, someone capable of contributing in multiple areas without necessarily elevating the overall ceiling of the teams he played for. The last time he was impactful was when he was in a Greg Popovich system that elevates guard play, and even then the team wasn't winning much with him.

Maybe a player with his skill set would fit more naturally alongside Booker than Green currently does. Considering Green's $36.3 million salary and declining perceived value, there's certainly an argument that swapping one distressed asset for another could make sense from a basketball operations standpoint.

But then there's everything else. Murray's recent public frustration with the situation in New Orleans, including allegations that he has used burner accounts to comment on videos, gives me a familiar and uncomfortable feeling. I call it PTKD. Post-Traumatic KD Disorder.

Say what you want about Jalen Green; we don't see him coming unhinged online when people challenge his fit.

One of the things I've genuinely appreciated about this current iteration of the Phoenix Suns is how relatively quiet they've been away from the basketball court. Outside of a few entertaining trolling commercials from Dillon Brooks, this group has largely avoided the public drama that surrounded previous versions of the franchise. They're not regularly lashing out at fans. They're not creating unnecessary distractions through social media. They're not turning every criticism into a public confrontation. They appear to handle their business internally, and I have tremendous respect for that.

Perhaps that appreciation comes from personal experience. I've been on the receiving end of Kevin Durant's verbal lashings because I dared to express an opinion about the basketball team I cover.  My apologies. I have opinions, I have a platform to express them, and that's precisely what I intend to do. As Don Draper might remind these players, that's what the money is for.

Now, perhaps a change of scenery would do wonders for Murray. Maybe getting out of New Orleans, joining a different organization, and playing alongside Booker would provide the reset he needs, both professionally and personally. That's entirely possible.

When evaluating a potential acquisition, I keep returning to the same fundamental question I've been asking about Jalen Green: Does he make your basketball team better? And with Murray, I'm not convinced the answer is yes.

When you examine his time in Atlanta and New Orleans, his on/off impact has generally been underwhelming. He's spent much of that period posting negative net rating results, raising questions about how much his individual production actually translates into improved team performance.

That's what makes this entire conversation so fascinating. In theory, the Suns would be acquiring a player with a negative statistical impact to replace another player with a similarly negative impact. You're essentially exchanging one set of problems for another, hoping that the new problems are somehow easier to manage. I'll take the guy with a better attitude every day out of the week.

There are potential advantages, of course. Murray would be cheaper, providing some financial flexibility. His presence could help clarify the responsibilities within the starting lineup, allowing Booker to operate more naturally in his preferred role while Murray handles additional facilitating duties. Even that doesn't solve every issue with the roster. The overlapping skill sets of Miles Bridges and Dillon Brooks would still exist. The Suns would still have problems with size, rebounding, and interior production. There would be no guarantee that Murray's arrival would suddenly create the offensive connectivity Phoenix has been searching for.

So where do I ultimately land? I think the Suns should wait.

If Murray is disgruntled today, there's a pretty good chance he'll still be disgruntled two months from now. His desire for a change of scenery doesn't necessarily create an obligation for Phoenix to rush into a transaction, especially when the player they'd be acquiring comes with legitimate questions of his own. Meanwhile, the Suns still have an opportunity to evaluate Jalen Green and determine whether there's a way to make his skill set work within the current roster.

Quite honestly, I believe the best immediate solution is moving Green to the bench. Allow him to operate with the second unit, where he can play a style more familiar to him without constantly navigating the responsibilities and tendencies of Devin Booker. Give him an opportunity to rebuild his confidence, establish some offensive rhythm, and continue developing the distribution skills the organization clearly wants him to improve.

The Suns' bench has already demonstrated a greater understanding of its collective identity. The players know their responsibilities, understand how to complement one another, and have shown an ability to sustain productive stretches without relying on one individual to orchestrate everything. Perhaps placing Green in that environment would help him develop comfort and confidence, ultimately making him a more effective contributor when his minutes overlap with the starters'.

If it doesn't work? Well, at least Phoenix will have gathered additional information before making another significant roster decision. No need to make a trade just to make a trade. Isn't that what got us into this mess?

The reality is that there's no obvious correct answer here. Green hasn't demonstrated that he's the right fit. Murray hasn't demonstrated that he'd necessarily be a better one. Both players carry significant salaries, legitimate concerns, and enough uncertainty to make any potential transaction a gamble. And if the Pelicans and Suns ultimately decide to swap them, what exactly are we accomplishing?

"Here's your problem. We'll take it off your hands if you take ours." "Thank you very much. Have a nice day." I like our problem better. Green is younger and more athletic, seems to be a better locker room guy, is less injury-prone, and seems to be simply having a hard time navigating his new role.

The Suns have a roster that needs work, and Jalen Green hasn't made the transition Phoenix hoped he would. But that doesn't mean Dejounte Murray is the answer. There's a difference between identifying a problem and finding a solution. Swapping two players with question marks attached to their names might make the transaction look productive, but it doesn't necessarily make the basketball team any better. How about we focus on getting some rebounding, eh? Suns are 9th in assists. They're dead last in rebounding. Let's focus on fixing what's broken.

Give Green a chance to find his footing in a different role. Give Murray and the Pelicans some time to sort through their situation. The Suns don't have to be the organization that solves everybody else's problems, especially when they have enough of their own.

#2907. Lakers' Austin Reaves day-to-day with bruised heel, doesn't undergo imaging

Any Los Angeles Lakers game in which Luka Dončić and Austin Reaves both exit with injuries is going to be concerning, but it appears the team has dodged any serious fallout.

Two days after the Lakers' two leading scorers left a win over the Milwaukee Bucks, head coach JJ Redick told reporters Friday that he expects Dončić to be available for Sunday's game against the Phoenix Suns. Dončić had already told reporters he was "good" the night of the injury, which was a tweaked ankle.

As for Reaves, Redick said the guard was day-to-day with a bruise on his heel. The matter is apparently minor enough that the team didn't even bother with imaging on the foot.

"AR, based on his physical evaluation and how he was feeling yesterday, we opted against imaging. He'll be day-to-day. We'll have him on the report tomorrow at 5 o'clock."

Even with their early exits, no pair of teammates is averaging more combined points per game than Dončić and Reaves. Dončić leads the league in scoring with 38.6 points per game, while Reaves is 15th with 24.7. Dončić is also ninth in the league in assists per game at 7.6.

Rotation guard Collin Sexton also exited Wednesday's game after a collision with Bucks guard Ryan Rollins. He needed stitches above his right eye, but Redick said a concussion was ruled out and he's "probably in the same boat" as Reaves.

The Lakers-Suns game is scheduled for 4 p.m. ET on Sunday in Phoenix.

#2901. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Uncertainty in the Middle East, elevated Treasury yields and fears about an AI bubble continue to keep global markets on edge. Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking stable income can consider enhancing their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

However, the vast universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Tracking the ratings of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as their recommendations are backed by in-depth research.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Chord Energy is an independent exploration and production company with long-lived assets primarily in the Williston Basin. With a quarterly base dividend of $1.30 per share, CHRD offers a dividend yield of 3.67%.

In a research note on third-quarter expectations, RBC Capital analyst Scott Hanold reiterated a buy rating on CHRD stock with a price target of $175. "We model production near the top of guidance and continue to see strong performance from new wells and base production," said Hanold.

Specifically, the five-star analyst expects Q3 2026 production at 283 thousand barrels of oil equivalent per day, or Mboe/d, in line with the Street's consensus and near the high end of the 278 to 284 Mboe/d guidance. Hanold said his Q3 2026 earnings per share estimate increased to $5.29 due to lower non-cash depreciation, depletion and amortization.

Meanwhile, cash flow per share estimate moved lower to $13.59 to reflect Hanold's update based on the current backdrop and commodity mark-to-market. He expects Chord Energy to report capital spending of $375 million for Q3 2026, slightly above the Street's estimate of $369 million.

Overall, Hanold remains bullish on CHRD, given its best-in-class balance sheet. He expects a free cash flow payout of 80% in the third quarter, with the possibility of an upside. Hanold expects investors to focus on management commentary on 2027 capital plans, Williston M&A opportunities, and long-term performance of 4-mile wells.

Hanold ranks No. 310 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Chord Energy KPIs on TipRanks.

Williams, an energy infrastructure company that delivers natural gas, is the next dividend pick. The company recently completed the $5.5 billion acquisition of Momentum Midstream, which will expand its natural gas infrastructure in the Haynesville to meet growing Gulf Coast LNG, power and industrial demand. At a quarterly dividend of $0.5250 per share, Williams offers a yield of 2.89%.

In her latest research note, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $87. Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, the analyst said that she expects WMB to announce strong quarterly performance.

Scotto raised her estimates to reflect insights from the quarterly catch-up call, the completion of the Momentum Midstream acquisition, and mark-to-market quarter-to-date commodity prices. She expects WMB to report Q3 2026 adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $2,036 million, which is modestly above the Street's consensus estimate.

Furthermore, Scotto expects Williams' Transmission, Power & Gulf segment to increase quarter-over-quarter, driven by the startup of the Socrates power project and favorable natural gas liquids, or NGL, margins.

Scotto noted that WMB stock has underperformed due to backlash related to data centers and investor anticipation of another Power Innovation project. She expects the company to announce at least one more project before the end of this year, potentially with a new customer and a contract duration of 15 to 20 years, which would be a positive catalyst for the stock.

Scotto ranks No. 175 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.2%. See Williams Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

EOG Resources, a crude oil and natural gas exploration and production company, will hold its third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 6. At a quarterly dividend of $1.02 per share, EOG offers a dividend yield of 2.75%.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on EOG Resources stock and increased his price target to $185 from $175. The analyst expects the company's Q3 cash flow per share to exceed the Street's consensus by 13%, driven by better-than-expected production.

Byrne expects crude oil production, excluding the UAE, to reach 551 Mboe/d, which is at the high end of guidance (546-551 Mboe/d) and above the Street's projection of 548 Mboe/d, driven by continued growth in the Utica.

Furthermore, Byrne expects cash operating expenses to come in at $10.27 per barrel of oil equivalent, below the midpoint of guidance of $10.05 to $11.35, helped by lower lease operating expenses. Overall, the analyst expects EOG to deliver free cash flow of $2.65 billion, supporting another quarter of buybacks near the minimum 70% shareholder return threshold and implying about $1.5 billion in share repurchases.

"As a first indication of the '27 plan, we expect EOG will message another year of mid-single-digit (~3.5%) growth on a flat capital budget (~$6.55bn), which is above the Street modeling 1% oil growth at $6.6bn," said Byrne.

Byrne ranks No. 303 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 18.5%. See EOG Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation and the Federal Reserve Board today published feedback letters for resolution plans submitted in October 2025. The agencies did not identify any shortcomings or deficiencies in these resolution plan submissions.

Resolution plans, also known as living wills, describe a banking organization's strategy for orderly resolution in the event of material financial distress or failure. #2894. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

#2893. Game Preview #13: Wolves vs Bulls

Not only has this season been an expected mess on the court, but fans now lose any reason to tune in without their rookie for the next one to two months. Now they get the red-hot Timberwolves? A rough season just got rufffff.

Who: Minnesota Timberwolves (8-3) vs. Chicago Bulls (1-9)

When: Saturday, November 14 at 7:00 PM CT

OFFRTG: 117.2 (7th)DEFRTG: 103.8 (1st)NETRTG: +13.4 (1st)eFG%: 56.1 (3rd)FTr: .279 (17th)TOV%: 15.7 (16th)OREB%: 35.9 (3rd)PACE: 101.08 (7th)FBPS: 16.1 (11th)2nd PTS: 18.3 (3rd)PITP: 52.5 (4th)

OFFRTG: 109.2 (25th)DEFRTG: 124.2 (30th)NETRTG: -15.1 (30th)eFG%: 50.3 (28th)FTr: .346 (5th)TOV%: 15.8 (17th)OREB%: 31.3 (16th)PACE: 103.75 (1st)FBPS: 15.6 (16th)2nd PTS: 15.6 (16th)PITP: 49.4 (8th)

Is Leonard Miller primed for a Medium Player Explosion (MPE)? He's averaging just 6.3 points in 15.9 minutes per game, but recent news broke that rookie sensation Caleb Wilson would be out over a month due to a broken right hand. A terribly unfortunate break for the worst team in the NBA. It does leave a gaping hole in production and the former Wolves legend (both Minnesota and Iowa) will certainly have an opportunity to step up. Expect Chicago's injury report to be lengthy as they'll be on the end of a back-to-back.

On Minnesota's side, I am expecting a revenge game from Ayo Dosunmu. He will finally be playing in his first game against his former team. A few weeks ago, Ayo penned a touching piece on The Players' Tribune where he discussed being able to play in the comforts of his hometown. From college at University of Illinois, to the pros with the Chicago Bulls, Ayo has finally spread his wings away from home. I'm penciling in a cool 30-point performance from Dosunmu.

Let's also not rule out a breakout game for Terrence Shannon Jr. Just like Dosunmu, TJ is also a Chicago native who finished his college career as an Fighting Illini. Both should have a field day attacking the cup against the 30th ranked team in points given up in the paint.

Chicago has an interestingly high free throw rate. Why is that? Norman Powell is sixth in free threes attempted per game (8.6) and second in free throw rate (35.7). Well, the former Bruin has missed a handful of the Bulls last games. Nic Claxton (two games played) and Matas Buzelis both average 4.1 FTA per game which would lead the Wolves behind Anthony Edwards and Rudy Gobert.

How will Chicago score without their two leading scorers in Powell and Wilson? One thing the Wolves can do is avoid gifting them free points at the charity stripe, no matter if Powell is in or not. Josh Giddey was a decent foul merchant a season ago, but is below average scoring the ball otherwise. Buzelis is at his best attacking the rim, but is having a very disappointing third rookie season. Tre Jones has typically been a thorn in the side of his hometown team. Maybe the power of goggles will power Jalen Smith at Target Center?

The Wolves recall what happened to them when they last played the Bulls 10 months ago. Minnesota dropped their fourth straight game against a lowly Chicago team by blowing a late fourth quarter lead (Ayo scored eight points against the Wolves that game!) On Saturday, they will exact revenge and throttle a lifeless Bulls team with a 27-point first half lead, cruising to an easy victory. Ayo scores 84 points by himself.
