#2821. Why the data center slowdown concern is overblown. Two stocks to buy amid the fear

The demise of data centers has been greatly exaggerated. So says every utility CEO and energy-focused investor we spoke with at the Edison Electric Institute's annual conference in Arizona. Here's why...

There is a lot of misleading, misdirected, or straight-up incorrect information floating around about data centers and the AI buildout. I'm not going to dive into the old water-use story — you can do your own research on that — but rather focus this week on what the insiders know: Some newly elected politicians either don't have the power to stop the buildouts or, if they do, the buildout will likely just continue somewhere else.

Ohio and Georgia are important states to watch with their new incoming political class, but for the sheer importance in size and scope, I'll focus on the energy and data center capital: Texas.

Incoming Governor Gina Hinojosa and incoming Senator James Talarico both campaigned on promises to go after what they believe to be affordability issues around data centers. I say "what they believe to be" because it's unclear how much data centers may raise your electricity bill, if at all.

First and foremost, data centers are largely a state issue, meaning officials elected to federal office have little control over outcomes, at least for now. In Texas, any cancellations or moratoriums on new builds will come from the statehouse in Austin. That's where current Governor Greg Abbott has already, and rather quietly, put his own "pause" on data center permitting. That delay has been underway for a few weeks. Also, in Texas, the Governor has less direct power than in some other states. The power the governor does have largely derives from the authority to appoint those in charge of the all-important permitting process.

In the Lone Star State, that power lies with two agencies: the Public Utility Commission of Texas (PUCT) and the even lesser-known Texas Commission on Environmental Quality (TCEQ). The PUCT regulates Texas' electricity, telecom, and water industries. It helps oversee growth of anything electricity-grid-related. So if a data center wants to connect to the public utility grid, it needs the commission's approval. Its chair — a pro-growth chap named Thomas Gleeson —is also on the board of Texas' electric grid powerhouse ERCOT and will serve his term with the utility commission through September 2029. Yes, three years from now.

That agency gets involved when a data center or power provider wants to connect to the electricity grid. But what about facilities that are off the grid and have their own dedicated power sources? Texas has less control over what happens then. That said, it does have to authorize certain environmental permits, and that responsibility largely lies with the TCEQ. The TCEQ was "BBI" (my own little term for "boring, but important") until data centers came along. It's not exactly the Alamo, but this obscure group — run by a regulator named Brooke Paup — is largely the "last stand" for permitting on those new builds. Paup was also appointed by Gov. Abbott and serves through next year. In other words, unless she and Gleeson step down or there is some way to oust them, the regulatory environment in Texas may not change much.

The bottom line in this brief foray into the Texas regulatory swamp is to highlight that there are things for investors to consider around the data center/energy world that may not be at the top of the headlines.

Important Note → Be mindful that I am an East Coast-based financial broadcaster and journalist, not an expert on Texas regulation or how the state operates. There may very well be things I don't know, things my sources aren't aware of, or other ways in which the new Governor and state House can assert power. For one, she recently said she may try to freeze electricity rates. Hint: that would be a terrible idea unless you want to damage the growth of badly needed new power sources like wind and solar.

Disclaimer is done. Let's assume I am wrong on Texas and there is a larger push to slow or stop data center growth in the state. Conversations with some pretty smart people lead me to believe that if those facilities aren't built in that state, they will be built in another: Louisiana, Oklahoma, or another state. Sources tell me that Louisiana Governor Jeff Landry and his team are already working the phones, telling companies that his state would love to welcome new business. Same for Oklahoma. Both are gas-rich, have plenty of power, and are only too happy to grab any data center dollars.

Let's acknowledge that not all data centers are going to be built… and never were. There are way too many plans to make them all happen. Some DCs were shelved before the election. Some others may soon be canceled, either for reasons of public pushback or otherwise. It's also not clear if we even need all the planned compute. There will be a time when companies realize they have enough — or at least enough for now — and turn off the spending taps.

Goldman Sachs lays it out in a new note late this week:

"The midterm election results pose only modest risks to AI investment, for now. We expect newly elected Democratic governors in states that account for over a third of planned US data center capacity to impose moratoria on new data centers. However, these will likely only impact projects not yet permitted, leaving the vast majority of planned 2027 construction unaffected. We therefore expect data center construction spending to remain strong next year, though moratoria that are stricter or longer-lasting than we expect pose downside risks."

The firm adds that it still sees spending on AI-related hardware coming in around $1.4 trillion by the end of next year. In other words, the spending boom is going to keep going, at least for now.

Another key point for all you energy- or utility-focused investors out there: While data centers are one huge driver of electricity demand, they aren't the only one. Electric cars, reshoring manufacturing, and the "electrification of everything" will also grow the need for more gigawatts. This is why we asked every utility CEO we spoke with at the EEI conference if they saw any slowdown in their internal electricity growth estimates. All gave the same answer: no. Not one hinted at a downturn. Not Exelon's Calvin Butler. Not Entergy's Drew Marsh. And not Drew Maloney, head of EEI. Of course, power demand is about more than just data centers, but it's still important for investors to hear it from the top.

Side note: Many of you think I'm wrong about my "hot take" on data centers and the election. If you missed last week's Power Insider, this is how you responded to my X poll on the election and energy.

So… why am I writing this week about AI and data centers? Because, as I've said before and will keep saying, AI and data centers are just energy stories in disguise. If they grow, energy demand grows. Demand for solar, wind, and natural gas grows. The economy grows. Because, after all, energy is the economy.

Oh, and in the past week, I've flown from Newark to Rome to Chicago to Phoenix and back to Newark. My brain may be a little mushy, so bear with me.

So now that my big take on data centers and regulation is over, let's get granular. What energy stocks do you buy right now?

Let's get the "1-2-3" on six stocks: one in solar, two in power, and three in oil and gas.

One: William Blair is upgrading First Solar (FSLR) to Outperform. The election result is part of the story. Analyst Jed Dorsheimer and his team believe the new Congress and certain state governorships will benefit local support for utility-scale solar, and that First Solar is best-positioned, though he does caution investors that it may take "some time to show up in the bookings." He adds that Texas is the largest builder of solar, so the shift in state-level leadership is a big deal. Fair warning, however: upgrades do not mean the stock will go higher. Deutsche Bank upgraded FSLR back in early July — an upgrade I wrote about — and the stock has fallen 22% since.

Two: Staying with the macro power theme, Barclays' Nicholas Campanella is particularly optimistic on two utility names. He tells Power Insider that he "really likes NiSource (NI) and Xcel Energy (XEL) right now." It has to do in part with what I just wrote about above. Campanella says the political outcomes in states such as Texas and Georgia may drive investors into utilities in other areas, particularly those with "more stable and established data center policies in place." That would fit with Indiana-based NiSource and Minnesota-based Xcel. While Minnesota may have a Democratic tilt, the company operates in eight different states. It is also working with Alphabet (GOOGL) on a massive new data center, and CEO Bob Frenzel told us that he is confident they can successfully navigate it all.

Three: Now to the "3"… three picks on what powers some of the power: oil and natural gas.

In an interview on CNBC, Bernstein analyst Bob Brackett says he likes ExxonMobil (XOM), calling it a great "barbell" play between natural gas and refined products. Brackett — a former Exxon employee — also believes it's a relatively "safe" stock to own right now in the volatile and headline-driven world of energy. Brackett has an Outperform rating and $186 target on the stock, giving it about another 10% upside from here.

Brackett also likes the lesser-known Expand Energy (EXE). If you're not familiar with Expand Energy, don't worry; it's still relatively new. The company is the combination of Chesapeake Energy and Southwestern Energy. It's a natural gas behemoth, and Brackett says the gas story is a "great long-term story" that's trading below a "midcycle price," and Expand fits right into it. Brackett also notes that Expand has a new CEO. He has a $139 target on the stock, making him one of the more bullish analysts on the Street. Expand has a median price target of $123.54.

Meanwhile, the chart looks tasty on another energy name: Cheniere Energy (LNG). Piper Sandler technical guru Craig Johnson likes the stock, and while he isn't a fundamental energy analyst, he was one of the few to upgrade the entire sector back in February when the Iran war began. Cheniere — along with Venture Global (VG) — is a pure play on why I was just in Europe: the worsening European energy crisis. Cheniere exports massive quantities of U.S. natural gas to customers in Europe, Asia, and other parts of the world. Johnson likes Cheniere in part because it recently broke out of a "very big base," and he wants to buy stocks that are on the upswing.

Cheniere is a well-run, rather quiet company with a fantastic CEO in longtime energy executive Jack Fusco. Jack has forgotten more about running an energy business than most will know, and the company continues to expand production. As I've pointed out for over four years, without American natural gas, Europe would be in dire straits and cutting demand even more than it already is. It's not hyperbole to say that American natural gas is — literally — keeping the lights and the heat on across Germany, Italy, and other countries.

Remember this incredible stat: just one tanker of LNG can produce a year's worth of power for over 100,000 average-sized European homes. Next year, Europe is still planning to stop buying LNG from Russia. That means demand for American natural gas is only going one way: up.

This week's Inside Line is with Amrita Sen, founder and director of market intelligence at Energy Aspects. Amrita is one of the smartest and most well-connected energy experts in the world, and she took a few minutes from her constant travel to answer a couple of questions.

This week's Take A Look is a little outside the norm, but an important interview nonetheless. It's with the U.S. Ambassador to Italy, business magnate, and former star of CNBC's Billion Dollar Buyer, Tilman Fertitta. In a wide-ranging interview from the ambassador's residence in Rome, we talked about that day's stock market crash, business, the consumer, and, of course, energy. Full disclosure: Tilman and I have been friends for over 15 years.

This Washington Post article was published a few days after the election. The underlying research from Harvard University and the University of Pennsylvania that it was based on is here and is worth a read. The study came out on October 9 (and yes, I missed it as well).

Not blaming data centers for higher energy costs is definitely random, but interesting!

#2820. Chicago Fed's Goolsbee says central bank 'has definitely got work to do' on inflation

Chicago Federal Reserve President Austan Goolsbee expressed concern about the latest inflation data, telling CNBC on Friday that the central bank likely will need to take action.

"I believe that the the central bank has definitely got work to do to continue to reestablish its credibility," the policymaker said during an interview on "Squawk Box."

His remarks came shortly after the Bureau of Labor Statistics reported that the producer price index, considered a proxy for wholesale prices, posted a 0.3% increase in October, which was slightly less than expected, but also indicated a 6.5% annual gain, the highest since November 2022.

With data in hand earlier in the week showing that the consumer price index rose a bit more than expected during the month, Goolsbee said the Fed is slipping further away from its 2% inflation target.

"We've been above 2% for almost six years, and while it sometimes we were making progress, lately we're not making any progress, and if anything, it's going the wrong way," he said.

"Inflation is rising. It's proved far more persistent than we predicted at the beginning from the supply shocks, and now we're adding this old-fashioned demand overheating coming from the AI data center buildout, that looks like it's starting to spread out of its lane," Goolsbee added.

Markets are fully pricing in a quarter percentage point increase when the rate-setting Federal Open Market Committee meets next on Dec. 8-9. There also is a better than 10% probability of a half-point hike, according to the CME Group's FedWatch gauge of futures markets. Goolsbee is not currently an FOMC voter but will be in 2027.

Earlier in the week, Fed Governor Christopher Waller indicated he would entertain a 50 basis point move depending on this week's inflation data.

Goolsbee did not speculate directly on whether the Fed might need to be more aggressive.

"It's it's all going to be about this ... question: What is happening to inflation, and are we on path back to 2%?" he said. "My feeling is, right now we're not."

Raphinha endured a frustrating evening in Singapore as Brazil lost 3-2 to Japan on November 14, with the Barcelona captain receiving a straight red card alongside head coach Carlo Ancelotti following protests over Japan's stoppage-time winner.

The result leaves Raphinha with an unwanted disciplinary incident to accompany an already difficult international break.

INDIANAPOLIS — About a year prior, Jalen Brunson nailed a stepback, game-winning 3-pointer in the final seconds against the Pacers.

On Friday night inside the same Gainbridge Fieldhouse, a nearly identical situation presented itself, but coach Mike Brown instead called on Karl-Anthony Towns. Captain Clutch was a decoy on the other side of the court. The result was a fadeaway miss off the rim from KAT, a 106-104 Knicks defeat and probable end to New York's NBA Cup defense.

"I thought it was a great shot. I was excited for the opportunity," Towns told The Post. "I got to make a quick decision. It's either OG is open on the rip screen and he has an open layup or I get to take the shot. I just was ready for the opportunity. … I'm happy with the follow through. I was happy with the technique. It just wasn't my day."

Karl-Anthony Towns (32) of the New York Knicks handles the ball against the Indiana Pacers in Indianapolis on Nov. 13, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

The Knicks launched an improbable comeback to reach that dramatic conclusion, recovering from an eight-point deficit in the final 90 seconds because Indiana kept coughing up possessions.

With 2.8 seconds remaining, the Pacers threw away an inbounds pass — largely because of Mikal Bridges' hounding defense — which set up KAT's opportunity.

Jalen Brunson of the New York Knicks plays defense during a game against the Indiana Pacers in Indianapolis on Nov. 13, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

Technically, the Knicks aren't eliminated from the NBA Cup with an 0-2 record. It's still theoretically possible to win the group or a wild-card spot with a 2-2 record. But in the NBA Cup's three-year history, no team has advanced with anything less than a 3-1 mark in the group phase.

The Knicks are the lone squad in their five-team group without a win.

New York Knicks center Karl-Anthony Towns (32) reacts to a call against the Indiana Pacers in Indianapolis on Friday, Nov. 13, 2026. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

There's still much more time to defend their bigger championship, but the Knicks (6-6) hardly have looked like the juggernaut of their magical run of May and June.

They missed 30 3-pointers and gave up 17 offensive rebounds in Indiana, the place of several Knicks heartbreaks over the past few years.

New York Knicks guard Jalen Brunson (11) shoots as Indiana Pacers guard Andrew Nembhard defends in Indianapolis on Friday, Nov. 13, 2026. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

Towns finished with 23 points and 15 rebounds, breaking a 56-year franchise record with his 12th double-double to start a season. He eclipsed Willis Reed's mark from 1970 and then came up just a little short with an opportunity to extend New York's NBA Cup hopes.

"That play was something we liked, and we went to it. But I'll say this: That play did not cost us the game," Brown said. "We were lucky a little bit, and our guys did fight to give us a chance down the stretch. But I don't care who you play, it's going to be hard to win a game on the road when you lose the possession game like we did, if we get blown by on defense as bad as we did, if you let your opponent win the physicality battle like we did. It's all a recipe for a loss. And that's where I'm disappointed."

#2817. Hackers stole $6.3 billion from Binance—2 habits that take 'a few seconds' can help you avoid similar traps

The biggest heist in cryptocurrency history began with small mistakes many internet users make. A hacking group tied to North Korea recently stole $6.3 billion from the crypto exchange Binance. The operation was sophisticated, involving fake job interviews and malicious code.

But it was also mundane. Although artificial intelligence played a role in the hack, this was not a cyberattack of the future, propelled by swarms of AI agents. Rather, it relied on humans who made some basic errors, like clicking a link because it appeared first on a search results page or trusting strangers at a fake company without taking a few seconds to look them up.

We work at the Digital Inquiry Group, a nonprofit that researches how people evaluate online information and creates free, evidence-based curriculum that helps people decide what to trust.

In over a decade of research, we've found that people frequently engage in online behavior that makes them more likely to get duped. Two simple strategies can help you better navigate today's information landscape, from traditional phishing to misleading social posts to AI fakes. Here's how.

The Binance heist began when one of the company's engineers downloaded compromised software. That engineer, according to the Reuters investigation of the hack, clicked on a sponsored search result that appeared higher on the results page and directed them to the malicious code.

Our research at the Digital Inquiry Group suggests internet users frequently behave as if first is best. They click on the first thing they see. They incorrectly associate a higher placement with vetted content, ceding judgment to tech company algorithms. Students have gone so far as to tell us "Google is almost never wrong" or "AI is always right."

Professional fact-checkers commonly practice click restraint. They understand that whether you're searching on the internet or querying a chatbot, first isn't always best. If you're researching an organization, the first link will often be to its own site, which is hardly likely to disclose anything that's wrong with it.

So the next time you search for something online, be wary of links to sponsored content, the organization you're investigating, or to open platforms where anyone can post and information isn't verified, such as Quora or Reddit. Read the brief snippets beneath each link, and make a more informed choice about what to open.

Always ask yourself: Who is behind this information? What makes them credible? Look for people and organizations that have direct expertise on the topic at hand, or have processes to ensure the accuracy of information, such as news outlets that employ editors and fact-checkers.

Binance says it caught that first malicious download, replaced the device and changed passwords. But later, another engineer downloaded compromised code as part of what they thought was a job interview, which included a virtual meeting with the supposed CEO of a fake company.

It's tempting to think that the engineer should have seen something was off. But the internet is a precarious place because bad actors know how to make content feel legitimate. Binance says its employee was snared by a hacker posing as an HR rep who "shared a social media account with him, including personal photos and videos posted on the platform, in order to build trust."

When they encounter a piece of unfamiliar content, professional fact-checkers don't just read it closely — they read laterally. They open new browser tabs to see what other, credible sources have to say. Fact-checkers know that bad actors make themselves look good on their home turf, but that it's a lot harder to fake a good reputation across the internet.

The next time you encounter something unfamiliar online, look for authoritative sources that can shed light on the person or organization. You might find a news article that confirms the claim you're looking into, or a report that identifies a supposedly neutral source as biased.

In some cases, you may find nothing at all, which is a signal in itself: if you look up a company that reached out to you and find no external confirmation of its existence, that should be an immediate red flag.

Had that first Binance engineer engaged in click restraint and verified the source of the software, they might have avoided the malicious download. Had that second employee taken time to seek external verification about the supposed HR rep, rather than trusting a polished social media profile, they might have realized that something didn't add up.

The internet and AI have made it harder to know whether what we see is authentic. They've also made it easier than ever before to investigate who or what we encounter online. It's always worth pausing to check. A few seconds up front can save us all a lot of time, grief and money later on.

Nadav Ziv is a researcher at the Digital Inquiry Group. Joel Breakstone is co-founder and executive director of the Digital Inquiry Group.

#2812. OpenAI launches ChatGPT Wallet, allowing its 'dots' AI agents to make purchases for users

OpenAI on Friday announced ChatGPT Wallet, a new payment feature that enables its "dots" artificial intelligence agents to make purchases on behalf of users.

The company built ChatGPT Wallet in partnership with American Express, Mastercard and Visa, and it allows cardholders to maintain their existing credit card rewards and protections. Eligible users enter their card information once, prompt their dot to find items to buy and approve each purchase with a security check.

OpenAI said its dots agents will never actually access a user's card details. Instead, their payment networks will provide the dot with a "payment token," which contains a scoped card number, security code and expiration date that is specific to a given transaction.

"Our goal here is to really focus on merchants and our network partners and start building out a really great foundation that's safe and easy to use," Chaya Nayak, who leads product for monetization and payments at OpenAI, told CNBC in an interview.

ChatGPT Wallet comes as a growing number of consumers are exploring how to use personal AI agents to manage their calendars, book travel and complete other day-to-day tasks. OpenAI introduced the dots agents during its annual developer conference in late September, shortly after Meta's Muse offering topped the App Store charts.

Purchases made through ChatGPT Wallet could eventually kick-start a new revenue stream for OpenAI, but the company said it is not taking a cut of transactions to start. With the initial launch, OpenAI is working to build relationships with merchants and position itself as a serious platform for shoppers, especially as vendors are beginning to experiment with personal agents.

OpenAI has pushed deeper into AI-enabled commerce over the last year, but some of its early attempts failed to take off. The company introduced an "Instant Checkout" feature last fall, which it quickly pivoted away from. By March, it had rolled out a revamped shopping experience that aimed to make it easier for users to find and compare products.

Nayak said ChatGPT Wallet builds on OpenAI's previous efforts, particularly around product discovery.

"We think that this is the next step, where if someone found something they're really excited to buy, then we want to be able to get them through to that endpoint," she said.

OpenAI's ChatGPT chatbot supports more than 1.2 billion weekly active users and nearly 100 million paying users as of the end of the third quarter. With such a broad user base, Gebb said OpenAI can decide to "turn up the volume" on ChatGPT Wallet when it wants to.

"It just makes sense to watch it, start it in a contained environment, and then keep rolling from there," Gebb told CNBC in an interview.

Merchants and vendors are still reckoning with the rise of personal AI agents, and some companies have opted to forgo them entirely. United Airlines said Wednesday that it will block agents from accessing its systems to book tickets, and Amazon prevents the agents from purchasing products on its platform.

OpenAI said Friday that agentic commerce is still in its "early days," and it published tools and guidance alongside its ChatGPT Wallet announcement to help businesses start to navigate the transition. The company explained how merchants can make sure their websites aren't inadvertently blocking OpenAI's agents, for instance.

"It's going to take time for the ecosystem to build the right infrastructure to be able to make this experience really amazing and seamless across all of their sites," Nayak said. "Really, who we are speaking to here is the networks and the merchants."

#2810. Supreme Court agrees to hear Robinhood challenge to IPO information omission lawsuit

The Supreme Court on Friday agreed to hear a case in which Robinhood seeks to block a class-action lawsuit over the omission of interim financial data from the online broker's initial public offering in 2021.

Robinhood wants the Supreme Court to overturn a ruling by the 9th Circuit U.S. Court of Appeals that revived a lawsuit by investors against the company and firms involved with the IPO, including Goldman Sachs and J.P. Morgan Securities.

A San Francisco federal court judge had previously dismissed the lawsuit.

The Trump administration and the Securities and Exchange Commission had urged the Supreme Court to hear Robinhood's appeal.

"We are pleased the Supreme Court has agreed to hear our case and look forward to presenting our argument," a Robinhood spokesperson said in a statement.

CNBC has requested comment from the attorneys representing the plaintiffs in the case.

Robinhood's IPO came on the heels of a surge in investor interest during the Covid-19 pandemic in so-called meme stocks, and trading in the crypto asset dogecoin.

In July 2021, Robinhood conducted its IPO, "for which it prepared a registration statement" that "contained only limited information about Robinhood's performance during the second quarter of 2021," the 9th Circuit panel that heard the case noted.

"After the initial public offering, Robinhood reported financial results from that second quarter, and its stock price dropped," the panel wrote.

The investors who sued Robinhood and the other defendants alleged the registration statement omitted material information it was legally required to disclose.

#2809. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Democrats' midterm election win has put the artificial intelligence trade in question, as voters clearly expressed opposition to the rapid buildout of data centers. The stock market could remain volatile amid AI trade concerns, macro uncertainties, and geopolitical tensions.

In this scenario, investors can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios to earn steady income. However, the extensive universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts assign ratings after in-depth financial analysis.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Viper Energy (VNOM) is a subsidiary of Diamondback and owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests, primarily in the Permian Basin. The company reported upbeat third-quarter earnings, supported by recent acquisitions and continued development activity from Diamondback and third-party operators. Viper announced a base cash dividend of 50 cents per share for Q3 2026, payable on Nov. 19. Based on the base and variable dividends announced over the past 12 months, VNOM's dividend yield stands at 5.7%.

Following the Q3 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper Energy stock with a price target of $61. The analyst noted that the company delivered solid Q3 results, with production, including oil and natural gas liquids, exceeding both TD Cowen and consensus estimates.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the key update was the announcement of two royalty acquisitions for $809 million in cash, including deferred consideration, and $82 million of newly issued shares.

Given the recently announced base dividend hike, Bilkoski expects Viper to direct incremental free cash flow toward buybacks. While the acquisitions increase leverage, the analyst believes that Viper has enough capacity for opportunistic buybacks.

Bilkoski noted that Viper has historically delivered one of the highest production per share growth profiles in his royalty coverage, but still trades at a notable discount to the royalty peer average and the company's historical FCF yield range. "In our view, its above-average growth warrants a higher valuation," said the analyst.

Bilkoski ranks No. 525 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.80%. See Viper Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Exxon Mobil (XOM) reported robust Q3 2026 profits, as its U.S. refineries boosted production to capitalize on the high diesel fuel prices sparked by the conflict in the Middle East. The oil and gas giant announced a quarterly dividend of $1.07 per share, payable on Dec. 10, reflecting a 3.9% increase. This marks Exxon's 44th consecutive year of dividend increases. At an annualized dividend of $4.28 per share, XOM offers a yield of 2.53%.

Impressed by the Q3 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Exxon stock and raised his price target to $193 from $180 to reflect higher downstream earnings, with the expectation of increased mid-cycle margins.

Gabelman noted that the strength in Exxon's refining business continues to offset Middle East-related headwinds. He added that the company increased its ownership in LNG projects, which should reduce its Middle East concentration over time from 75% to 50% and lower the likelihood of diversification-focused mergers and acquisitions. Beyond LNG, Gabelman expects Exxon's growth in the 2030s to come from Guyana, Kashagan, Permian, and possibly Venezuela.

Gabelman noted that Exxon announced a dividend hike of nearly 4% and plans to maintain buybacks at $20 billion for 2027. "The decision to maintain reduces the pro-cyclicality nature of the buyback," said the analyst.

Gabelman ranks No. 347 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.90%. See Exxon Mobil Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Williams (WMB), which owns and operates energy infrastructure to deliver natural gas, reported strong third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of $0.525 per share, or $2.10 per share annualized, payable on Dec. 28. WMB offers a dividend yield of 3.09%.

In reaction to the Q3 2026 results, CIBC analyst Robert Catellier reaffirmed a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $82. The analyst noted that Q3 results were essentially in line with expectations and reflected continued strength in execution across WMB's infrastructure portfolio.

Catellier said that Williams' Q3 performance was driven by higher transmission, storage, and power generation revenues, as well as contributions from the Momentum Midstream acquisition.

He noted that Williams maintained its full-year EBITDA guidance of $8.3 billion to $8.5 billion. In fact, results are tracking toward the higher end of the guidance range, backed by contributions from recently completed projects and the Momentum Midstream deal. WMB continues to target long-term EBITDA compound annual growth rate of at least 11% through 2030.

Catellier expects WMB's long-term targets to be supported by incremental transmission and storage opportunities, including Transco's Southeast Supply Enhancement II project and an 84 MMcf/d expansion of the Garden Connector project.

#2808. Wholesale prices rose 0.3% in October, putting annual rate at 6.5%, the highest in nearly four years

Wholesale prices increased less than expected in October, but inflation pressures persisted and the Federal Reserve is likely still on target for an interest rate increase soon.

The producer price index, which measures the prices domestic producers get for their products before they reach the consumer, rose a seasonally adjusted 0.3% for the month, against the Dow Jones consensus for a 0.4% increase, the Bureau of Labor Statistics reported Friday.

Stripping out food and energy, core PPI posted a 0.3% gain, meeting expectations.

On an annual basis, headline PPI accelerated 6.5%, up 0.2 percentage point for the highest annual rate since November 2022.

The report comes just a few weeks ahead of the Federal Reserve's next policy meeting, Dec. 8-9. Markets are fully pricing in a quarter percentage point increase in the central bank's key overnight borrowing rate, with slight odds that the Federal Open Market Committee might opt for a half-point hike.

Stock market futures added to gains following the release while Treasury yields were mixed.

Earlier this week, the BLS reported that inflation at the cash register was hotter than expected.

The headline consumer price index saw a 0.4% increase in October while core was up 0.3%, both 0.1 percentage point higher than expected. Because of the government shutdown in October 2025, the BLS couldn't collect some data and refrained from putting out a 12-month inflation figure for CPI.

However, the figures showed that the Fed remains a good distance from its 2% inflation goal.

"Today's PPI data – which shows some signs of improving, albeit still too-high – is unlikely to change the Fed's approach in December because the CPI data was already outside their comfort zone," said Chris Zaccarelli, chief investment officer for Northlight Asset Management.

Moreover, policymakers are beginning to worry that inflation generated by the Iran war and higher fuel prices, along with President Donald Trump's tariffs, are beginning to spread through the economy. The Fed generally would look through such price pressures as temporary, but the breadth of inflation could indicate that inflation is becoming more entrenched.

"We've had some signs of trouble now for several months in a row," Chicago Fed President Austan Goolsbee said in a CNBC interview Friday. "If you look at how broad-based the inflation is, it's not just that we're above the target and have been for some time. It's that in categories that are not driven by oil prices and not driven by tariffs, you're still seeing stalled progress and in some cases going the wrong way."

Stripping out food, energy and trade services, PPI saw an increase of 0.5% in October.

Goods prices increased 0.5% in October, with more than 60% of that move driven by goods excluding food and energy, which actually saw a 0.1% decrease. On an annual basis, the goods index jumped 10.2%, the biggest 12-month surge since October 2022.

Final demand services prices increased 0.2%, most of which was linked to transportation and warehousing services, and within that category a 3.9% gain in truck transportation of freight.

"We've been above 2% for almost six years, and while it sometimes we were making progress, lately we're not making any progress, and if anything, it's going the wrong way," Goolsbee said. "Inflation is rising; it's proved far more persistent than we predicted at the beginning from the supply shocks."

Other items in the PPI report also showed multiyear gains. Prices for intermediate processed goods soared 14.6% from October 2025, the biggest annual gain since July 2022. Intermediate prices for services posted a 6.9% increase, the most since November 2022.

#2806. Pacers 106, Knicks 104: “I’m sure Indiana will look like an all star team tonight.”

Daltong called it. "I'm sure Indiana will look like an all star team tonight." The Hoosiers may have entered the game 4–8 overall and 1–4 at home, but even while weathering the absence of Tyrese Haliburton, they have another gear available to them. For instance, on Veterans Day, when they ripped open the fourth quarter with 14 unanswered points. Or tonight, when they outscored the Knicks (6–6) by 13 early in the final frame and held on for a 106–104 win.

Tonight's tilt at Gainbridge Fieldhouse was another Group B game in the NBA Cup tourney. The Knicks were favored because they're the more talented team on paper, but their effort showed up too late to steal the win. They were simply out-hustled for most of the game. In the positive column, Karl-Anthony Towns registered his twelfth straight double-double (23 PTS, 15 RBS), breaking Willis Reed's franchise record for consecutive double-doubles to start a season. And Mikal Bridges took 12 shots! For 18 points! On the less positive side, Josh Hart went scoreless on four shots, the Knicks lost the paint by 20 and second-chance points by nine, and yet again they attempted double-digit fewer free throws than their opponent (IND 18-of-25, NYK 10-of-13).

Shield your eyes from all this yellow . . . looks a little like a filthy urinal.

The game started inelegantly, with Andrew Nembhard (12 PTS, 4-14 FG, 6 AST, 6 TO) taking the opening tip in for an easy layup. At the other end, Hart thought KAT was going to cut. Instead, KAT crowded Jalen Brunson (33 PTS, 11-22 FG, 11 AST, +8) along the arc, and Nembhard deflected Hart's pass out of bounds. That kind of confusion is predictable this early in the season, especially with Hart having missed a few games already. So is KAT's ability to steamroll Ivica Zubac (12 PTS, 13 RBS) for snacks at the cup.

OG Anunoby (11 PTS, 4-13 FG, +8) laced an early triple when Hart noticed that Pascal Siakam (19 PTS, 5-13 FG) was napping in transition. OG and Pascal have a history, and they made each other's jobs difficult for most of the night. (After that three, OG would make one of his next seven attempts.)

The oft-maligned Bridges chipped in six points and a steal (welcome back!), and Hart had four boards and three dimes. Brunson scored just two points but set up three baskets before picking up his second foul, an offensive foul, and heading to the bench.

Jay Huff brought Indiana back with eight quick points off the bench, including two threes and an alley-oop from that pest T.J. McConnell. Miles McBride answered with a longball, but the Knicks went more than three minutes without scoring after that. Deuce missed his next three attempts, Shamet bricked a couple, and Huff blocked Andre Drummond at the rim. Indiana went on a 15–5 run, taking a 24–23 lead on an OAKAAKUYOAK Obi Toppin bank shot. Towns returned and restored the lead with a driving and-one. The good guys were up 27–26 at the horn.

If you enjoyed the stanky shooting and one-and-dones of the first quarter, the Knicks cordially invited you to more of the same in the second. OG tied it at 31 on another Towns-as-hub laser, then Indy rattled off a 13–4 run. No Diawara tonight, but Pacôme Dadiet made a brief (as in one minute) appearance in the second.

Yet again, free throws were the biggest bugaboo. In their last game, the Knicks shot 14 fewer free throws than Cleveland. In tonight's second quarter, Indiana went 13-of-17 while New York shot . . . 1-of-2. Call the vet! These zebras have rabies!

Brunson hit a turnaround and fed Tyler Kolek for a late three to regain some ground. Indy led 58–49 at the break.

Through the first half, our heroes were dishing the ball fine, with seven more assists than the Hoosiers. Rebounds and points in the paint were basically a dead heat. The voodoo was in the fouls. The Knicks had committed twice as many as the Pacers (14–7). And somehow Rick Carlisle found it necessary to complain about the calls.

Brunson led an 8–0 run that brought New York within five, but the comeback stalled before they could get back to black. Kelly Oubre Jr. and Nesmith each scored seven in the period, Zubac added six points and five boards, and the hosts were up by 14 again.

From there, the Knicks closed on a 15–2 run to get within a point. McBride hit two bombs, Shamet added one, and Drummond grabbed three defensive boards and a steal. Deuce and Anunoby both missed chances to take the lead in the final eight seconds, so the Knicks went into the fourth trailing 85–84.

From there, two scenarios were possible. Either the Champs would maintain their momentum and turn back those pesky Pacers, or Indy would do what they did against the Wiz on Wednesday and go on some crazy run. 'Twas the latter, fans. After Anunoby tied it at 87, the hosts answered with a 15–2 run. Nembhard scored six points in the quarter, Oubre added five points and two steals, and Zubac finished with nine offensive boards. The Knicks kept fumbling and bumbling the ball away. This time Captain Clutch couldn't save them either, managing only four points in the fourth after scoring 16 in the third.

New York still had a shot to tie it in the final minute. Anunoby picked off Siakam's pass with three seconds left, but Towns missed a 15-foot turnaround and the Pacers held on. Ballgame.

#2805. Despite loss, New York Giants will enter Week 11 still atop the NFC East

The New York Giants suffered a disappointing loss to the Washington Commanders Thursday night, a loss that could haunt them for the rest of the season.

"It's disappointing, yeah," head coach John Harbaugh said after the 24-21 loss at MetLife Stadium. "I mean, I think that's the best word. It's just disappointing. We worked hard for this, and we put a hard week in. And we were prepared.

"The guys came out with, I thought, tremendous energy. We played very hard. Plays were made. Plays were missed. But it wasn't good enough to win the game. And that's the disappointment of it. So it's disappointing, but you can't let it bring you down... We'll keep fighting."

The loss dropped the Giants to 5-3-1, but they've held on to first place in the NFC East for at least another week. Washington improved to 3-5-1 and is now third in the division. The win, however, gives them a head-to-head tiebreaker over the Giants.

The NFC East is still in the Giants' hands. They lead the Commanders by two games and will at least lead Dallas by one game regardless of Sunday's outcome.

The Cowboys, 4-5, are in second place and are hosting the 7-1 San Francisco 49ers this Sunday afternoon. A win would move them to 5-5, a major upset, as prediction sites show 72 percent of the public favoring San Francisco in this game. A Dallas loss would drop them to 4-6 but keep them in second place.

The missed opportunity was not lost on Harbaugh. He maintained a brave face during his postgame presser, but he was clearly deflated.

"It would have been nice to open up a lead in the division. We didn't open it up as much as we could have if we had got the win," he said. "We've got a lot of tough games. What do we have, [eight] games left? So it's going to be [eight] dogfights. That's what it's going to be. It's the National Football League. I'd like to say we're going to win all [eight] of them. But it's probably not very likely. You're probably not going to be putting any money down on that.

"So we've got to win enough. And this was one that got away. We've got to find a way to get the next one. And the next one after that. And the next one after that. And get as many as we need to win the division. That's what we've got to find a way to do. So just try to keep it in perspective is very important."

The Giants will get a few extra days of rest before their next game next Sunday at home against the AFC South-leading Jacksonville Jaguars. Last week, the NFL flexed the game from the early television window to the late window.

#2804. Retirees sometimes tap savings for one-off expenses. Here’s what advisors say to consider first

Retirees facing a large expense that's not in their regular household budget may turn to their traditional 401(k) or individual retirement account balances to cover it.

While retirement savings may be the easiest place to turn for the money, financial advisors say there are a few things worth considering before making one-off withdrawals.

"Be aware of the fact that it's not as simple as just taking out the amount of money that [covers] what you want it for," said certified financial planner Victoria Trumbower, managing member of Trumbower Financial Advisors in Bethesda, Maryland. The firm ranked No. 19 on CNBC's Financial Advisor 100 list for 2026.

Read coverage of CNBC's 2026 Financial Advisor 100 list, including stories featuring ranked firms and their advisors:

At the same time, research shows that many retirees aren't even spending their nest egg. About a third still have 100% or more of their initial assets when they reach their mid-80s, according to a May study by the Employee Benefit Research Institute, a nonpartisan research group.

"Retirement savings are meant to be spent," said CFP Alex Canellopoulos, director of wealth and a partner with Vista Capital Partners in Portland, Oregon. "A one-time withdrawal isn't necessarily a bad decision, as long as it fits within a sustainable retirement plan."

In 2025, about 15% of 401(k) owners ages 60 to 72 with balances of at least $100,000 who had left their employer made an ad hoc withdrawal from their account, according to Vanguard's How America Retires 2026 report, which analyzed more than 125,000 ex-plan participants.

This age group falls between the onset of penalty-free withdrawals — age 59½ — and the threshold for starting required minimum distributions — currently age 73. RMDs are amounts that retirees must withdraw annually from traditional 401(k)s and IRAs.

Not all 401(k) plans allow occasional withdrawals from ex-employees — about 43% did in 2025, up from 16% in 2015, the Vanguard study says. If a plan doesn't offer such ad hoc distributions, a former employee who wants to tap that money would have to withdraw or roll over the entire balance. Typically, they roll it over to an IRA and take withdrawals from there.

The most common reasons for retirees taking irregular distributions, based on a 2025 Vanguard survey of 8,000 clients noted in the recent report, are paying off debt, with 31% of respondents citing it; covering unforeseen expenses such as medical care and home repairs, noted by 23%; and discretionary spending, with 22% citing it as a reason.

Regardless of why you take the money, advisors say a key consideration is the impact on your tax situation.

Distributions from a traditional 401(k) or IRA are generally taxed as ordinary income. If you don't have other means to cover the tax bill, you may have to withdraw extra from your retirement account to compensate — which creates a tricky situation.

"It's like a double whammy because you have to pay income tax, but you don't have any money to pay income tax, so you've got to withdraw more money to pay the tax, and then you've got to pay taxes" on the extra amount, Trumbower said.

For illustration: If you need $50,000 after taxes and assume a combined federal and state income tax rate of 25%, the math says you would need to withdraw about $66,667. That's because the money withdrawn to cover the tax bill is itself taxable. A $66,667 withdrawal taxed at 25% would result in about $16,667 in taxes, leaving $50,000 after tax.

"It's going to cost you a lot more than you think," Trumbower said.

A taxable withdrawal from retirement savings can also trigger additional costs elsewhere.

For example, if you're receiving Social Security retirement benefits, the added income could cause a larger portion of that money to become taxable. Depending on how much other income you have, 0%, up to 50%, or up to 85% of your Social Security benefits may be subject to federal income tax. A large retirement-account withdrawal can increase that other income and make more of your benefits taxable.

For retirees on Medicare, a large taxable withdrawal also could increase their Part B and Part D premiums through the income-related monthly adjustment amount, or IRMAA.

For 2026, that surcharge applies when modified adjusted gross income exceeds $109,000 for an individual filer or $218,000 for a married couple filing jointly. Medicare generally bases the surcharge on tax information from two years earlier, meaning 2026 premiums are typically based on 2024 income.

"What catches many people off guard is that they often don't feel that impact until a couple of years after the withdrawal," said CFP Ciano Villaquiran, a partner with CURO Financial Planning in San Diego.

For some retirees, it may be worth delaying the additional withdrawal needed to cover the tax bill until the next calendar year, Trumbower said. That second distribution would be taxable in the following year, potentially spreading the income — and its tax impact — across two tax years.

"Pushing the [additional] distribution from the IRA into the following calendar year may possibly avoid jumping up into a higher tax bracket," Trumbower said.

However, she said, retirees should still withhold enough tax from the initial distribution to avoid an underpayment penalty. The federal income tax system is generally pay-as-you-go, meaning taxpayers must pay enough tax through withholding or estimated payments during the year rather than waiting until they file their return.

Generally, taxpayers can avoid an underpayment penalty if their withholding and estimated payments equal at least 90% of the current year's tax liability or 100% of the prior year's tax liability. For certain higher-income taxpayers, the prior-year threshold is 110%. Any remaining tax due generally can be paid when the return is filed.

If you have a brokerage account, that may be a better source to fund a one-off expense, Trumbower said.

Taxes on long-term capital gains — profits on investments such as stocks held for more than one year — are generally lower than ordinary income tax rates. Depending on your taxable income, some long-term capital gains may even be taxed at 0%.

For 2026, the 0% long-term capital gains rate applies to taxable income of up to $49,450 for single filers and $98,900 for married couples filing jointly. Above those thresholds, most long-term capital gains are taxed at 15%, with a 20% rate applying at higher income levels.

Depending on the expense, retirees may be able to borrow money instead of tapping their savings, Trumbower said.

For example, a home equity loan or line of credit could help pay for a major home improvement project, while a car purchase could be financed with an auto loan rather than a large withdrawal from retirement savings.

Of course, borrowing comes with its own costs and risks. The loan payments would need to fit within the retiree's budget, and the interest paid could outweigh the potential benefits of leaving retirement savings untouched.

Additionally, it's worth considering how the withdrawal could affect your savings over time.

"Pulling a large amount out for a one-time expense doesn't just reduce the account balance today; it can reduce what that money could have grown into down the road," Villaquiran said.

For illustration: $50,000 earning 5% annually would grow to about $81,400 after 10 years, according to the Securities and Exchange Commission's Investor.gov compound-interest calculator. If a retiree withdrew $10,000 at the start and left $40,000 invested under the same assumptions, it would grow to about $65,200 — a difference of roughly $16,300 after 10 years.

CNBC receives no compensation from placing financial advisory firms on our Financial Advisor 100 list. Additionally, a firm's or advisor's appearance in our ranking does not constitute an individual endorsement by CNBC of any firm or advisor.

#2802. Hassett denies report he's leaving White House: 'Not true'

National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett on Friday poured cold water on a report that he's expected to leave the White House in the coming weeks.

That report is "not true," Hassett told CNBC's Eamon Javers in a text message.

Semafor reported earlier Friday afternoon that Hassett "has been inquiring about roles outside the administration," even before Republicans' drubbing in last week's midterm election.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, and his counselor David Zervos, are among the names being floated to replace Hassett, according to Semafor, which cited unnamed people familiar with his plans.

But a White House official told CNBC in a statement later Friday, "There is no factual basis for this reporting."

"Kevin Hassett is a key member of President Trump's economic team and will continue working to advance President Trump's economic policies," the official said on condition of anonymity to discuss the matter.

Zervos, reached by phone after Semafor's story published, declined to comment.

While Hassett denies he is departing the White House, several other Trump administration officials have stepped down since Democrats' staggering wins in the Nov. 3 midterm, flipping Congress blue and overhauling President Donald Trump's plans for the rest of his term.

White House chief of staff Susie Wiles left her role Friday and was succeeded by Stephen Miller. Multiple Department of Justice officials have also left since Election Day, including Harmeet Dhillon, a leader in the DOJ's civil rights division, and John Eisenberg, who headed the national security division.

#2801. International player Joshua Weru on his development behind the scenes

International player Joshua Weru on his development behind the scenes originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Joshua Weru was the talk of training camp this summer. While draft pick Uar Bernard grabbed headlines this spring, it was the Eagles' other International Player Pathway Program player who began to stand out in August.

The edge rusher from Kenya flashed in practices and even in the preseason a bit — to the point where some worried he'd be claimed if the Eagles dared to waive him to get him to the practice squad.

But that was months ago and now we're in November.

Weru has spent all season on the Eagles' practice squad. And most days this season, Weru walks through the Eagles locker room mostly unnoticed. He'll sit at his locker without being bothered and hadn't really been asked for an interview all season. Until last week. So Weru seemed a bit surprised when he was approached and asked for a few minutes, which he graciously handed over.

What have the last couple of months been like?

"It's been good," Weru said. "I continue to say I'm so lucky to be here. The amount of reps we get on scout team and developmental have been good. Just seeing things better and recognizing blocks quicker. Just getting my strike stronger. Even though I'm not necessarily getting stronger in the weight room, like actual game strength is getting slightly better. Just those things because you're more used to doing certain movements so you're getting better at it."

Weru, 23, hasn't been elevated yet this season so he hasn't played in a game since the preseason. Weru misses playing, but last week invoked the old proverb — absence makes the heart grow fonder — to explain his situation.

Over the last couple months, Weru knows he has gotten better. Although he's unsure how to quantify it, his growth is undeniable.

Away from the spotlight of training camp, Weru has settled into a rhythm as a practice squad player. Tuesdays are big for him to get in extra voluntary work. And then on days the Eagles practice, he goes out early to get in what Nick Sirianni always calls "500 shots" of extra work. Those extra sessions for Weru and fellow rookie Keyshawn James-Newby are run by outside linebackers coach Jeremiah Washburn and assistant linebackers coach Tyler Scudder.

But a big part of Weru's role as a special teams player is contributing on the scout team. So each week, he dons a pinny and tries to give the Eagles' offensive line the best looks he can for that upcoming game.

Jordan Mailata has seen a lot of Weru on the scout team this season and admitted the rookie got under his skin a little bit to end last week: "He's very fast and twitchy so he gave a great look this week."

Mailata admitted that because of Weru's lack of football knowledge, misdirections will fool him to where he'll over commit or lose contain in the run game. But Mailata has already seen Weru improve in those areas week by week.

"He's going through that but he's doing a great job of not taking that too personal," Mailata said. "He's hard on himself a good amount. His effort is greater than mine ever was. I just saw him chase down Saquon (Barkley) it was incredible. Incredible."

Weru takes pride in his job as a scout team player and keeps a close eye on the Eagles' offensive linemen on Sunday.

"That gets me fired up, getting to watch them," Weru said. "If I see that they've been doing well, I feel like I helped a bit at least."

The interesting part of being a scout team player for someone like Weru, who hasn't been playing football for very long, is that it exposes him to other styles of defense. This year on scout team, Weru has been learning how to play out of three-point and four-point stances, which has taken some getting used to.

Weru said he didn't realize how hard it would be to get out of a stance like that.

"I feel like I'm slowly getting better at that," he said. "It's also helped me get more reactive because it's not playing to the rules that we have. So you kind of have to know what's going on."

Weru has been practicing every week, while his fellow IPP teammate, Bernard, has been on the Practice Squad Injured List since Oct. 9.

Bernard and Weru were big stories back in training camp and many wondered if they would make the 53-man roster and feared if they didn't, another team would scoop them up. But both players were cut, cleared waivers and immediately signed to the Eagles' practice squad.

While Weru obviously wanted to make the Eagles' roster, he never wanted to leave. And now that he's away from the spotlight a bit, he has been able to grow each week behind the scenes.

No one really knows what the future holds for Weru. Even though the Eagles once hit it big on an IPP player in Mailata, the odds are stacked against Weru and Bernard. But there's no harm in trying and Weru has continued to put in the work.

"It's been really good because just growing and building relationships with guys in my room, coaches, the development staff, all my teammates," Weru said. "So I just feel increasingly comfortable. I can see how much time they're willing to invest in me. I'm just happy to be here."

#2800. AI risks are the biggest threat to the financial system, Fed survey finds

Threats from artificial intelligence topped the list for greatest risks to the financial system, far exceeding any other dangers, according to respondents who participated in a Federal Reserve survey released Friday.

The central bank's twice-yearly look at a variety of dangers saw a surge in respondents saying that the biggest danger comes from AI along several fronts including debt, stock market valuations and security risks.

"Respondents noted three distinct areas of concern: (1) elevated equity valuations and the possibility that lower-than-expected earnings could result in lower valuations and a market correction; (2) cybersecurity risk, stemming from either a malicious attack by sophisticated state actors or damage caused by frontier AI models themselves; and (3) risks associated with complex forms of AI-related financing," the report stated.

The report comes with Wall Street on edge over high-flying AI hyperscalers, while communities are on alert for the rapid spread of data centers used to power the technology. Some 56% of voters in this month's midterm elections said data centers were of crucial or major importance in their choices, according to exit polling.

Respondents to the Fed survey released Friday include a variety of market participants, economists, analysts and institutional sources. The report covers the period up to Oct. 28, just a few days shy of the election.

Of the group, 87% cited AI as a "salient risk" to the system. That was up from 50% in the survey during the spring of this year and well ahead of the next most-cited danger, a risk asset correction, which was mentioned by 52% of those surveyed.

Other leading risks were higher long-term rates (48%), cyberattacks (43%) and fiscal debt sustainability (43%), the latter coming with the U.S. debt over $40 trillion and the budget deficit near $2 trillion.

The report otherwise found the system fairly stable, though it mentioned that hedge fund leverage is at its highest since 2013 and concentrated on a relatively small group.

#2798. Giannis Antetokounmpo and Bam Adebayo dominate Cavaliers in 133-121 loss in Miami

The Miami Heat probably salivated when they saw the injury report and noted the lack of bigs on the Cleveland Cavaliers' roster. Evan Mobley was announced out on Thursday with a toe injury. Early on, it looked as if they would be able to survive his absence on Friday night. However, as the game progressed in Miami, the issues only compounded.

Despite a decent scoring night from James Harden (20 points) and Donovan Mitchell (25 points), the Cavaliers' offense was too dependent on outside shooting. The Cavaliers were relying heavily on one big man on the floor throughout the game. Against most teams, this might be a fine approach. However, the Heat boast a lineup with Bam Adebayo and Giannis Antetokounmpo.

The Heat's All-Star duo gave the Cavaliers fits all night, running downhill and providing a huge problem that they just didn't have the personnel to stop. The Cavs were constantly having their smaller wings and guards tossed into switches that would see them in mismatches that resulted in good looks for both Giannis and Bam.

The Cavaliers, without Mobley's defensive presence, were not up to the task. Bam (37 points on 13-for-19 shooting) and Giannis (37 points on 15-for-20 shooting) heavily contributed to the Heat's 54 points in the paint. The Cavaliers were not able to generate nearly enough offense inside to match Miami's output, scoring 40 points in the paint by comparison.

There were some bright spots for the Cavaliers. Tyrese Proctor (22 points), Meleek Thomas (12 points), and Jaylon Tyson (16 points) all contributed with timely buckets and reliable shooting on a night where the Cavaliers were playing catch-up for the whole second half.

A loss like this stings. It felt like they were generating positive momentum and turning the page from the early season struggles after their big win against the New York Knicks. However, losing Mobley and facing an imposing frontcourt like Miami's halted any forward progress.

The Cavaliers look to turn the page when they face the Dallas Mavericks Saturday night in Cleveland.

#2797. Future Eagles Fits: Jeremiah Smith headlines an explosive 2027 WR class

Future Eagles Fits: Jeremiah Smith headlines an explosive 2027 WR class originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Welcome back to Future Eagles Fits, our weekly deep dive into the prospects who could eventually find their way to Philadelphia.

Every week, we're going inside the tape and looking at prospects through an Eagles-centric lens — breaking down the athletic profile, positional versatility and scheme fit that could ultimately determine whether a player makes sense in Philly. Along the way, we'll examine how prospects are being evaluated across the league as draft boards begin to take shape and conversations are had with representatives across the scouting landscape.

For the Eagles, it's been a mixed bag on the outside this fall. DeVonta Smith and Dontayvion Wicks have missed significant time, Darius Cooper has shown some flashes albeit in small spurts, but rookie Makai Lemon, who currently leads the team in targets, receptions and receiving yards through the first nine weeks of the season, has done an excellent job all things considered. The first-year wideout has been a focal point in the offense and looks like a legitimate complementary WR2 moving forward. Still, however, there are questions about the overall composition of the group and whether Philly has enough explosiveness on the outside to consistently threaten defenses at all three levels. You know what Smith offers, and Lemon has provided a reliable presence, but adding another dynamic playmaker with the ability to create separation, stretch the field and generate yards after the catch could give this offense another dimension.

Currently, the Eagles hold the fifth overall pick in the 2027 NFL Draft, along with the 25th overall pick following the trade of Jordan Davis to Jacksonville. That gives Howie Roseman unique flexibility at the top of the draft, particularly if Philadelphia wants to make another significant investment in the receiver position after drafting Lemon on Night 1 last spring. Whether it's targeting a potential franchise-caliber weapon with an early first-round selection or finding another explosive playmaker later in the round, there are several intriguing options to consider.

While there is still a ton of football left to be played and the entirety of the pre-draft process still to play out – which will alter draft boards – here are six pass-catchers to know in the upcoming class — two potential Day 1 targets, two Day 2 prospects and two Day 3 options who could provide some pop on the outside.

Day 1: Jeremiah Smith, Ohio State | 6'3″, 225 lbs, estimated 4.43 40 time (via summer testing)

If the Eagles are fortunate enough to have Jeremiah Smith available with the fifth overall selection, sprint the card up to the podium.

Simply put, Smith is as elite a wide receiver prospect that the NFL has seen since the likes of Julio Jones coming out of Alabama years ago. A "potential All-Pro as a rookie" as a source said, his combination of size, physicality, and explosiveness is as impressive as you'll find at the position in this draft class, or any other. At 6-foot-3, he has the ability to overwhelm defensive backs physically while maintaining the athleticism and explosiveness to separate at the top of routes, the strength to consistently win through contact and the type of ball skills that allow him to make plays far outside his frame hauling in passes like a Venus flytrap above the helmet of opposing corners.

A former five-star recruit and Heisman trophy candidate this fall, what separates Smith from other physically imposing receivers in the past is his ability to dominate at all three levels of an offense. He's not simply a big-bodied possession receiver who wins in the red zone. He can threaten defenses vertically, create separation on intermediate routes, and turn underneath opportunities into explosive gains with his ability to run through contact and generate yards after the catch.

There's also a level of polish to his game that makes his evaluation particularly exciting. Smith has the physical tools to dominate NFL corners immediately, but he's also shown the ability to consistently win in different situations and against different coverage looks. He's the type of receiver who doesn't need a perfect quarterback situation to produce, making him about as close to a quarterback-proof offensive weapon as you'll find. Quite frankly, he's a player who could have made the jump to the NFL a couple of years ago and still been one of the more talented receivers in the league. He's that special of a talent.

For the Eagles, Smith would represent an opportunity to add a legitimate cornerstone piece to the offensive skill group and showcase what would be arguably the NFL's top wide receiver tandem alongside DeVonta Smith. With questions surrounding the long-term composition of the receiver room and the potential need for another dominant outside presence, his ability to win vertically, create after the catch and consistently command attention from opposing defenses would immediately change the way teams have to defend the Eagles. If he's available at No. 5, no matter what your depth chart looks like, you turn in his card and don't think twice. He's the top player in the 2027 class.

Day 1: Jackson Harris, LSU | 6'2″, 205 lbs, 4.45 estimated 40 time (via summer testing)

A transfer to Baton Rouge from Hawaii, Harris has gone from being a likely Day 3 pick to an athlete that will likely hear his name called on Night 1.

At his size, Harris brings an appealing combination of outstanding route-running and playmaking ability on the outside. He's a receiver with the type of frame NFL teams covet, particularly when evaluating players who can operate outside the numbers and give quarterbacks a legitimate target in contested situations. His transition to the SEC has been particularly impressive, and the production he's put together this season has helped elevate his draft stock. After announcing himself on the SEC stage in Week 4 with 195 receiving yards against Texas A&M, Harris is coming off of electric back-to-back performances against Auburn and Alabama to the tune of 21 catches for 359 yards and two TDs combined in the last two weeks. Defenses know the ball is going his way, and they still can't do anything about it.

For the Eagles, the 25th overall selection could be an interesting range to explore if Harris remains available. His size and ability to provide another outside presence would give the Eagles some additional flexibility with its personnel groupings.

Day 2: Deuce Alexander, Ole Miss | 5'11", 180 lbs, 4.33 estimated 40 time (via summer testing)

If you're looking for an explosive weapon who can immediately add a completely unique dimension to an offense, Alexander is the guy here on Day 2. A Wake Forest transfer, Alexander brings a completely different physical profile than some of the bigger-bodied receivers at the top of the board as his burst and top end speed will force opposing coordinators to know where he his on every snap.

He's not going to overwhelm defensive backs with size, but his fundamental explosiveness and ability to create separation are what make him such an interesting prospect. There are some similarities to Lions WR Jameson Williams in the way he moves and threatens defenses vertically, as Alexander has the type of acceleration and track speed that can force opposing defenses to respect the deep ball, while his ability to create separation gives him opportunities to make plays underneath, as well. He's the type of receiver who can force advantageous matchups for an offense simply by being on the field.

For the Eagles, Alexander would provide a different element to the receiver room. Philadelphia already has a few established options who can operate in the intermediate areas of the field, but adding another receiver with legitimate vertical speed could open up opportunities for the entire offense by forcing defenses to cover every single blade of grass. His ability to threaten defenses over the top and generate explosive plays would make him an appealing complementary piece, particularly if the Eagles are looking to add more speed without allocating first-round capital.

Day 2: Ryan Wingo, Texas | 6'1″, 214 lbs, 4.37 estimated 40 time (via summer testing)

Wingo is another receiver who makes just about everything look easy when watching the tape. Operating in Steve Sarkisian's offense at Texas, he's been utilized in a variety of ways, and that versatility is one of the more appealing aspects of his evaluation.

At 6-foot-1, Wingo has an impressive combination of size, strength and elite athleticism. He's a well-built receiver who can operate outside, but his skill set extends beyond simply lining up on the perimeter and running traditional route concepts. Texas has done an excellent job finding different ways to get him involved, whether that's through jet sweeps, the screen game, vertical opportunities or allowing him to create after the catch to showcase his track speed (as a former All-State sprinter during his prep years in Missouri).

His versatility is highly intriguing as he doesn't need a specific type of play call to make an impact. He's comfortable working with the ball in his hands, has the speed to threaten defenses vertically, and also possesses the physical profile to create problems when defensive backs attempt to tackle him in space. That alone is unique. He's the type of player who can provide an offense with multiple answers depending on the defensive personnel and coverage they're facing.

There's also something to be said about how naturally he operates within the offense. Wingo doesn't necessarily need to be force-fed opportunities to become an impact player, and his ability to contribute in different areas makes him an appealing evaluation for teams looking for more than just a traditional outside receiver.

For Philly, that versatility could be particularly valuable. The Eagles have continued to look for ways to create more explosive opportunities within its offensive structure, and Wingo would provide another player capable of contributing both on designed touches and as a legitimate downfield threat. His skillset expands a playbook.

Day 3: Cayden Lee, Missouri | 5'10", 175 lbs, 4.55 estimated 40 time (via summer testing)

Don't dismiss Lee because of his size, as his ability to consistently make plays regardless of the situation is what makes him worth monitoring. An Ole Miss transfer, Lee has established himself as one of the most productive receivers in the SEC and currently leads the conference in receiving yards with 835 through nine games.

At 5-foot-10, his game isn't predicated on overwhelming defensive backs physically. Instead, his game is built around nuance – the ability to consistently catch the football, mirroring similar routes, create separation and make plays beyond his frame. He has some of the best hands in the country and understands how to find openings in coverage and give his quarterback an opportunity to complete passes, and there's a level of consistency to his game that stands out when watching him. There are some similarities to Lemon in the way he operates as a receiver, as well. He's not the biggest, fastest, or most physically imposing player on the field, but his ability to consistently get involved, make difficult catches and produce within an offense showcases a heck of a football player.

The Eagles don't necessarily need another receiver who profiles as a traditional outside target, but finding a reliable player who can contribute as a WR4 or WR5 while providing some additional explosiveness to the offense potentially on special teams is never a bad investment. His ability to consistently make plays and his production against SEC competition make him a name worth keeping an eye on as the process unfolds.

Day 3: Justin Bowick, Oklahoma State | 6'3″, 203 lbs, 4.50 estimated 40 time (via summer testing)

Bowick is a name whose draft stock could continue trending upward as the evaluation process moves along. Playing at Oklahoma State and catching passes from Drew Mestemaker, potentially the first QB off the board in April, Bowick has an opportunity to continue building his profile as a big-bodied perimeter target.

At 6-foot-3, he brings a physical profile that immediately pops on tape. He's a large presence on the outside who can present matchup problems for opposing defensive backs, particularly when working in contested-catch situations and giving his quarterback a bigger window to throw into. His size alone makes him an interesting developmental prospect, especially for the Eagles who could eye another outside receiver who can provide a different physical element to their offense.

Right now, he has mid to late Day 3 grades from teams but is likely a player to fully indulge himself in the pre-draft process, meaning a good showing at an All-Star game (Senior Bowl or East-West Shrine), the NFL Combine, and pro day circuit could move him up boards significantly.
