#2795. Tokenization platform Securitize gains 10% postmarket on Q3 results, move into asset-backed finance

Tokenization platform Securitize climbed about 10% postmarket after reporting third quarter growth alongside increased operating expenses, and announcing an expansion into asset-backed finance through a strategic acquisition.

Miami-based Securitize, which made its public market debut in July, operates in the relatively new corner of the crypto equity universe involved in institutional tokenization infrastructure. Tokenized assets on the platform grew 9% in the third quarter, to $4.8 billion, from the same period a year ago. Aggregate transaction volume rose 20% from a year ago, to $4.3 billion. Revenue of $11.5 million was up 28% from last year.

The growth came as bullish sentiment around tokenization gained traction throughout the third quarter, ahead of the Security and Exchange Commission formalizing the long awaited Innovation Exemption on Sept. 17, which created a temporary pathway for trading of certain tokenized stocks and strengthened the regulatory outlook.

Profitability is still meaningfully below year-ago levels, due in part to costs associated with going public over the summer, as well as the scaling up of the tokenization business at an active time for the entire sector, Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Securitize reinforced "certain areas of the company like finance, compliance, legal [and] cybersecurity," which increased corporate costs "compared to a year ago when we were privately held," Domingo said. "We're in in a very good position. The adjusted EBITDA … is improving significantly from previous quarters."

Securitize reported an adjusted EBITDA loss of $2 million, compared with positive adjusted EBITDA of $3.6 million a year ago, better than a $5.5 million loss in the second quarter. The company posted a 183% increase in operating costs and expenses.

Also on Monday, the Securitize board authorized a $50 million share buyback and announced the acquisition of River One, an institutional asset-backed financing platform developing technology to connect stablecoin capital with traditional lending markets. Terms were not disclosed.

Securitize sees an opportunity to modernize the $9 trillion market for asset backed finance by using stablecoins to finance real world assets, generate yield and tokenize investment positions and credit products, Domingo said.The acquisition also marks an expansion beyond Securitize's core niche markets at a time when tokenization's competitive landscape is growing, with traditional asset managers like BlackRock and Franklin Templeton, and established financial market infrastructure providers like DTCC and Broadridge, entering the industry.

"We have been very active in tokenized funds, treasuries [and] yield … And we've been obviously recently very active in tokenized stocks, so this adds another piece of the puzzle in the emerging tokenization space for us to capture revenue growth."

#2794. Luis Enrique is ready to AXE 18-year-old PSG flop as Barcelona return looms for Dro Fernandez

Paris Saint-Germain teenager Dro Fernandez could make a rapid return to Barcelona after struggling to secure regular first-team minutes under manager Luis Enrique. The 18-year-old midfielder has barely featured for the French giants this season, sparking intense speculation that his brief spell in Paris may already be over.

Fernandez has found life difficult at PSG since arriving from Barcelona during the last winter transfer window. The French club paid €8 million for the highly-rated Spaniard, but he has failed to establish himself in the French capital.

At just 18 years old, the step up appears to have been too steep for the young talent. He has managed a mere 343 minutes of football across all competitions this season, finding himself increasingly sidelined by manager Enrique.

Despite initially requesting the transfer, Enrique has seemingly lost faith in the teenager's immediate ability to influence matches. Reports in Spain suggest the manager is already open to sanctioning a departure to help the player's development.

According to El Nacional, Enrique has recognised the player's urgent need for consistent minutes. The Parisian coach believes it is still too early to offer him regular playing time, and if Dro wishes to return to Barca, he could facilitate his departure.

The report added that the problem for this player from La Masia is that PSG's investment was based on his potential, while he needs playing time immediately.

Fernandez's drastic drop in playing time is starkly illustrated by his recent European and domestic snubs. After starting the opening Champions League clash against Bratislava, he completely disappeared from the squad for the subsequent three European fixtures.

In Ligue 1, his fortunes have been equally bleak, highlighted by a brief four-minute cameo in a recent fixture against Troyes. This lack of impact has drawn criticism from observers in France, who doubt his ability to perform at the highest level.

Former PSG player and RMC pundit Jerome Rothen recently offered a damning assessment of the teenager. "I have nothing against him, but if you put him in another Ligue 1 club, I'm not even sure he would be a starter," Rothen remarked.

The situation leaves Fernandez at a crucial crossroads in his early career. While he continues to train with the PSG first team, his development risks stalling without competitive action.

A swift return to Barcelona is now viewed as a realistic solution for all parties involved. The Catalan club could offer the midfielder a familiar environment to rebuild his confidence and fulfil his early potential.

#2793. Nvidia stock suffers longest weekly losing streak since 2012 as market value sinks by $1.7 trillion

Nvidia investors turned bearish before this week's Jensen Huang saga. But the selling on Wall Street accelerated in the past few days, sending the stock to its sixth straight weekly drop, the longest losing streak since 2012.

The chip giant, which spent most of the year as the world's most valuable company, has lost 30% of its value during the losing streak. That's a drop of roughly $1.7 trillion in market cap and it pushed the company below Apple.

Huang, who co-founded Nvidia and led it for 33 years, resigned on Thursday after two separate extramarital affairs — one with an alleged Chinese spy and the other with an employee — were made public. The board named finance chief Colette Kress as interim CEO while it conducts a search for a full-time successor.

Nvidia shares fell 13% this week, the most since April 2025. News of the first reported affair hit the front pages over the weekend, when Huang sent a letter to employees apologizing for being "unfaithful to my wife." The board convened late Wednesday after The New York Times reported on the other affair, this one with the company's vice president of health care.

By early Thursday morning, Huang was out, capping a stunning fall for the Nvidia co-founder, who had emerged as one of President Donald Trump's top allies in the corporate world.

"With the unfortunate departure of CEO Jensen Huang we expect a challenging transition for NVIDIA over the next few months," Gil Luria, an analyst at D.A. Davidson, wrote in a report on Thursday. Luria lowered his price target to $200 from $300, but maintained his buy rating. "We believe the market has appropriately adjusted for this transition," he wrote.

Last week, prior to Huang's departure, Nvidia shares dropped about 9%. Much of the decline came after the midterm elections, which resulted in sweeping victories for Democrats and the passage of dozens of ballot measures that could slow the artificial intelligence buildout.

In the weeks before the election, Nvidia's stock was sliding alongside the rest of the AI trade on concerns that rising interest rates would punish debt-heavy companies. While Nvidia is wildly profitable, big customers like neoclouds are reliant on the debt markets.

Heading into next week, the pressure is on Kress. Nvidia is scheduled to report quarterly results on Tuesday. Analysts are projecting 91% year-over-year revenue growth to $108.9 billion, according to LSEG. That's up 18-fold from the same quarter in 2022, just before OpenAI launched ChatGPT and kicked off the generative AI era.

Analysts at Oppenheimer wrote in a report on Thursday that, even though they see the company reporting solid earnings, "we expect fundamentals to take a backseat near term."

"The departure of Mr. Huang could, in our view, translate to a lingering share overhang," they wrote.

#2792. Why was Jordan Davis listening to Sinatra on his way out of Philly?

Why was Jordan Davis listening to Sinatra on his way out of Philly? originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The trade came as a shock to Jordan Davis, so on the way to the airport he did the only thing that made sense.

"On the way to the airport in Philly, I had a moment," Davis said. "You know, I try not to cry. But I prayed and I played a song, Frank Sinatra, My Way.And I think that just encompasses (my) time there and just stepping away and looking toward a new future.

"And obviously the time that I spent in Philly, I have grown so much from that. And I became a better player because of it, became a better man because of it. And it's not to say that I would ever sit there and turn my back on Philly. I don't have any hate. I don't have any bad blood against it. It's just the business of it all. It's just the nature of the beast. And I'm grateful for the time. But right now, I have another mission. I have a new city to embrace.

"Excited to give back to the community and get into the community and see what all Jacksonville has to offer. So I guess I'll save that for retirement. When I really do the big big cry."

Davis spoke to Jacksonville media on Wednesday and clips from that interview were posted on social media by Action Sports Jacksonville, which is Jacksonville's CBS47 and Fox30.

The Eagles traded Davis, their 2022 1st-round pick, to the Jaguars Monday for a 1st-round pick in 2027 and a 2nd-round pick in 2029.

"Excited to work," Davis said. "We have a good front, so excited to be a piece of it. I'm just optimistic about the future. I'm optimistic about me playing a role and being who I am and being a piece to add to this already good team. And however far that we go, I hope to be a part of it."

Davis was a four-year starter with the Eagles, a starter on two Super Bowl teams, a champion in 2024 and a key figure on the NFL's No.1 defense. He had a Pro Bowl-caliber season in 2025 but his play dropped off in the nine games he played for the Eagles this year before the trade.

Jaguars general manager James Gladstone said in a Zoom call with Jaguars media he believes defensive tackles in general can continue to improve well into their career and thinks Davis still has plenty of up-side."That was a major piece to the puzzle," he said. "It's a position that ages well, and certainly look forward to seeing him integrate here in the early going, while at the same time knowing that it's going to be a longer-term decision that we made with him.

"And so making sure that his development over these next few years is something we really think through and plan out. But I think that's certainly a big piece to the why behind it."

With the trade, Davis went from last place to first place, from two wins to six wins. And he went from the NFL's No. 19 scoring defense to No. 2.

And if the Jags' defense seems a little familiar it's because defensive coordinator Anthony Campanile was Vic Fangio's linebackers coach with the Dolphins in 2023 and runs a lot of similar stuff.

"I'm here to do whatever the coaches need me to do," Davis said. "Still figuring out a plan, still learning the playbook. … It's easy to learn. It's different languages. The structure is the same as coming from Philly. The defensive structure is the same. It's the same tree. But it's just learning the language, learning the little nuances of the play.

"It's a lot more fronts that they play here than they did in Philly with a little bit of effort (on the) grindstone, and I'll be here a little bit later (Wednesday) night, I'll pick it up soon."

Jaguars coach Liam Coen recalled coaching against Davis in college and just being struck by his size.

"Jordan Davis, competed against and coached against him when he was at Georgia in 2021 when I was at Kentucky," he said. "And I just remember him walking the field pregame and I'm wondering how we're gonna gain a yard with that defense they had there.

"A young ascending player that has a lot of freakish traits. I mean, he's a large human being that can move, that does have some rush and some three-down value."

Davis joins a terrific defensive line that also includes Georgia teammate Travon Walker, the first pick in the 2022 draft that also produced Davis at No. 13 overall.

"It's gonna be great having my brother back with me," Walker said in another Action Sports video. "If you look back at the season he had last year and the season that he's going to put together this year, obviously, he's still potentially is going to grow every day and that's something that we always preach on there, at Georgia and I'm pretty sure his coaches (with the Eagles) preached on that really as well and now, he's here. So now coach is pretty saying the same thing every day. He wants to get better every day."

The Jaguars on Monday also acquired safety Jessie Bates from the Falcons and defensive tackle Davon Godchaux from the Saints as they reshaped their defense for the stretch run.

"I'm a big guy too, but Jordan, he's huge," Godchaux said. "I'm excited to play with him, feed off him, him feed off me. It's gonna be special. We've got a great group of guys who were already here before we got here. So we're all gonna come together. But, I mean, we have a great group on paper. We still gotta go out and prove it each and every week."

Davis leaves a team that's lost seven straight games and joins a team that on Sunday will be going for its sixth win in seven games. The Jags face the Titans in Nashville Sunday afternoon. The Jags already had their bye so Davis could play 18 games this year.

"I think anybody who plays in big games has a certain level of experience that they could bring to another level," Davis said. "It takes a lot to go to the Super Bowl and it takes a lot to win a Super Bowl, but. right now we're in the process of just one game at a time.

"I know everybody likes to look to the end goal, but you can't see at the end if you don't see where your feet are right now. So I'm just enjoying it, enjoying the moment, looking for my first dub as a Jag, hopefully this weekend. We'll figure out where it goes at the end."

#2791. Ecuador wants to monetize its biodiversity — while still drilling for oil

Ecuador, known for its "megadiverse" Galapagos Islands, Amazon rainforest, Andes and Pacific coast, wants to protect its natural landscapes for years to come — but it faces a tricky balancing act.

As well as preserving its nature, the South American country also plans to capitalize on its critical minerals and continue to extract oil from parts of the Amazon — both of which have led to protests from Indigenous and environmental groups.

One way Ecuador is planning to channel funds into its nature is via new financial tools, such as "debt-for-nature" swaps and its first sovereign green bond. It is also aiming to raise its environmental profile on the global stage by hosting the U.N.'s biodiversity summit, known as COP18, in 2028.

Ecuador, which uses the U.S. dollar, is no stranger to green finance. In March, IDB Invest, the private-sector arm of the Inter-American Development Bank, and Ecuadorian lender Banco Bolivariano issued a $120 million biodiversity bond to back conservation projects. The government also completed two swaps in 2023 and 2024 — deals in which a country's debt is refinanced on better terms in exchange for commitments to spend the savings on conservation. Together, they freed up about $1.1 billion for conservation in the Amazon and Galapagos Islands.

Juan Carlos Blum, Ecuador's minister of environment and energy, said the country hopes to issue sovereign green bonds for the first time in 2027, to raise finance to protect its coastal and mountainous regions and the people whose livelihoods depend on them. The government is looking to the European Union for advice on such instruments, he said. The bloc set green bond standards in 2024.

For Blum, one of the challenges is public awareness. "A lot of us are not aware of how we depend on nature, how we depend on the biodiversity, how vulnerable we all are towards climate," he told CNBC by video call.

Another challenge for Ecuador, as it looks to protect its biodiversity, is its reliance on oil.

The country is a significant oil producer in Central and South America and in October, the government announced plans to open up new tenders for oil exploration in parts of the Amazon, according to local media reports. The news was reportedly met with criticism from Indigenous leaders, who strongly oppose the plans.

"We are being careful on that balance between biodiversity and natural resources use, renewables or not renewables, and it's a delicate matter," Blum said. "That's my day-to-day reality, dealing with balancing this."

About 80% of Ecuador's energy was generated from oil and oil products in 2024, according to the International Energy Agency, while about two-thirds of its electricity was produced using hydroelectric power.

Cracking down on illegal mining is a focus for Blum, who told CNBC he was aware of the environmental impact of legal mining, but that illegal mining was a far bigger problem.

At Ecuador's first Bio Summit held Nov. 4 to Nov. 6 in Quito — an event aimed at helping the country's natural assets "enter the global financial system" — a new way of monetizing nature was introduced.

Marcelo Salame, the event's president, told CNBC he is working on a project to bring so-called biodiversity credits to the country for the first time, to "preserve and monetize" a 260-hectare, or 640-acre, area of the Prosperina forest owned by ESPOL University in the port city of Guayaquil.

Salame said biodiversity is "multidimensional," with credits reflecting a whole ecosystem rather than carbon emissions alone.

Biodiversity credits work in a similar way to the carbon credits that large companies buy to meet sustainability goals, but track a range of measures such as the number of species in a given area or tree canopy cover, with the money raised funneled back into nature protection.

But whereas carbon credits can be used to offset emissions, biodiversity credits cannot. "It's all about funding verified nature outcomes and not, to date at least, compensating for damage," Martin Vaquié, co-founder of Berlin-based Bloomlabs, a company that tracks the biodiversity market, told CNBC.

Jerry Toth, a conservationist and co-founder of Ecuador-based Third Millennium Alliance, a nonprofit that aims to protect the "last remnants" of Ecuador's Pacific Forest, told CNBC that carbon markets can reduce the ecological value of a forest to a single metric: how many tons of carbon it stores.

"That can be potentially dangerous because then you have people that are optimizing for this one metric at the expense of all the other things," he said. Toth hopes biodiversity credits can capture the nuances of an ecosystem.

Toth also runs chocolate maker To'ak — which made headlines for producing the "world's most expensive chocolate bar" — using ancient Nacional cacao grown in Ecuador's Manabí province and processed in Quito.

Third Millennium Alliance works with farmers to restore degraded land by planting cacao in the shade of taller trees, and To'ak pays them around $1.20 per pound of cacao, about double the current market price, Toth said.

Ensuring that chocolate production does not deplete nature is "the key question," Toth said. "Our goal at the outset was, how do we use chocolate to reverse deforestation?"

Ecuador recognizes its "immense" ecological and biological resources, Toth said. "It's still trying to figure out how to … capture that value in a way that makes sense both for people in Ecuador and for the ecosystem."

#2790. What Kansas football’s coaches had to say before KU plays at WVU

LAWRENCE — Kansas football has a must-win game Saturday, on the road against West Virginia in Big 12 Conference play.

The Jayhawks (3-6, 1-5 in Big 12) have lost back-to-back games that they had chances to win. Their hopes of making a bowl game for the first time since 2023 are sinking rapidly. The Mountaineers (5-4, 2-4) are a group that has shown a lot of improvement in the second year of head coach Rich Rodriguez's second stint at WVU.

The game itself will now kick off at 2:30 p.m. (CT) in Morgantown, West Virginia, due to an adjustment made by the Big 12 as a result of the FAA ground stop that occurred Friday. While KU wasn't affected by the travel issues, the kickoff was moved up from 4:30 p.m. (CT). It'll also be televised on ESPN2 now, instead of ESPNU.

RELATED: How to watch Kansas football vs West Virginia: TV channel, streaming

Here's what KU's coach had to say earlier this week, while speaking with reporters ahead of the matchup:

Kansas head coach Lance Leipold described West Virginia's offense as dangerous and explosive in so many areas. WVU redshirt sophomore quarterback Michael Hawkins Jr. leads the Big 12 in rushing touchdowns. It's clear, to Leipold, that Rodriguez is starting to put things together in year two and has brought in the weapons to accomplish what he desires — much like UCF head coach Scott Frost this season.

Leipold detailed that the Mountaineers aren't turnover-prone and won't beat themselves. There's speed and explosiveness in the backfield, including senior running back Cam Cook, not to mention weapons on the outside. It's not just a challenge KU's defense must meet, but a situation where the Jayhawks' offense has to control the ball as it puts points on the board, too.

Kansas associate head coach Andy Kotelnicki highlighted just how multiple WVU's defense is, perhaps the most multiple he's seen in his career. The Mountaineers do a lot of different things up front and in coverage. That can be to create confusion and benefit from offensive protection miscues, or to force turnovers.

Kotelnicki added West Virginia, which has allowed 41 or more points in five of its last six games, gets specific to its opponent on how it uses its ability to be multiple week-to-week. It'll be important for KU to have eye discipline. And he also wants his athletes to play fast and be proactive instead of reactive.

Kansas defensive coordinator D.K. McDonald was with KU when it crushed WVU 41-10 last year in Lawrence, but sees a much different Mountaineers offense this season. Things change, he explained, when you have better players. He allowed there's not even much to take from last year's game, except for some of the plays West Virginia ran.

McDonald described WVU as having a combination of one of the most dynamic quarterbacks in the league and one of the best running backs in the league. That's before even mentioning the talent at wide receiver, and the intensity the offensive line plays with. Tackling and staying in your gap have each been mentioned a lot, because if the Jayhawks don't, McDonald anticipates there being a lot of celebrations in Morgantown his side won't enjoy.

#2789. Nets are learning the hard way that their offense can’t rely only on Julius Randle

MEMPHIS, Tenn. — It's still early, but the Nets have had a couple of weeks of data to learn this new team, to see what works and what gets them worked over.

What they've found out the hard way was less Julius Randle iso ball and more dribble handoffs, less watching and more moving.

"It's a little bit of both. Earlier in the year, obviously you're trying to [learn]," Michael Porter Jr. admitted heading into Saturday's tilt at Memphis. "We didn't get a chance to play a lot of games with Julius. But Julius doesn't want to sit around and iso all game, either. So we've had conversations in film sessions where we're just seeing what works, and then we're trying to use each player to their skill sets.

"I know Day'Ron [Sharpe] can really pass the ball as well. He can, he's good in the [dribble handoff]. So, him getting more usage out of that is helping him play better as well. So I think that we're a ball-movement and a player-movement team. And then we have the luxury of having Julius, who can get those tough buckets off the dribble in those iso situations. So it's a balance."

Brooklyn was all kinds of unbalanced to start the season.

Between poor spacing, a lack of movement — both the ball sticking and players not running to their spots — and a certain amount of natural deferring to the Nets' only All-Star and their best on-ball scorer since the Kevin Durant-Kyrie Irving era, the offense stagnated.

Brooklyn Nets forward Julius Randle (30) goes to the basket past New Orleans Pelicans center Derik Queen (22) in New Orleans, Wednesday, Nov. 11, 2026. AP Photo/Tyler Kaufman

Through their first five games, the Nets went winless and looked terrible. Randle was averaging 5.4 isos — granted, often catching so-called grenades as the shot clock wound down. Their Offensive Rating sat at a wretched 104.8, and the fifth-worst Net Rating in the league.

But they've looked much better in splitting the past six games. Randle's isos have more than halved down to 2.3. Instead, Porter leads the league in DHOs at 5.1; but he's seen them bump from 4.6 to 5.5, per Clutchpoints. And Sharpe has seen his assists vault from 1.2 in the winless start to 6.5 in the 3-3 run since.

The Nets have been the beneficiary coming into the Grizzlies game, with the Offensive Rating spiking to 113.4 and their Net Rating vaulting to a more solid 15th.

"We've always been running our offense like that. The first couple games of the season, it was like we got a lot of new players. So we're trying to figure out each other's game, especially with Julius," Sharpe said. "He brings a lot of gravity, so I'm trying to find out how to play off him. We're all trying to figure out how to play off of him. After film, after talks, we figured out what's working best for us. And I feel we just keep doing that and keep building towards that."

Julius Randle of the Brooklyn Nets looks to pass during a game against the New Orleans Pelicans in New Orleans, Wednesday, Nov. 11, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

The Nets divide their season into five-game blocks. And it took a winless first section for them to figure out what worked, and what was getting them worked over.

Brooklyn coach Jordi Fernández said it wasn't so much a wholesale change of the playbook or what was being run. As the Nets learned each other, they've just started running those same plays better, and with more conviction and precision.

"We run the same plays, but they were not run with the same purpose," Fernández said. "You see now Julius is finishing at the rim, how decisive he is with his drives, how much [more] active [Porter] has been with his cutting and mixing up his cuts and coming to the ball, and also Day'Ron catching it low and feeling comfortable with assisting or making those passes.

"So it just speaks of the work that the group is putting in, adjusting who I am in this new group and how can I be better for the group? And all these guys that I've mentioned — not just these three — but everybody is trying to do the right things not for themselves but for the group."

#2788. They train people to avoid 'honeypot' scams—here are the common red flags they spot: 'It feels so good to be wanted'

Shawnee Delaney often kicks off a training session by telling a room full of corporate workers: "OK, raise your hand if you've ever gotten a flattering message on LinkedIn just complimenting your background and your profile and your experience."

"Every hand goes up," she says. Then, she tells attendees how responding to a simple adulatory message could unwittingly open the door to a potential romance scam.

Delaney is a corporate security expert, well-versed in teaching people how to avoid “honeypot” schemes, in which a bad actor uses romantic or sexual interest to build trust and gain information, access or leverage over a target. The term is currently making headlines in the wake of former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's resignation from the company, following reporting on his admitted affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

Honeypots, also known as "honey traps," are a very human form of social engineering attack, a category that also includes various forms of phishing and use of deepfake technology. And you don't have to be a billionaire tech CEO to possess information that scammers may value.

As competition in the global artificial intelligence race heats up, OpenAI is reportedly training employees to recognize espionage tactics including bribery, romantic manipulation and blackmail, according to The Wall Street Journal. On Nov. 3, cryptocurrency exchange Binance revealed that a recent cyberattack — which saw roughly $6 billion of digital assets stolen — was partially made possible by a fake job interview scheme that infected an employee’s work computer with malware.

"People can get solicited, bribed, romanced, etc.," says Kevin Tian, co-founder and CEO of Doppel, an AI-powered platform meant to help identify and deflect social engineering attacks. He adds: "If you've got access to sensitive IP [intellectual property] — it's not just AI companies — you can be a target."

Increasingly, scammers can use AI to scour websites ranging from Reddit to Pinterest for an individual's personal information and customize a ploy designed specifically for them, Tian says. Once they have relevant data points, attackers tend to use "the oldest tricks in the book: elicitation, flattery, ego," says Delaney, a former intelligence officer with the Defense Intelligence Agency and the founder and CEO of Washington, D.C.-based corporate security consulting firm Vaillance Group.

In many cases, Delaney notes, targets have an inkling that something is suspicious — but they'd rather allow themselves to believe the interest is legitimate. So how do you keep yourself from falling victim? Trust your self-awareness, common sense and ability to identify situations that feel too good to be true, she says.

"The bottom line is, we're all human," says Delaney, adding: "It feels so good to be wanted, or to be respected, or to be appreciated, or loved."

Delaney, who has worked with Fortune 500 companies such as Uber and Merck, advises corporate clients on cybersecurity risks and develops training programs to improve employees' awareness of security threats. Her sessions aim to build awareness of manipulative connections, such as romance scams, from how they begin to the natural progression they often take. "Introduction, then repeated contact, favors, sensitive requests — the secrecy, the pressure," she says.

Bad actors may tap into any information you post online to find an interest that can be mirrored and exploited, or an acquaintance who can be infiltrated and make an introduction. If someone you know and trust makes an introduction, you might be less likely to "go do due diligence" on the new person, even if they're a stranger, Delaney says.

One of Vaillance's services includes building a "targeting package" around an executive or C-Suite member, says Delaney. "We can show how they are vulnerable and how we would target that organization just by leveraging the people around them in that circle: spouses, friends, kids, parents, administrative assistants, neighbors," she says. "That's when people's eyes open."

From there, she discusses red flags to spot. Conversations that begin with flattery and seemingly innocuous small talk can gradually shift toward digging for information, introductions to colleagues or access to documents, says Delaney. It's human nature to want to be helpful and fulfill friendly requests, she adds. Add a potential romantic connection to the mix, especially amid an ongoing "loneliness epidemic," and the urge to comply can be even harder to resist, she says.

If you feel yourself getting cornered in a situation where a recent acquaintance or romantic partner keeps asking for information you're not comfortable revealing, Delaney says it helps to have first practiced polite refusal. "One of my favorites is: They could just respond with: 'Why do you ask?'" she says. "Put the other person on the defensive. You could give a nondescript answer. You can say you don't know. You can say you can't respond."

If you're ever suspicious of a new connection — even if you don't want to be — do yourself a favor and conduct some basic vetting, she recommends. You might examine their social media profiles and other publicly available information, for example, to see if their story checks out.

If you can't find enough information to allay your suspicions, don't be shy about alerting your employer's security or human resources team, she says. For smaller companies, or scams outside the workplace, you can file a complaint for cyber threats or romance scams with the Federal Bureau of Investigation's Internet Crime Complaint Center.

And pay attention if your new paramour starts asking you specific questions about your finances or work credentials, says Delaney.

"If you have no game at home, and you get off an airplane somewhere and suddenly everyone's all over you, we call that a red flag," she says.

#2787. Nvidia is done with Jensen Huang. The fallout in Washington is just beginning

Jensen Huang is out at Nvidia. Washington is just getting started with him and the company he co-founded.

Huang, the leather-jacket-clad CEO of the world's most important chipmaker, resigned from Nvidia on Thursday after revelations that he had an affair with a suspected Chinese spy. But the spy saga is gripping Washington, and lawmakers are preparing for investigations into how the now-ex-CEO's affair may have damaged U.S. artificial intelligence dominance, the national security implications and the executive's close relationship with President Donald Trump and his Cabinet.

Republicans will have their chance first during their last six weeks controlling both chambers of Congress. GOP leaders immediately summoned Huang to Capitol Hill and said they would subpoena him if he doesn't agree to show up. In January, Democrats, who are already eager to make their mark with oversight when they take over the U.S. House and Senate, will be in the driver's seat.

"In this environment where there's some really high stakes when it comes to our national security, I would expect there would be some follow-up, yeah," Senate Majority Leader John Thune, R-S.D., told reporters at the Capitol on Thursday.

Huang and the woman with whom he reportedly had a relationship, Alice Huang, an executive at the Singaporean technology company Megaspeed and an alleged Chinese spy have drawn congressional attention. And Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick has drawn fire after reports this week that he stalled President Donald Trump's crackdowns on firms routing Nvidia chips to China.

Some Congress members have also called for an investigation into the role of the Bureau of Industry and Security, a subagency of the Commerce Department that manages and enforces export controls, in the Huang scandal.

The New York Times reported on Monday that Nvidia's general counsel, Tim Teter, claimed in emails to Commerce investigators probing Alice Huang's company that he'd been in touch with Jeffrey Kessler, who leads BIS. The Commerce investigation into Megaspeed, which allegedly smuggled restricted chips to China, was then transferred to the Justice Department.

By Thursday night, Senate banking committee Chair Tim Scott, R-S.C., summoned both Huang and Lutnick to testify before his committee in December. Later that same night, Lutnick had "proactively agreed to appear voluntarily before the Banking Committee to address their questions," Kristen Eichamer, a Commerce Department spokesperson, said in a statement.

Scott threatened to subpoena Huang if he does not appear. A banking panel spokesperson did not immediately respond Friday morning when asked if they'd heard from Huang.

"Recent reports raise serious concerns about our national security and the protection of sensitive U.S. technology," Scott wrote to Huang. "The American people deserve answers."

And with Democrats poised to take control of both chambers come January, the tech mogul's close ties to the White House will add fuel to an already burning fire as the new majority spools up myriad investigations into the Trump administration.

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., in a post to X on Thursday, said "Congress must demand answers from Mr. Huang and others at Nvidia about how they may have put U.S. national security at risk." Schumer is in a contested race to become Senate majority leader in the next Congress.

The senators who will lead those investigations, and hold subpoena power in the new Congress, are gearing up for Nvidia to take center stage.

Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., the likely incoming chair of the Senate banking panel, said in a statement Thursday that "Mr. Huang, NVIDIA executives, and NVIDIA's board of directors still owe Congress answers about the company's compliance with U.S. export controls

Warren and Sen. Chris Coons, D-Del., the top Democrat on the Senate Foreign Relations subpanel with jurisdiction over East Asia and the Pacific, sent a letter Wednesday to Lutnick, Secretary of State Marco Rubio and White House Chief of Staff Stephen Miller instructing them to preserve all documents and communications related to Nvidia's exports of "advanced AI chips to China or to entities that may divert such chips to China."

Even before Democrats take control in January, Huang is expected to appear before Congress.

House Foreign Affairs Committee Chair Brian Mast, R-Fla., is actively negotiating for Huang to testify before House committees and told reporters Thursday the Nvidia CEO has agreed to appear in the coming weeks.

Details of that testimony are still being worked out, Mast said. It would likely include his committee, as well as the House intelligence panel and the House Permanent Select Committee on China, and it would be under oath and transcribed. It's not clear at this point whether it would be public.

"We want to see him basically in a week … Sooner rather than later," Mast told reporters Thursday, adding that he believed it would happen before Thanksgiving.

If Huang doesn't willingly appear, Mast said he will subpoena him.

Mast, along with House Select China Committee Chair Rep. John Moolenaar, R-Mich., and House Intelligence Committee Chair Rep. Rick Crawford, R-Ark., sent a second request Thursday to Huang and Teter requesting they appear and giving them an 8 p.m. ET Friday deadline to respond. Mast also sent a second request to Kessler.

Rep. Josh Gottheimer, D-N.J., a member of the House Intelligence Committee, said Friday during an appearance on CNBC's "The Exchange" that he wants answers from Huang on the national security and export implications of the affair.

"From an intelligence committee perspective, you can imagine from an AI perspective, how important this is for our national security, for our critical infrastructure and understand when it comes to chip policy, what exactly got to China and ... did Megaspeed divert chips?" Gottheimer said.

The calls for immediate investigation into Huang and Nvidia on the day of his resignation represent a stunning fall from grace in the halls of power for the former CEO. Huang was one of the most effective voices for the AI industry in Washington — able to toggle between leveraging his relationship with Trump and defending his industry from fears about AI's risks to lawmakers.

"He is always the TED Talk guy," one senior Democratic staff member who was granted anonymity to describe private meetings they had attended with Huang, told CNBC. "He's really good and articulate, and really good at explaining why AI is important and why it's so important for the United States to be leading here."

The staffer said Huang was a "happy warrior" for AI, and stayed longer than scheduled or anticipated for questions from lawmakers. But in the meetings with lawmakers the aide attended, they said Huang did not aggressively push against export controls or get too "deep in the weeds" to really "lobby one way or the other" with the lawmakers.

Huang has repeatedly resisted export controls publicly, a move that has rubbed some lawmakers the wrong way.

Some lawmakers even lamented the loss of Huang, despite the inevitable buzzsaw he now faces on Capitol Hill.

"It's just sad all around," Sen. John Fetterman, D-Pa., said of Huang's resignation.

#2786. Playoff roundup: Lakeland Christian, Santa Fe Catholic are big winners

LAKELAND — Lakeland Christian's 1-2 offensive punch of Brody Brenneman's passing and AJ Truedell's rushing simply overwhelmed Zephyrhills Christian.

Brenneman threw for four touchdowns, and Truedell rushed for three scores and caught a touchdown pass in the Vikings' 54-6 victory on Friday night in the Class 1A, Region 3 quarterfinals at Viking Stadium.

Lakeland Christian (10-0-1) will play St. John Neumann at home on Nov. 20 in the regional semifinals, putting the winner of that matchup on a likely showdown against Carrollwood Day (8-3) after Thanksgiving.

The game was close briefly in the first quarter. After Truedell rushed for a 24-yard touchdown to give Lakeland a 7-0 lead, Zephyrhills Christian answered with a touchdown from Giorgio Baker. The extra-point was blocked and the Vikings maintained the lead.

Then Lakeland pulled away. Brenneman threw his first touchdown pass of the game, this one 62 yards to Cael Straub who missed three games with an injury.

Straub showed know ill-effects of his injury as he did most of the work on the touchdown play. With Brenneman scrambling he tossed the ball to Straub standing at the 39-yard line near the sideline. Staub made a move cut back to the middle of the field and outraced everyone to the end zone.

Brenneman and Truedell went back and forth on touchdown plays. Truedell scored next with a 34-yard touchdown run. Brenneman found Messiah Borders of a 33-yard touchdown pass. Truedell followed up with a 42-yard run, and Brenneman's 2-yard touchdown pass to Jayden Wall capped the first half scoring.

Brenneman and Truedell the combined for the first touchdown of the second half as a scrambling Brenneman flicked a 10-yard touchdown pass to Truedell.

Backup quarterback JD McCoy's 9-yard touchdown pass to Wall finished the scoring.

Truedell finished with well-over 100 yards rushing as his three rushing touchdowns accounted for 100 yards.

Victory Christian led after the first quarter and went into halftime tied with St. John Neumann, 21-21. However, the Celtics shut out the Storm in the second half and scored 15 points in the third quarter to come away with the 36-21 victory.

6A-3: Haines City's upset bid quickly fades

Haines City got off to a fast start and built a 16-7 lead over Sarasota Riverview but couldn't maintain the momentum.

Riverview eventually got rolling and came away with a big 52-28 win to advance to the regional semifinals.

Lake Gibson hung close with Tampa Gaither but fell short at Gaither prevailed 16-9.

Trailing 10-3, Caleb McFarlane found Lawrence Silas for a touchdown pass, but a missed extra point allowed Gaither to maintain a 10-9 lead early in the third quarter.

Gaither scored later in the quarter to take the 16-9 lead.

The Braves threatened in the fourth quarter but Gaither intercepted McFarlane at the Gaither 20. Late in the game, Lake Gibson again drove deep into Gaither territory but turned the ball over on downs at the 25.

Santa Fe Catholic senior Reagan Korlacki rushed for 216 yards and four touchdowns on 21 carries to lead Santa Fe Catholic overcame a slow start and defeated St. John Paul II 52-20 in the regional semifinals.

Santa Fe will play host to Evangelical Christian on Thursday in the regional finals.

The Crimson Hawks trailed 14-0 after the first quarter but scored 17 unanswered points in the second quarter to take a three-point halftime lead.

Santa Fe Catholic then outscored St. John Paul II 35-6 in the second half.

The Crimson Hawks did most of their damage on the ground. They finished with 369 yards of total offense with 308 yards coming on the ground.

Bertran Bates, Cam Womack and Chase Sherwood also rushed for touchdowns.

Merritt Island overwhelmed Tenoroc, 63-0, in the Titans' first playoff appearance as a district champion.

#2785. Eagles continue reshaping roster by reportedly releasing veteran wide receiver

Eagles continue reshaping roster by reportedly releasing veteran wide receiver originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Hollywood Brown's brief and odd Eagles career is over after four games and five catches.

The Eagles are releasing Brown by mutual consent of the player and the team, Jordan Schultz of the Scultz Report posted on social media Thursday night.

The Eagles signed Brown to a one-year, $5 million contract in March with the expectation that he'd be a significant part of the Eagles' passing game following the departure of A.J. Brown. But even with Brown gone and DeVonta Smith and Dontayvion Wicks both hurt much of the season, Brown has been an afterthought.

He's only played in four of the Eagles' nine games, getting nine targets and catching five passes for 63 yards along with two rushing attempts for 12 yards. His longest catch was a 19-yarder from Tanner McKee in the loss to the Cowboys. He had a season-high 30 yards in that game.

Brown expressed frustration with his limited role in a recent conversation with NBC Sports Philadelphia:

"Of course, you want to be somewhere where you play, but at the ultimate end of the day, it's not my plan. So I might want to play, I might want to contribute, but if that's not the plan for me, I can't dwell on it too much or you'll be unhappy. And then you're taking that back home. I can't take that back home to the wife and kids. So I gotta try to stay as positive as I can."

Asked how that was going, he laughed and said, "Some days better than others."

He added: "We're all dealing with something and we're dealing with something as a team. So I don't wanna add that extra stress."

Brown said he expected to play more but was dealing with it because he didn't want to put personal issues ahead of team needs.

"I did (expect a bigger role), but I do understand stuff happens," he said. "I mean, you can't take anything personal in this business, so you kind of just got to go about your process and when that time happens, whatever happens, you've just got to be ready for it."

Brown, a 1st-round pick in 2019, had a 1,000-yard season for the Ravens in 2021 and over 700 yards in both 2020 and 2022. He was 49-for-587 with five touchdowns last year. In his career, he has 378 catches for 4,385 yards and 33 touchdowns.

The Eagles, 2-7, have lost seven straight game. Their offense is ranked 32nd in the NFL in yards per game, 30th in points per game and 29th in passing yards per game.

With Brown no longer on the roster, the Eagles have rookie 1st-round pick Makai Lemon, 2nd-year undrafted free agent Darius Cooper and Elijah Moore as healthy wide receivers. Smith and Wicks have both missed the last two games but could be back after the bye week.

Lemon is the Eagles' leading receiver with 37-for-312 and a touchdown and Smith (26-310-2), Wicks (19-284-1) and Cooper (13-200-4) are next.

The Eagles have two WRs on the practice squad – Erik Ezukanma and Xavier Gipson.

Because it's after the trade deadline, Brown is subject to the waiver claim process despite being a vested veteran with seven years of pension credit.

#2784. Lakers’ 11-2 start hasn’t convinced everyone they’re title contenders

The Lakers are among the NBA's top teams this season.

But not everyone is convinced even with the team's 11-2 start. Former NBA champion-turned-analyst Kendrick Perkins doesn't believe the Lakers have done enough to convince the public that they are legitimate title contenders.

Los Angeles Lakers' Luka Doncic (77) reacts during the second half of an NBA basketball game against the Milwaukee Bucks in Milwaukee, Wednesday, Nov. 11, 2026. AP Photo/Aaron Gash

"I don't believe that they're title contenders yet because they haven't got a real signature win yet," Perkins said on the "Road Trippin' Show."

"They haven't beat OKC, although OKC is injured. They haven't beat a Houston Rockets team or the Spurs or the Knicks. I need a real signature win-type moment."

Thus far, the Lakers' toughest matchups have been against the Blazers, Warriors and Pistons. LA's schedule dictates when the team faces top-tier teams, but it appears that has kept Perkins at bay before embracing the Lakers as true title contenders.

Austin Reaves suffered his heel injury during the second half of the Lakers' win over the Bucks on Wednesday. AP Photo/Aaron Gash

Los Angeles Lakers' Luka Doncic smiles during an NBA basketball game against the Milwaukee Bucks, Wednesday, Nov. 11, 2026, in Milwaukee. AP Photo/Aaron Gash

Things were looking dim when Austin Reaves and Luka Doncic exited Wednesday night's game with injuries. However, the Lakers revealed positive injury news about both stars at Friday's practice.

The Lakers' success is largely contingent on the health of Doncic, who escaped a major injury scare to his ankle. If the team can maintain its winning ways while keeping stars including Doncic and Reaves healthy, LA seems on track to make a deep playoff run in the post-LeBron James era.

#2783. After Jensen Huang’s exit, what top financial advisors are doing with Nvidia: Exclusive CNBC poll

Shares of Nvidia continued to slide after the market open on Friday after declining 4% on Thursday following news of co-founder Jensen Huang's resignation as chief executive, amid reports about his personal relationships and national security concerns. The news has raised questions for investors about what comes next for a company at the center of the artificial intelligence boom.

To gauge how wealth managers are responding, CNBC polled members of its Financial Advisor 100 and Financial Advisor Council. Thirty of the 122 firms and professionals contacted responded to the online, multiple-choice flash poll, conducted between 11 a.m. and 5 p.m. EST on Nov. 12. The poll is a snapshot of respondents' views, not a measure of all financial advisors.

Most respondents, 19, said they are holding their Nvidia positions steady. Another four said they are increasing their stake, while seven said they do not own the stock. None said they were selling at the time of the poll.

"Most of our clients are in a position to hold; the reason is because it makes up a smaller percentage of an overall diversified portfolio," Winnie Sun, managing director and founding partner of Irvine, California-based Sun Group Wealth Partners, said in an interview. Sun is a member of the CNBC Financial Advisor Council.

Read coverage of CNBC's 2026 Financial Advisor 100 list, including stories featuring ranked firms and their advisors:

In open responses, several advisors wrote that they are taking a "wait-and-see" approach before changing their investment strategy with Nvidia and are telling their clients to do the same.

"Don't react to short-term headlines," wrote certified financial planner Peyton Jamison, president of Jamison Private Wealth Management in Alpharetta, Georgia. "The company's valuation is extremely attractive, and the focus should remain on its underlying fundamentals and long-term investment potential." His firm is ranked No. 78 on the 2026 CNBC Financial Advisor 100 list.

Asked what factors could change their approach to Nvidia, 13 respondents cited the outlook for AI demand, 11 pointed to valuation, and 10 mentioned company leadership or governance. Some said the decision depends on the client, with eight citing risk tolerance and seven mentioning portfolio concentration. Seven said they do not expect to change their approach.

Nvidia is scheduled to report its third-quarter fiscal 2027 financial results Tuesday, after the market close. In poll remarks, advisors said the company's fiscal third-quarter earnings will help determine their course of action.

Advisors said that for investors, what to do may depend on how much Nvidia stock they already own, when they expect to need the money and how comfortable they are with volatility.

"If this is a company that's going to keep you up at night and you just feel really uncomfortable with these unknown risks, then there was always the option to sell and move that capital to another stock or another portfolio," Sun said.

Nvidia exposure can also come through mutual funds and ETFs. Nvidia's large market value makes it a significant holding in many broad-market funds, including funds that track the S&P 500, as well as target-date funds in retirement-plan options.

Investors can check their Nvidia exposure by reviewing the fact sheets for the funds in their 401(k) or brokerage accounts, which typically list a fund's largest holdings. A stock's decline does not translate one-for-one into a fund's decline.

However, investors with dedicated technology, semiconductor or growth funds may have more Nvidia exposure than they realize, advisors say.

"Nvidia is a big part of virtually everyone's portfolio whether they take an active or passive index approach," wrote Andy Pratt, a partner and director of investment strategy at Burney Wealth Management in Reston, Virginia, which is ranked No. 34 on the 2026 CNBC Financial Advisor 100 list.

He said he advises clients not to overreact to short-term news and to let the dust settle before making decisions.

— CNBC's Stephanie Dhue contributed reporting to this article.

#2782. Lutnick faces questions over Nvidia spy scandal. His sons' firm has deep ties to the chipmaker

In late September, Cantor Fitzgerald hosted Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang and other top executives from the chipmaker for investor meetings in New York, according to a Cantor research report reviewed by CNBC.

The Wall Street firm liked what it heard.

"We continue to view NVDA as the best risk/reward in our coverage," Cantor analyst C.J. Muse and his colleagues wrote in a research report dated Oct. 3 that maintained a $350 price target and overweight rating on the stock.

Yet the firm, formerly run by Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick and now led by his sons, Brandon and Kyle, reported a nearly 50% decline in the number of Nvidia shares it held between September 2025 and March 2026, according to a CNBC analysis of securities filings that excludes options. Nvidia had been one of the largest stock positions reported in Cantor's filings.

The decline coincided with White House and U.S. intelligence officials pressing Lutnick to crack down on Megaspeed, a Singapore-based company suspected of funneling Nvidia's advanced artificial intelligence chips to China. Lutnick repeatedly resisted those efforts, Axios reported Nov. 11.

By the time Cantor hosted Huang in New York, Lutnick had also received a classified intelligence warning, according to a Nov. 10 Axios report, about Huang's ties to a suspected Chinese spy and had appeared alongside the CEO at a G20 technology summit in North Carolina.

Even as Cantor pared back its Nvidia stake, it continued doing business across the chipmaker's AI empire. A CNBC review of securities filings, corporate announcements and Wall Street research found the firm has helped arrange billions of dollars in financing for Nvidia's business partners while maintaining close ties to the chipmaker's leadership.

Those ties have drawn fresh scrutiny following Huang's resignation Thursday and Axios' reporting that Lutnick repeatedly resisted a crackdown on companies suspected of smuggling Nvidia chips to China, despite President Donald Trump authorizing new export restrictions on those companies for a third time on Nov. 5.

An ethics expert said the relationship between Lutnick and his former firm's investments may raise questions, given what the secretary knew about Nvidia.

Richard Painter, the former chief White House ethics lawyer for President George W. Bush and now a corporate law professor at the University of Minnesota, said that while "there's no proof of" Lutnick sharing nonpublic information with Cantor Fitzgerald, "the appearance doesn't look great."

"His conversations with his sons better be about publicly available information," Painter told CNBC.

There is no evidence that Lutnick's sons had advance knowledge of the intelligence concerns, that changes in Cantor's reported holdings were tied to any warnings or that the firm's business interests influenced their father's decisions.

"Cantor has to date from time to time had only immaterial stand-alone proprietary positions in Nvidia, unlike the positions reflected in 13F filings which were entered into in the ordinary course of business in connection with client swap transactions, that are common in institutional markets," a Cantor spokesperson told CNBC.

Cantor declined to answer CNBC's other questions. The Commerce Department, the White House, Howard Lutnick, Brandon Lutnick and Kyle Lutnick did not respond to CNBC's requests for comment or detailed questions about the findings.

As scrutiny of Huang intensified, Cantor Chairman Brandon Lutnick was publicly praising the chipmaker's leadership. "Nvidia is one of the great companies on the planet, and they have an amazing leadership team," Brandon Lutnick told Fox Business on Wednesday.

Trump is standing by Howard Lutnick. A White House official told Reuters on Thursday that the president maintains "complete confidence" in the commerce secretary.

But cracks in the administration's unity emerged Friday, when Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent told CNBC's "Squawk Box" that the departments of Treasury, Defense and State and the Office of the U.S. Trade Representative all "were in favor of putting Megaspeed on the entity list for months."

Bessent pointed to Commerce as the department responsible for imposing the entity list, which identifies restricted businesses and other groups, adding, "You will have to ask them about their internal processes."

Nvidia was Cantor's second-largest reported stock position before Howard Lutnick left Wall Street to become commerce secretary in February 2025, behind only bitcoin investor MicroStrategy, now known as Strategy. At the end of 2024, the firm disclosed more than 3.5 million Nvidia shares worth $479 million, according to its quarterly securities filing.

That share count held steady through September 2025, but its value climbed 36% to $652 million, according to Cantor's quarterly securities filing, as the Trump administration moved over the summer to ease restrictions on Nvidia's H20 chip sales to China.

Over the following six months, however, the number of Nvidia shares Cantor reported holding fell by 49%, to 3 million in December and 1.78 million by the end of March 2026, with their value dropping from $652 million to $310 million, according to a CNBC analysis of securities filings.

Pressure on Howard Lutnick was also mounting on Capitol Hill. Sens. Jim Banks, R-Ind., and Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., in March urged the secretary to suspend or reconsider Nvidia's export licenses after three people linked to Nvidia customer Super Micro Computer were indicted on charges of conspiring to smuggle AI chips to China.

"The Trump administration, I think, leaks like a sieve when it comes to nonpublic government information getting to various hedge fund guys," Painter said, though he stressed there is no evidence Howard Lutnick had shared such information with his sons.

The filings do not establish why Cantor's reported share count fell or whether its overall financial exposure to Nvidia declined. Nvidia’s stock had more than doubled from its April 2025 lows by late October, before retreating in the following months. The stock fell about 6.5% between the end of September and the end of March.

Cantor's reported Nvidia holdings remained near that lower level through June 30, the latest quarter covered by public disclosures, at 1.81 million shares. Its next quarterly filing is due Monday.

Cantor has not publicly explained the decline.

Most of those shares were attributed to CF Secured, a Cantor brokerage subsidiary that uses stocks and derivatives to hedge customer transactions, according to its audited financial statements. Cantor also disclosed substantial Nvidia options positions during the time period CNBC examined, overwhelmingly skewed toward put options, which can protect against declines in the stock price.

As concerns about Huang escalated inside the Trump administration in May, according to the Nov. 11 Axios report, Cantor was helping finance the expansion of one of Nvidia's newest business partners, securities filings show.

On May 7, Nvidia and Australia-based cloud computing provider IREN announced a five-year, $3.4 billion cloud computing contract and a strategic partnership giving the chipmaker the right to invest up to $2.1 billion in IREN.

"That's not a passive financial investment. Nvidia is a partner who wins as we deliver," IREN co-CEO Daniel Roberts told investors on the company's quarterly earnings call that day.

Five days later, as Trump headed to China aboard Air Force One, Secretary of State Marco Rubio warned him about Huang's relationship with a suspected Chinese spy, The New York Times reported Tuesday. Huang, who had not initially been invited on the trip, joined the president's delegation during a refueling stop in Alaska.

Howard Lutnick had already been sent a highly classified intelligence memo detailing concerns about Huang's relationship, according to the Nov. 10 Axios report.

On May 12, the day Trump departed for China, IREN announced the pricing of a convertible bond offering that ultimately raised $3 billion when it closed two days later. Cantor was one of seven bookrunners on the deal, alongside JPMorgan Chase and Goldman Sachs.

On the day the offering closed, May 14, Lutnick's Commerce Department quietly cleared roughly 10 Chinese companies, including Alibaba and ByteDance, to buy Nvidia's advanced H200 chips, though some of those approvals predated the intelligence memo.

It is unclear when IREN hired Cantor for the offering or how much the firm earned from the deal. Cantor declined to answer CNBC's questions about its work with IREN.

Cantor reported a substantial position in IREN's stock.

By the end of June, Cantor reported $115 million in IREN shares and $362 million in Nvidia shares. Together, the two companies accounted for more than 9% of the value of Cantor's reported $5.2 billion in securities holdings, excluding options, according to CNBC's analysis of the filing.

IREN was not the only company in Nvidia's orbit that turned to Cantor for financing.

In October 2025, Cantor advised AI infrastructure developer Lancium on a $600 million financing deal to support its data center campus in Abilene, Texas, the first site of Stargate, the massive AI infrastructure project backed by OpenAI, SoftBank and Oracle.

Newmark, a commercial real estate firm controlled by Cantor, also helped finance the Abilene campus, arranging a $7.1 billion construction loan in May 2025.

Ten months later, Nvidia agreed to invest $2 billion for a roughly 20% stake in Lancium, with up to $1 billion more tied to future milestones, The Information reported in August. Lancium, which is also backed by Blackstone, confirmed an investment and a technology partnership on Aug. 24 without disclosing financial terms.

It's unclear whether Cantor continued advising Lancium after the financing deal or played a role in Nvidia's investment. Neither Cantor nor Lancium responded to questions about the investment.

Cantor's reported ties also extend to Nvidia's supply chain.

In May 2025, days after Howard Lutnick announced the transfer of his ownership stake in Cantor to his children via trusts, Semafor reported that bankers at the firm had informally advised executives at Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co., which makes Nvidia's most advanced chips, on navigating Washington. Cantor called Semafor's report "baseless and false."

TSMC received billions of dollars in federal subsidies created during the Biden administration and administered by the Commerce Department to fund its U.S. manufacturing expansion.

Democratic senators called for an ethics investigation in December into whether the Lutnick family stood to benefit from Howard Lutnick's push to expand AI data centers.

And on Wednesday, Sens. Warren and Chris Coons, D-Del., demanded that Howard Lutnick, Rubio and White House chief of staff Stephen Miller preserve records on Nvidia's chip exports to China and intelligence warnings about Huang.

The senators questioned whether "prudent defensive counter-intelligence action was taken or national security recommendations were overruled."

"The place where I think the Trump administration has most failed here is in being persuaded by Jensen Huang and others to license the export of some advanced chips," Coons said Friday on CNBC's "Squawk Box," accusing the administration of turning a blind eye to the black market for more advanced chips.

#2780. Warning to NFL general managers: Howie Roseman is about to fleece you

Warning to NFL general managers: Howie Roseman is about to fleece you originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

A broken-down receiver, a washed-up guard and an underachieving defensive tackle walk into a bar. And Howie Roseman walks out with two 1st-round picks and three 2nd-round picks.

If I were an NFL general manager, I wouldn't even answer the phone when Howie calls. Because you're going to get fleeced.

And if there's one thing to feel good about during this miserable season to nowhere, it's the way Howie has stockpiled premium draft picks with a series of what sure seem like lopsided trades.

It all started in June when Roseman shipped disgruntled and divisive A.J. Brown to the Patriots for a 1st-round pick in 2027 and a 5th-round pick in 2027.

Brown was one of the best in the league not too long ago. He owns the two-best receiving seasons in Eagles history – 88-for-1,496 and 11 TDs in 2022 and 106-for-1,456-7 in 2023.

But it sure seems like his knees won't allow him to be that kind of player again. The A.J. Brown we saw hobbling around and dropping passes in his final game in an Eagles uniform, the playoff loss to the 49ers in January, seems to be closer to the player he is now than the Pro Bowler of a few years ago.

The Patriots got a WR whose balky knees have allowed him to play only three games so far this year, with just nine catches for 101 yards, three drops and a fumble.

Of 95 WRs who've been targetted at least 20 times so far this year, Brown's 15 percent drop percentage is highest. And his catch percentage is 89th. Maybe he gets healthy the second half of the season and reverts back to that three-time Pro Bowler. Or maybe this is just who A.J. Brown is right now. Either way the Eagles got a 1st-round pick for a guy who had no future in Philadelphia, didn't want to be here and may be a shot fighter at 29.

Then came the Cam Jurgens trade, and it's still hard to believe the Ravens donated the Eagles a 2nd-round pick for a guy who just hasn't been the same player as he was in 2024. Jurgens was a tremendous interior lineman before his career was derailed by injuries. Although he made a second straight Pro Bowl last year, he was only a shell of the player he was in 2023 and 2024. This year, he struggled both at center and guard before Howie someone finagled a 2nd-round pick out of the Ravens for him.

How has it gone in Baltimore for Jurgens? You always take Pro Football Focus grades with a grain of salt, but out of 89 interior linemen who've played at least 300 snaps his 43.9 combined grade with the Eagles and Ravens is 4th-worst.

In Jurgens' three games in Baltimore, the Ravens – with both Lamar Jackson and Tyler Huntley hurt – have scored 33 points, gone 0-3, losing 35-13 to the Bengals, 36-10 to the Bills and 33-10 to the Jaguars after starting the season 4-1.

Then there's Jordan Davis. He was a good player here and at times a very good player. He probably should have made the Pro Bowl last year. But overall? As the 13th pick in the 2022 draft and the 10th-highest paid interior lineman in the league? He's underachieved, in particular this year. He was a key starter on two Super Bowl teams but he just never dominated the way you wanted him to, the way he's being paid to. And he played just 52 percent of the snaps in his nine games this year, which ranks 27th among starting interior linemen so far this year.

Great guy, natural leader, inspiring backstory, solid lineman. But hardly indispensable. Not when you also have Jalen Carter under contract and Moro Ojomo now in line for an offseason extension. The Eagles don't really have a third high-level rotational defensive tackle now – Ty Robinson, a 4th-round pick last year, will likely take over the role. But if you can get 1st- and 2nd-round draft picks for essentially a part-time player that's a no-brainer.

Add it up and Roseman got 1st-round picks in 2027 and 2028, 2nd-round picks in 2027, 2028 and 2029 and a couple 2027 5th-round picks to boot for a beat-up wide receiver, a fading interior lineman and a defensive tackle who hasn't lived up to expectations.

The way Roseman drafts, most of those picks are going to turn into important pieces as the Eagles build back toward respectability.

There hasn't been a whole lot to celebrate on the field this year, and everything points to more of the same over these last eight games.

But up on the second floor of the Jefferson Health Training Complex, Roseman is doing all he can to make sure it doesn't happen again.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2779. What's ahead for AI financing after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang departs

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's departure hasn't produced a broad shock to the credit market, but it is putting the company's role as a financier of artificial intelligence infrastructure under scrutiny.

Huang, who stepped down Thursday amid an espionage and adultery scandal, built Nvidia into an AI powerhouse in the 33 years since he co-founded the company. Chief financial officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO as the company searches for a permanent chief executive.

Nvidia not only supplies its graphics processing units to hyperscalers and tech giants, it also provides financing to other companies to help the industry expand. That could all change.

"Although we expect the company to fulfill all of its previously announced commitments, we believe that going forward, it may be more difficult for the company to negotiate new multi-party investments among ecosystem partners at the same scale it has done up to this point," Goldman Sachs analyst James Schneider said in a note Thursday.

The news of Huang's resignation comes after investors were already concerned about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Tech bond yields have risen and spreads have widened on credit default swaps, most notably in lower-rated names. Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier.

On Friday, the yield on Nvidia's 4.5% bond due in 2031 sat at 5.717%, nearly 5% higher week to date. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

Tech stocks slumped Friday with the Nasdaq 100 closing down 1.2% as markets continued to digest the Nvidia news. Yields on hyperscaler bonds ticked slightly higher on Friday, signaling the broader concern about AI debt lingers.

It's the smaller AI companies that could ultimately see an impact if Nvidia makes any changes to its AI financing. The company had $36 billion in off-balance-sheet AI cloud agreements, or backstops, for neoclouds, according to its last quarterly financial filing in July.

Neoclouds CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all in Nvidia's portfolio. Their shares were down sharply Thursday.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child for AI debt fears, saw its bond yields retreat after its quarterly earnings on Tuesday eased investor concerns. The yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 was 14.79% on Friday after climbing to 16.7% last week. The spread on its five-year CDS also dropped more than 17% week to date, although it remained above 1,000 — which is generally considered a sign of distress.

It remains unclear how Nvidia's support for those companies may change, said CreditSights analyst Andy Li.

"We don't know what the ultimate strategy for Nvidia is going to be coming out the other side," he said.

"However, I think directionally, just given the fact that Jensen was so crucial in selling the Nvidia story and being a very aggressive deal maker, it's likely that a lot of this creative deal-making and circular financing and equity and credit support is going to moderate," he added.

CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC Wednesday, before Huang's departure, that Nvidia "is not one person."

"There are thousands of people over there that interface with thousands of people at my company every day. They integrate on the technology. They integrate on the physical infrastructure. They integrate on the networking," he said in an interview with "Squawk on the Street." "They have been a great partner for us for years. They will continue to be a great partner for us."

Bob Lang, founder and chief strategist at Explosive Options, doesn't see any big changes in terms of Nvidia's relevance or its ability to finance for now. As CFO, Kress was involved in the financing deals, and the search for a permanent CEO is going to take some time, he noted.

The real issue is just how much money the company will want to lend for AI financing, rather than, say using its hundreds of billions in free cash flow for other purposes, like share buybacks, capex, or acquisitions.

"Big companies like Nvidia and Meta, they're holding all the cards," he said. "When they decide that the game is over, that's it — it's going to be curtains for some of these smaller companies."

— CNBC'S Kif Leswing and Isabel O'Brien contributed reporting.
