#2778. Analysis: Companies love a ‘rockstar’ CEO. Until they depend on one

When Jensen Huang quit, it struck me how quickly the conversation shifted from his departure to whether Nvidia could manage without him.

It wasn't about whether the company had the technology, customers or talent to keep growing, but whether anyone else could do what Huang had been doing for the business. That made me wonder: did investors believe in Nvidia, or just Huang?

The company is hardly short of capable people. Interim CEO and Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress has been with Nvidia since 2013. Nvidia has experienced engineers, established customer relationships and a leading position in the artificial intelligence chip market.

But, listening to guests on CNBC this week, I noticed how much of their concern was about the influence Huang had built outside Nvidia.

On Friday, just a day after Huang quit, Dylan Patel of SemiAnalysis told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" how Huang would persuade executives at smaller companies that make critical components to commit capacity to Nvidia. This helped it secure "the majority of supply chain capacity," he said.

Without Huang, competitors could try to persuade those suppliers to allocate more capacity to them, Patel argued. And so the big question is, how much of Nvidia's advantage comes from its technology and how much comes from the relationships Huang built?

There are obvious benefits to elevating CEOs to be rockstars. They can attract investors, open doors and give a company a new identity. A charismatic leader attracts attention, reassures investors and makes a company's strategy easier to sell.

Huang was particularly good at this. He helped shape the conversation about where AI was heading and made Nvidia central to it. Donning his trademark leather jacket, Huang took to stages around the world, courting world leaders to lay out his vision and explain why Nvidia would be at its center.

Before Huang resigned, Eric Ross told "Squawk Box Asia" that Huang "is the company."

That may sound like an endorsement, but it also helps explain why his departure has raised so many questions.

Bernstein's Stacy Rasgon made an interesting distinction on the same program, also before Huang's departure. Nvidia had capable executives who could keep the business running, he said. What was harder to replace was Huang's vision.

"They need a visionary like that," he said.

But for a company whose valuation reflects not just what it sells but expectations of what AI could become, replacing that vision may prove harder than replacing the CEO.

Duncan Clark pointed to another part of Huang's job. He had become a bridge between Silicon Valley, Asia and Washington, and one of the most prominent voices for the wider AI industry.

"I don't think you can replace him with one individual," Clark said on "Access Middle East" on Friday. Replacing him with multiple people is "going to take time, and it won't be the same," he added.

That distinction may be important. Nvidia's board has begun a search for a permanent CEO and said it will consider both internal and external candidates. But whoever takes the job may not personally assume every role Huang had come to play around the company. Some of his responsibilities and relationships could end up being spread across Nvidia's broader leadership team.

For now, the board is emphasizing continuity. New chair Harvey Jones said Kress is backed by an experienced leadership team and that Nvidia expected its operations, customer commitments and product roadmap to continue without interruption. Kress, meanwhile, pointed to Nvidia's people and leadership team as strengths as the company moves forward.

Other boards will be watching, particularly those that have allowed their CEOs to become the face of everything their companies do.

When a charismatic leader is the only person outsiders hear from, it becomes harder to convince them that anyone else can take over. There is also the question of accountability.

Clark described Nvidia's situation as a governance failure. And my colleague, "Squawk Box Europe" anchor Steve Sedgwick, raised a similar point on Friday about how corporate America has approached ESG.

Much of the political debate has focused on its environmental component, Steve said. President Donald Trump has repeatedly attacked environmental and climate policies using phrases such as "green scam." But Steve questioned whether the governance element has been overlooked, particularly at some of America's biggest technology companies.

"When you have a 'Rockstar' CEO you need way more governance, NOT less," he said, citing his recent conversations with CEOs and senior executives.

His point was that powerful leaders can come to believe the normal rules don't apply to them. Without strong governance to challenge them and prepare for their departure, the consequences for a company can be far greater.

Linda Ford, chief executive of The Chartered Governance Institute in the U.K., said Nvidia showed what could happen "when a company's leadership is built around one person." She noted that Nvidia had never previously had a board chair, adding: "A high-profile leader can be an asset, but the board needs to work hard to protect its independence and provide the scrutiny shareholders are entitled to expect."

And that gets to the bigger issue. How easily can a board challenge a CEO who has delivered extraordinary success, particularly when investors have come to see that person as indispensable?

Companies spend years putting leaders in front of investors and customers and making them central to the business. But how much effort goes into making sure the company can function without them?

Nvidia now has to show that the business Huang built can stand on its own. Other boards might want to ask themselves whether they could say the same.

#2777. Mikal Bridges follows through in first game after promise to ‘be better’ for Knicks

INDIANAPOLIS — Mikal Bridges repeated it eight times in a three-minute interview Friday: "Be better."

The floundering shooting guard took ownership of his struggles before a rejuvenating performance in Friday's 106-104 loss to the Pacers, saying he's the lone problem and solution.

"I just look in the mirror, I just got to be better," said Bridges, who scored a season-high 18 points on 9-of-12 shooting Friday. "Nobody else to blame but me, and I just got to go out there and, whatever it is, just be better and go harder."

Bridges was light on specifics, but his shooting numbers were ugly. Before Friday, he was at 32.9 percent from the field overall and 26.5 percent on 3-pointers — despite many of his looks being wide open.

Mikal Bridges (25) of the New York Knicks dribbles against the Indiana Pacers during an NBA Cup game, Friday, Nov. 13, 2026, in Indianapolis. NBAE via Getty Images

Head coach Mike Brown responded by benching Bridges for long stretches, preferring to close games with reserve Landry Shamet.

"When times are tough, you just gotta own up to it and just be better," Bridges said.

After Bridges played just 17 minutes in Wednesday's loss to the Cavaliers — the fourth time in 11 games that he's logged 17 or fewer — Brown said it'd be "hard to find minutes" for Bridges and "even for times for Josh Hart."

Brown reasoned that reserves Shamet and Deuce McBride are playing well.

But the Knicks also have $150 million tied up in Bridges, who said he's not relying on flipping a switch to excellence in the playoffs like the past two seasons.

"I try to be my best throughout the whole year. Obviously, struggles come, which obviously nobody wants, but like I said, in these moments, gotta look in the mirror, and you're tested the most and just see what you're made of," Bridges said. "So, just gotta be better."

Bridges had a different tune last season during a slump, when he blamed a lack of opportunity for his performance decline.

"Sometimes you try to get open, and sometimes it doesn't find me. Just try to find ways to stay aggressive," Bridges said in March, a little more than a month before he started a dominant playoff run. "That's it."

This time, Bridges was self-scrutinizing.

New York Knicks forward Mikal Bridges (25) shoots against the Indiana Pacers during an NBA basketball game in Indianapolis, Friday, Nov. 13, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

"I got to do everything better," he said.

Brown said he hasn't thought about removing Bridges from the starting lineup because he's a proven vet and it'd be a "panic decision" so early in the season. But there's a minutes crunch at the wing with Bridges, Hart, Shamet and McBride.

On Friday, Bridges played well, and Hart (zero points, 0-for-4 shooting) was benched.

"There are several different ways you can look at it," Hart said. "You can look at it, if [Shamet and McBride] have it going, cool. Good. You could you look at it like, 'OK, maybe something else has to change [like a trade].'

"But there's going to good days with that, bad days."

Free-throw discrepancy was a storyline in the first half. The Knicks took just five free throws in the opening two quarters; the Pacers quadrupled that amount with 20. Over the entire game, the Pacers shot 25 free throws to the Knicks' 13.

Frustrations boiled over late in the second quarter when Brown berated a referee over a non-call. Brown was irate and picked up a technical foul. The Knicks trailed at the break, 58-49.

"I did get a tech. But the reality was the Pacers were playing harder than us. It was out of frustration," Brown said. "Some of it because … we have a guy like Jalen who is an All-Star and an MVP candidate, and he takes zero free throws in the first half. … That, in itself, is frustrating to know that we're getting beat that bad at the free-throw line and they're missing some calls. Are we fouling them? Yes. And we have to do a better job earlier. But in the same breath, the discrepancy shouldn't be that bad two games in a row."

Brown also lamented the free-throw discrepancy in Wednesday's loss to the Cavaliers.

#2775. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.

#2774. Milton Williams takes action to address Patriots’ penalty problem

Pre-snap penalties have been a major problem for the New England Patriots defense this season.

Defensive tackle Milton Williams has had enough.

"I put in fines this week," the defensive tackle revealed Friday "$1,000 fine for whoever jumps offsides."

Williams has been guilty of the infraction himself, getting flagged twice in the first three weeks of the season and again in Week 7. Those miscues helped inspire the new system.

"I had a couple early in the season, and I feel like we talked about it, but is not enough consequences that come with it other than hurting the team," Williams said. "So [if] it hurt your pockets, maybe you'll think about it a little more."

The penalties have become more prevalent in recent weeks, with the Patriots defense flagged for seven infractions over the last two games. On the season, New England leads the league in encroachment penalties (4), ranks fourth in neutral zone infractions (5) and is tied for the eighth-most defensive offsides penalties (3).

Williams said cleaning up those small details will be an important part of the defense's effort to bounce back after allowing a season-high 45 points to the Green Bay Packers last week.

"It's on us to be ready to go, be ready for the hard count, we've been jumping offsides. We've got to be ready for that stuff going into every game," Williams said. "It's costing us. We want to get teams in third and five-plus situations so we can go out there and rush. Jumping offsides is just giving them third and short, so we can't go rush the passer."

According to Williams, any fines collected will go toward members of the team's training staff or custodians in the building.

"We'll take care of somebody in the building. They'll have a pretty good lunch on us, if we can't do what we're supposed to do," he said.

Since signing a four-year, $104 million contract with the Patriots last offseason, Williams has established himself as one of the team's leaders. Craig Woodson was not yet aware of the fine system from his safety position, but he praised Williams for holding his teammates accountable.

"I mean, that's a leader right there," Woodson said. "That's something that we got to make sure that we can't give them free yards. So, for him to do that, that's a leader."

#2773. Microsoft apologizes for massive outage that took down Outlook and other 365 services

Microsoft issued an apology Friday for a wide-ranging outage that shut down Outlook and other Microsoft 365 products, as the company worked to restore service nearly 24 hours after connectivity problems began.

"We are deeply sorry for the disruption this incident has caused our customers and partners. We know the trust they place in our services and take that responsibility extremely seriously," a Microsoft spokesperson said Friday.

On Saturday, Microsoft said it had addressed the underlying issue and restored service to the remaining impacted customers and regions.

The connectivity disruption stemmed from "an issue during an earlier network capacity upgrade," Microsoft said in a Friday statement. The software giant added that the underlying cause of the outage had been addressed and that they were still working to restore services to some customers.

Engineers scrambled to solve connectivity problems that began shortly before 1 p.m. ET on Thursday.

Email and calendar service Outlook, along with multiple Microsoft 365 services such as Sharepoint and Copilot, were largely down during the morning hours in the U.S. on Friday and throughout the Asian and European business days. Numerous external services that connect with Microsoft products were also hit. Users began to regain access to email and other services Friday morning.

In an update around 10:30 a.m. ET on Friday, Microsoft noted "encouraging recovery progress" and said that "service availability and email delivery performance [was] improving across a growing portion of affected infrastructure" as Outlook and other Microsoft 365 tools began to come back online.

In an earlier update Friday morning, Microsoft said that email queues were "approaching normal levels," but that intermittent issues were expected as recovery efforts continue. It had previously given updates by region, with Asia Pacific services among the first to start normalizing.

Engineering teams have been taking affected systems offline for fixes and bringing them back online gradually stages. They are prioritizing efforts to preserve emails delayed during the recovery process and reduce email queues and backlogs, extending the timeout window, whereby delayed emails continue to attempt delivery, to 48 hours to help protect messages.

Microsoft says it has not detected any malicious activity causing the outage, which it initially blamed on a traffic surge in part of its infrastructure and issues with a component responsible for processing server health. The company said it would issue a full report once its assessment of the outage was complete.

The company said in its initial updates on Thursday that the connectivity problems related to infrastructure "elevated load across multiple regions," caused by a "number of servers being unexpectedly unavailable; straining the remaining infrastructure." Some servers were failing automated health checks, it said.

Microsoft shares traded largely flat Friday.

Governments and businesses across the U.S., Asia-Pacific and Europe said they were monitoring the situation, which was causing widespread disruption.

The U.K.'s National Health Service said the outage was affecting some of its services but that patients should attend appointments as normal. Calls "may be subject to long waits" as a result of disruption to the organization, a hospital group serving South West London and the English county of Surrey said on X.

Earnings for Swiss luxury goods giant Richemont were slightly delayed on Friday due to the outage. Electric truck maker Workhorse Group, which was expected to report third-quarter results on Nov. 12, rescheduled the release to Nov. 16, citing business disruptions caused by the global Microsoft outage.

U.S. Rep. Josh Gottheimer, D-N.J., told CNBC's "The Exchange" that he asked for a briefing on the outage. Gottheimer worked at Microsoft prior to serving in Congress.

"Is there something that went on that's nefarious here, or is this just something else? These outages occur in a lot of the companies, but the size of it and the length of it and the timing of it," Gottheimer said.

Air India said it had "temporarily rerouted" emails so it could continue to receive customer messages, adding that its flight operations were not affected.

Australian Home Affairs and Cyber Security Minister Tony Burke said in a press conference that Microsoft "believe they are dealing in some ways similar to what we saw with respect to CrowdStrike where there is an update that has gone awry," Reuters reported.

A fault with an update issued by cybersecurity company CrowdStrike caused one of the world's biggest IT system outages in 2024, hitting airlines, banks and more.

Microsoft reported in 2025 that Copilot, its collection of AI assistants and tools, had more than 100 million monthly active users.

The incident highlights a business "resilience and dependency issue that has been decades in the making," James Owen, managing partner of Digital Risks at consultancy Control Risks, said by phone.

"We have become, organizationally in the private sector and more broadly, increasingly dependent on a smaller segment of technology and infrastructure as critical to daily life and business activities."

"Organizations are becoming more aware through a geopolitical lens about what technologies they adopt, whether their services are provided by firms in the U.S., or China, or elsewhere, but this shows the importance of proper recovery planning in the case of an IT outage. That could be manual backups, protocols that do not require internet connection, investing in diverse suppliers and crisis management planning rehearsals," he said.

Amazon Web Services and Google users also reported outages on the site on Thursday.

Amazon wrote on X that services were "operating normally" and an "unrelated disruption elsewhere on the internet is prompting some inaccurate news reports." The company referred users to its health dashboard for "accurate data."

At 4 p.m. ET Thursday, Microsoft said it had "validated an alternative service health check method" that brought some affected servers back online.

"We're now applying this change more broadly," Microsoft wrote. "We're also preparing automated recovery systems to continually detect and restart unresponsive servers, to maintain service stability while this change progresses."

#2772. Japan vs Brazil: The tactics board - Samurai Blue's transition mastery overcomes Vinicius Junior's individual brilliance

Japan secured a dramatic 3-2 victory over Brazil, leveraging high-intensity transitions to dismantle a star-studded Seleção. Despite two goals from Vinicius Junior, Japan's tactical discipline and late adjustments proved decisive in the final moments.

Hajime Moriyasu entered this international friendly with a clear objective: to stress-test Japan's defensive structure against the world's most elite individualists while maintaining a lethal threat on the break. Recognising Brazil's penchant for expansive possession, Japan sought to create a compact mid-block that would lure the South Americans into central traps before exploding into the half-spaces via their technical wingers. The goal was to prove that tactical cohesion could override the raw talent gap usually associated with the Selecao.

On the opposite bench, Carlo Ancelotti utilised a flexible system designed to isolate Vinicius Junior in one-on-one situations. Brazil's tactical blueprint revolved around verticality and quick rotations in the middle third, aiming to pull Japan's disciplined backline out of position. By deploying a high defensive line and tasking Bruno Guimarães with ball progression, Ancelotti intended to suffocate Japan in their own half and exploit the spaces between the lines with rapid, short-passing combinations.

Japan lined up in a structured 4-2-2-2 formation that prioritised defensive lateral compactness. Their pressing triggers were highly specific; rather than chasing the ball aimlessly, they allowed Brazil's centre-backs, Gabriel and Cunha, time on the ball until they attempted to find a midfielder. The moment Bruno Guimaraes or Andre Luiz received with their back to goal, Japan's double pivot of Ao Tanaka and Daichi Kamada swarmed, initiating rapid transitions. This was evident in the opening 20 minutes, where Takefusa Kubo and Keito Nakamura exploited the spaces vacated by Brazil's marauding full-backs. Kubo's 15th-minute opener and Nakamura's strike shortly after were products of S. Nakano and Ritsu Doan's ability to find vertical passes immediately after a turnover. Defensively, Japan dropped into a 4-4-2 low block when sustained pressure arrived, forcing Brazil to cross from wide areas rather than penetrate the box centrally.

Brazil operated primarily in a 4-3-3 that frequently morphed into an asymmetrical 3-2-5 in possession. With Carlos Augusto providing width on the left and Estevao tucking in from the right, the Selecao focused their attacks through the left-hand channel to facilitate Vinicius. The Real Madrid star was the focal point, provided with the freedom to roam across the front line. Brazil's build-up play relied heavily on Guimaraes dropping deep to form a back three, allowing full-backs to push high. While this created numerical advantages in Japan's final third, it left their defensive transition vulnerable. Despite dominating the ball, Brazil's pressing was often disjointed, failing to stop Japan's initial exit pass. However, their individual quality remained undeniable, as seen in both of Vinicius's goals, which stemmed from Guimaraes finding pockets of space to release the winger behind Japan's defensive line.

The defining tactical battle took place in the central corridor between Japan's double pivot and Brazil's attacking midfielders. Moriyasu won the initial skirmish by instructing Kamada and Tanaka to sit deeper than usual, negating Raphinha's ability to operate as a traditional 'number ten'. This forced Brazil to rely on individual dribbling rather than systemic penetration. The mismatch was found in the speed of Japan's wide players against Brazil's slow recovery runs. While Ancelotti's side possessed the technical floor to equalise through Vinicius's double, they lacked the structural integrity to manage Japan's substitutes. Moriyasu's decision to remain patient despite being pegged back to 2-2 allowed Japan to wait for the specific transition moment that led to T. Sakamoto's winner in stoppage time.

Both managers were proactive as the game fluctuated. At the hour mark, Ancelotti introduced Endrick and Allan to add physical presence and energy to a fading press. This temporary shift to a more aggressive 4-2-4 helped Brazil claw back into the game, culminating in the 75th-minute equaliser. Moriyasu responded with a flurry of tactical substitutions, most notably bringing on T. Sakamino, Takumi Minamino, and Junya Ito in the 72nd minute. These changes refreshed Japan's counter-pressing intensity. Realising Brazil were over-committing in search of a winner, Moriyasu shifted Japan to a temporary 5-4-1 defensive shape in the final ten minutes, bringing on Ko Itakura to solidify the box. This defensive reinforcement provided the platform for one final break, as Sakamoto found the net in the 91st minute, perfectly punishing a Brazil side that had lost its tactical shape in the dying embers.

Ultimately, Japan's victory was a triumph of systemic discipline over individual brilliance. While Vinicius showcased why he is among the world's best, Brazil's inability to defend transitions cost them dearly. Moriyasu's use of bench depth to maintain a high-intensity press in the final quarter proved to be the masterstroke. By sacrificing possession for structural integrity and clinical verticality, Japan exposed the defensive frailties of Ancelotti's expansive system. The Samurai Blue's tactical maturity in the closing stages ensured that their collective plan trumped the Selecao's star power.

#2771. Meet the seven co-founders of Anthropic

Four researchers, a former journalist, a public policymaker, and an engineer founded Anthropic in 2021. Six years later, the artificial intelligence company is about to go public, with trading expected to begin on Nov. 19, in what will be one of the biggest IPOs on record.

Those seven co-founders are poised to each be worth many billions of dollars.

At the end of 2020, siblings Dario and Daniela Amodei were vice presidents at OpenAI, the company that kicked off the generative AI frenzy with the launch of ChatGPT. But the two had grown frustrated with their employer over its approach to AI safety. Fearing a world where AI models' capabilities would progress faster than our understanding of them, the duo launched Anthropic early the next year.

They took a handful of OpenAI colleagues with them: Tom Brown, the company's research engineering lead on GPT-3; Jack Clark, a policy director; Jared Kaplan, a contract research consultant; and Sam McCandlish and Chris Olah, both members of OpenAI's technical staff.

They were all deeply concerned about OpenAI's ambitions to focus on commercialization, and they now stand to be some of the first to cash in on the AI boom.

"They really started Anthropic not because they were hoping to get rich, but they started Anthropic because they like wanted to shape this really important thing in the world, and they thought that other people might do it worse than them," said Jeffrey Ladish, who joined Anthropic as a contract safety engineer in September 2021, and now serves as the executive director of AI safety institute Palisade Research.

Collectively the co-founders own about 10% of Anthropic. Each has about 240,000,000 class A shares, or about a 1.4% stake.

During Anthropic's most recent funding round, those shares — valued at $58.90 each — amounted to about $14 billion in value for each co-founder. If Anthropic's IPO reaches its target of $70 to $78 per share, or a market valuation of nearly $1.4 trillion, it would push those stakes to $17 billion to $18 billion.

"It's extremely hard not to be influenced by your incentives," Ladish said. "You have investors, and money is not just like an end; it's also a means."

Anthropic did not respond to requests for comment.

The face of Anthropic is CEO Dario Amodei. He holds the most shares of the bunch: 245,259,100 including shares subject to voting proxies.

Amodei, 43, didn't get his start in business. He comes from the world of research, obtaining a doctorate in computational neuroscience from Princeton University in 2011. He then went to work at Stanford University as a postdoctoral scholar from 2011 to 2014.

Even in the private sector, Amodei's experience was focused on research. He worked at China's Baidu, leading research on speech recognition from 2014 to 2015, followed by a stint at Google Brain working on deep learning and neural networks, foundational scientific frameworks for today's large language models. He joined OpenAI in July 2016.

On the Big Technology podcast in July 2025, Amodei said he got into AI to solve the "almost intractable" and "slow moving" problems of biology.

"I think my reason to start a company was that I had worked at other companies and I just didn't feel like the way those companies were run was really oriented towards trying to have that impact," he said, adding that the desire for impact was often used by companies as an insincere ploy to recruit talent.

At OpenAI, Amodei is credited with co-inventing reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF), alongside Anthropic co-founder, Tom Brown. RLHF is a fine-tuning method used to align model outputs with human needs. That could mean training a model to speak politely or to not offer violent or harmful solutions to problems.

Four years her brother's junior, Daniela Amodei, 39, serves as the president and board chair of Anthropic. The Amodeis are both San Francisco natives, growing up in the Mission District, just a few miles away from Anthropic's current headquarters.

The pair were teenagers during the height of the dot-com bubble and its subsequent fallout, both attending Lowell High School, a magnet school and one of the most prestigious public schools in the country. After graduating, Daniela went on to study English literature and politics at the University of California, Santa Cruz, earning her degree in 2009.

Daniela started her career in politics, working for Matt Cartwright, a Democrat representing what was then Pennsylvania's 17th congressional district, and then moved into tech, taking on various roles in risk management at Stripe for five years.

In a June interview with Stanford's Graduate School of Business, she said her experience in politics, specifically campaigns, naturally led her to startups and Silicon Valley.

"I was like, 'Wow, a small number of people that work really hard, who are young and driven, can really change the world,'" she said.

Anthropic's chief compute officer, Tom Brown, 39, is perhaps the most entrepreneurial of the bunch. After graduating from the Massachusetts Institute of Technology with a Master of Engineering in 2010, Brown went on to found two startups, MoPub and Grouper.

MoPub was a mobile advertising business acquired by Twitter in 2013. There he served as founding engineer in 2011. Grouper, where Brown served as co-founder and technology chief, was a Y Combinator-backed group dating app.

After Grouper, Brown pivoted to research, sharing on Y Combinator's Lightcone podcast in August 2025 that he was self-taught on machine learning research, a daunting task since he received a B- in linear algebra at university.

"I kind of flip-flopped on it for six months trying to build up courage to" make the switch, he said.

Alongside co-inventing RLHF, as a researcher at OpenAI, Brown led the training efforts on GPT-3, a model that marked a historic leap from GPT-2, going from 1.5 billion parameters to 175 billion.

Jack Clark, 37, the head of public benefit at Anthropic, left his career as a journalist in 2016 to work at OpenAI. Clark is now also the leader of the Anthropic Institute, the company's internal think tank that explores AI's social and economic impacts.

A native of the U.K., Clark grew up in the seaside town of Brighton and later studied English literature and creative writing at the University of East Anglia. He then went on to write for publications including ZDNet UK, The Register, and Bloomberg.

In an email to a colleague at Bloomberg at the time, Clark said his decision to leave journalism was because he had "never believed in something strongly before" and that he needed to join OpenAI "and live with the consequences, which I hope are good."

Kaplan, 42, Anthropic's chief science officer, still holds a position as an associate professor in the department of physics and astronomy at Johns Hopkins University. The theoretical physicist got his PhD in physics from Harvard in 2009, working for four years after as a postdoctoral fellow at the SLAC National Accelerator Laboratory, a particle physics research facility run by Stanford and funded by the Department of Energy.

Kaplan co-pioneered scaling laws, or the notion that large language models get exponentially better when trained on larger and larger data sets, alongside Dario Amodei. He also pioneered "constitutional AI," or a method of aligning models by using a list of safety principles and policies. His day-to-day work concerns safety assessments for models, responsible scaling policy at the company, and existential risks.

In an interview with The Guardian in December 2025, Kaplan noted that the decision over whether or not to let AI "recursively self improve," or write the code to enhance itself without a human in the loop, would be "in some ways the ultimate risk, because it's kind of like letting AI kind of go," and that he expects this risk will be taken sometime between 2027 and 2030.

Anthropic's chief architect, Sam McCandlish, 36, came to OpenAI as a fellow focused on AI safety. He also led early research on coding tool Codex, which now constitutes a significant portion of OpenAI's revenue.

McCandlish claims he and the rest of the Anthropic co-founders were not following an entrepreneurial spirit when they charted out on their own. In a December 2024 video from Anthropic's YouTube channel, he said, "None of us wanted to found a company. We just felt like it was our duty."

McCandlish, like Kaplan, also came from the world of academia, completing his PhD in theoretical and mathematical physics at Stanford in 2017 and working at Boston University as a postdoctoral fellow the year afterward. His work focused on quantum gravity and tensor networks.

Now, his work focuses on pre-training, or the first step of training a large language model on swaths of data, among other things.

Chris Olah, 34, heads Anthropic's interpretability research, a branch of study in machine learning that attempts to understand how a large language model "thinks" — or rather, which data inputs and reasoning methods it uses to arrive at its outputs.

Like McCandlish, Olah was not originally keen on starting a company. In the same December 2024 YouTube video, Olah said, "I was arguing for a long time that we should do a nonprofit instead, and just focus on safety research."

Olah had a nontraditional path to Anthropic, dropping out of the University of Toronto after one year and working for short stints as a developer at the government agency Environment Canada and software startup Xelerance.

His career truly took off when he was awarded a Thiel Fellowship in 2012, which he used to pursue research pertaining to 3D printers for two years. He then joined Google Brain, first as an intern and then as a researcher, before moving to OpenAI in 2018.

While together the seven founders may only own about 10% of total equity, they will have a majority vote. Founder LLC, the legal entity set up that owns a single Class F share, gives Anthropic's co-founders a 50.1% majority voting control over the company. While Benjamin Mann, a member of Anthropic's technical staff and also an ex-OpenAI employee, is often called a co-founder, he is not an owner of Founder LLC.

The co-founders are expected to collectively sell over 20 million shares during the IPO, resulting in roughly $250 million in cash per founder, according to Anthropic's prospectus.

They plan to dedicate 80% of the wealth from their personal Anthropic equity to philanthropy, according to the IPO filing, though the pledge isn't legally binding. Anthropic is also setting aside 5% of its shares to form a new public benefit fund — to finance nonprofits, research, and potentially direct community grants — with potential to grow the size of the fund as the company itself grows.

#2770. Diving deeper into Cavs 133-121 loss to Heat: Supporting players shine in losing effort

Facing a front line of Giannis Antetokounmpo and Bam Adebayo is a tall task for any team — especially one without the services of Evan Mobley (toe). The Miami Heat's ability to attack the depleted frontcourt led to a 133-121 loss for the Cleveland Cavaliers.

37 points on 15-of-20 shooting, 9 rebounds, 2 assists, 1 block

Typically, the Cavs use Jarrett Allen as the primary defender on Giannis, with Mobley shading in his direction to provide help at the rim. Without Mobley, Allen was left on an island guarding Giannis without much help behind him. Allen fought hard, but he could only do so much.

The Heat have struggled as a team this season, but those issues haven't come at the expense of Giannis's individual numbers. He's still been playing at a superstar level.

37 points on 13-0f-19 shooting, 14 rebounds, 3 assists

Peyton Watson drew the primary assignment on Adebayo with Mobley sidelined. Watson needed to help on Giannis drives and was giving up considerable strength in his individual matchup with Adebayo. These two factors allowed Adebayo to get whatever he wanted inside, as evidenced by shooting 9-of- 12 in the paint.

25 points on 9-of-21 shooting, 7 assists, 4 rebounds

This was a good game from a process perspective. Mitchell got rid of the ball when traps came his way, and trusted his teammates when he needed to. Miami's defense was collapsing inside on any dribble penetration, leaving room for outside shots. Mitchell took advantage of this, going 8-for-17 on jump shots. The issue came on drives and floaters inside, where he was just 1-for-4.

Anything bought from the links helps support Fear the Sword. You can buy the comfortable Cavs hoodie HERE. You can also shop all of Homage's Cavs gear HERE.

4 points on 2-of-10 shooting, 4 rebounds, 2 assists

This was a tough matchup for Watson. He was asked to guard a player he had no business defending due to injuries and, unsurprisingly, struggled. The issue was that he couldn't take advantage of Miami's propensity to shrink the floor on the other end.

Watson's frustrations from the outside continued in Miami. He went 0-for-5 from three on a night when the Cavs desperately needed some additional floor spacing.

The process hasn't been an issue for Watson. He's taking the right shots and doing some encouraging things on the defensive end, but the results just haven't consistently been there.

22 points on 7-of-9 shooting, 4 rebounds, 3 assists, 1 steal

Proctor has been coming into his own over the past week. At the start of the season, he seemed hesitant to put the ball on the floor and attack as a scorer when given opportunities. That has gradually shifted in recent games, culminating in an impressive offensive performance in Miami.

On Friday, Proctor was hunting for his outside shot. He didn't hesitate when the ball swung to him on the perimeter. That's the confidence the Cavs need from someone who should get plenty of open looks given the attention the starting guards draw.

The version of Proctor we've seen over the past few games is indeed a rotation player. At this point, the question is whether he can still maintain that confidence in all aspects of his offensive game when he isn't shooting as well as he has recently.

"Bam and Giannis kind of had their way with us."

– Kenny Atkinson on what went wrong in this game

You're just not winning a game like this when Miami's front court combines for 74 points.

The Miami Heat were in the 98th percentile for offensive rating this tgame (137.1).

Miami dominated inside. They retrieved 34.8% of their missed shots (73rd percentile), had a free-throw rate of 33 (85th percentile), and scored 56 points in the paint. Controlling the paint and forcing your way to the free-throw line is still the most effective way to generate a great offense.

As easy as it would be to blame the Cavs for these issues, sometimes you just have to tip your cap to the other team. Miami had the personnel to take advantage of Cleveland's injuries, and they did.

Zach Lowe made a good point on his most recent pod that I believe is worth expounding on: The Cavs have slowly been evolving their identity.

Cleveland found out in their 2025 series with the Indiana Pacers that they need more well-rounded players who can pass, dribble, and shoot.

Their initial effort in identifying those supporting pieces didn't necessarily work out (Lonzo Ball), but their recent attempts have been much more encouraging. Guys like Peyton Watson, Mario Hezonja, Meleek Thomas, and Tyrese Proctor can fill multiple roles on the court and are more than just specialists. Jaylon Tyson also fits that box, although he was drafted before the Pacers series. This archetype stands in stark contrast with previous supporting players like Dean Wade, Georges Niang, De'Andre Hunter, and Keon Ellis, who were more specialized in one particular skill.

Quietly, the Cavs have shifted from a team with specialists around their core players to one where their core is insulated with more well-rounded skill sets.

It made sense to put specialists around the Cavs in 2023. That team desperately needed more shooting around the original core four after Mitchell's first season in Cleveland. It's nearly impossible to win playoff series if you can't spread the floor in any meaningful way. Prioritizing shooting allowed them to advance in the playoffs in subsequent years, but if they intend to take the next step, they needed to diversify their rotation players.

Early injuries to Sam Merrill and Nae'Qwan Tomlin have given some of the newer supporting players more room to shine, and they've taken advantage of it. Against the New York Knicks, it was Thomas and Proctor who carried them through some of those bench lineups, and it was Watson who made the game-saving block. Here, it was Proctor who provided consistent scoring, Thomas (12 points) who supplied some shot-making at the end of the third to get them back into the game, and Tyson (16 points) who filled in well as a starter.

The Cavs are still figuring some things out, and are far from a finished product. But if you wanted something positive to hang your hat on early in the season, it's the fact that the supporting pieces have the potential to change how this team plays.

Stars still matter. The Cavs will have a tough time winning playoff series this spring if Mitchell or Harden lay an egg. But the emergence of some of these supporting pieces has the potential to change what is asked of the stars during close postseason games. Maybe Thomas can carry some of the scoring load for stretches in the second and third quarters. Maybe Watson's ability to recover defensively alters how the two-big front line operates defensively and further insulates the guards. Maybe Proctor can help bring the ball up against the press, alleviating that burden from the stars.

The Cavs didn't get the win on Friday. Trying to defend a Giannis-led team without Mobley is a tall task. Results notwithstanding, we've seen some encouraging steps forward from some of the supporting cast the last week or so. That could make a huge difference if the stars can get healthy and more in sync than they have been.

#2768. Trump doesn’t plan to raise Nvidia, spy issue during China trip: U.S. officials

President Donald Trump is not expected to raise allegations of a Chinese intelligence operation involving former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang during his upcoming meeting with Chinese President Xi Jinping, senior U.S. officials said Friday.

Officials also said that Elon Musk, the CEO of Tesla and SpaceX, will accompany Trump on the trip to Shenzhen, China, for the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit on Nov. 18 and 19.

The White House, earlier Friday, said Trump plans to have a three-way summit there with Xi and Russian President Vladimir Putin.

Jensen Huang's relationship with the suspected spy, Alice Huang, who is not related to him, has raised concerns that a Singapore-based company where she was an executive diverted restricted Nvidia chips to China.

"The situation with Nvidia should not really have any bearing on the trip," an official said on the press call, speaking on the condition that they not be identified.

"In all of our discussions and negotiations with the Chinese, we do not negotiate American export controls ... and we don't expect it to figure at all in any of our conversations with the Chinese while we're there."

Asked if Trump would raise the allegations against Alice Huang to Chinese leader Xi Jinping, the official said, "The focus of a meeting with President Xi, at least on the economic side, is going to be about implementation of the trade agreement."

"When it comes to some of these national security matters and examinations and investigations that are going on in the U.S., [that is] probably not something that we're going to read the Chinese in on at this moment," the official said.

Another official said, "I don't expect the president to raise it — the Alice Huang issue."

China has called the allegations against Alice Huang baseless.

Huang resigned this week as Nvidia's CEO after damning reports that he had an affair with Alice Huang, who was an executive of Megaspeed, a cloud-computing company that is being eyed for possible facilitation of sending billions of dollars worth of Nvidia chips in violation of U.S. restrictions on those chips.

The scandal has rocked Nvidia and raised questions about whether the Commerce Department, which is led by Howard Lutnick, has been aggressive enough at blocking advanced chips from ending up in China.

During Friday's call, a reporter asked why Musk was going on the trip with Trump.

"I don't want to speak for the president on exactly why Elon's going," an official said.

"Obviously, he has tremendous business interests in the U.S.-China relationship. He's a big part of our national security," the official said. "There are numerous reasons, given the engagement that the president is going to have, where I think Elon would be of tremendous benefit, just even as an advisor to the president."

#2767. Tom Izzo comments on Michigan State basketball's win vs. Northern Iowa

On Friday night, Michigan State basketball followed up their huge win over Duke with a 34-point win over Northern Iowa. Despite the huge point differential, Tom Izzo was a little disappointed in his team after the game, as Northern Iowa was able to cut into the lead at times due to some sloppy defense from the Spartans.

Below, you can see what Izzo had to say about the game:

"[Nick] Saban, love him to death, 'cause I think my guys drank a little bit of his flavor. That rat poison." Izzo is referring to Saban's famous "rat poison" line about players believing their own good press.

"I had a long meeting with Jeremy this morning, and he vowed what he was going to do, and he might have had his best game as a Spartan… The way he ran the team, the way he shot the ball, the way he scored it. Nine assists, no turnovers."

"I blamed Jeremy and Coen. And yet I couldn't even blame Jeremy, so I had to blame myself in the huddle, because he did such a good job… I'll make sure tomorrow morning I get after him, because nobody can withstand too many compliments."

"I love the way he responded to a meeting. I guess I've got to do a better job with more meetings."

"We get up 19-2, and I know all about what everybody wants to say: 'Well, they took the foot off the pedal.' I didn't take my foot off the pedal. They took their foot off the pedal."

"Give [us] credit for Duke. Talk about how tough and good a defensive team we are. Don't give [us] credit for this, because we weren't as tough and we didn't play as good a defense. So that's the way it works."

"I just thought things came too easy, and we showed immaturity."

"At halftime, we didn't sit around and drink tea… We were a little bit upset with the way we were playing, and give the kids credit, they responded."

"That was as physical a team as we'll play."

"We wanted to get the ball inside, but we knew you couldn't put it down. It was like a swarm of bees… You put the ball on the floor, it's getting taken away."

"That was one of the harder-playing teams we've played in a couple years. They reminded me of the old Purdue teams. They play hard as hell. They're very well coached."

On the 61 fouls: "That should set a North American record. How can you play a game when there's 61 fouls?"

"I do think we have one of our best shooting teams. I've said that from day one… That start was unbelievable, and every one of them was bottom of the net."

"When I started, we couldn't shoot the ball in the ocean. That's why we were the great offensive rebounding team, because, as Jud would say, our best offense was the missed shot. And now we're making shots. And if we could add the defense that we normally play, then a good team becomes a really, really great team."

On Julius's three: "Julius, God bless him… That'll be a trivia question 50 years from now."

"Like most coaches will tell you, the best thing about a freshman is when he becomes a sophomore."

"It doesn't matter if you're 15 or 50, the effort should be there if you're an athlete."

"Up in the U.P. where I lived, the basements up there are where the furnace is, and they're always dark and they're cold. I used to go down there to get something for my mother. I was 8 years old, and you know what I did the whole way? I talked, 'cause I was scared to death… 'How's the weather up there, Mom?' Anything that I could hear a voice, 'cause voices are comforting. When you don't hear a voice, it's not comforting."

"Younger generation, you've got a lot of positives. That ain't one of them. Communication's not one of them."

"If you can't communicate, sooner or later you're going to get interviewed. So you better learn how to communicate."

When a reporter said the communication "could be a little bit better," Izzo replied: "God, you're so kind. I think it's bad."

"It's going to be by committee for a while… The disadvantage is nobody's raising their game enough that I can feel comfortable with that."

"Jordan got a little stinger. I don't think it's anything serious, but they'll probably X-ray it three times, MRI it four times, and then figure out what it is. But I don't see that being a major problem."

#2766. San Antonio vs Golden State, Final Score: Spurs punish the Warriors 123-85 for their 11th straight win

The Spurs blew out the Warriors in the most lopsided game of the year. Without the services of Steph Curry, Golden State had to depend on super rookie Yaxel Lendeborg to provide firepower, and Spurs defense simply shut him down, holding him to 3 points on 1-13 shooting. Two way player LJ Cryer led the scoring for the visitors with 20.

The entire roster got into the action as Luke Kornet had one of his best defensive efforts of the season with a couple of blocks and most importantly, giving Victor Wembanyama plenty of comfortable minutes on the bench resting, playing 20 minutes. Stephon Castle broke out of his mini-slump with 32 points in 25 minutes. Carter Bryant continued his progression to becoming a rotation player with a solid effort on both ends of the court. Tarris Reed played almost the entire fourth quarter. I can't find anything to complain about the Spurs' performance in this game. It was just about perfect. And the team keeps getting better. It's good to be a Spurs fan.

Blake Griffin really seemed kind of like a conceited jerk when he was a player, but as an announcer, he's insightful and has a good connection to his colleagues in the studio and asks really good questions of the guests and players. I don't know if I was wrong in my initial assessment of his character, but I kind of like this Blake.

Draymond is the career leader for the NBA in technical fouls with 166. However many he's had, he's earned a lot more. He has a real talent for getting away with an incredible amount of complaining and questionable plays, and he only gets called for a small fraction of them.

It annoys me when the announcers called the Warriors' rookie Yaxel LendeNborg. Where did the extra 'N' come from? It's Lendeborg, thank you.

Congratulations to the Pacers who held off a furious last minute rally from the Knicks to win 106-104 in the lead-game on Prime. I thought that game was going to go to overtime.

The Spurs started the game on a red-hot 12-0 run, causing an early Steve Kerr timeout. That didn't help as the Silver and Black kept pouring it on and the Warriors were colder than interstellar space. They didn't score a single point for the first half of the quarter, finally getting a bucket from Gui Santos just after the midpoint after the Spurs 18-0 start. Luke replaced Victor in the lineup, and the Spurs defense was just good instead of being impenetrable, and Golden State scored an impressive 14 points in the rest of the quarter, trailing 38-14 after one quarter, with two-way player LJ Cryer doing a lot of the damage.

Lendeborg pretty much believes that he never commits a foul. I think that's part of the Warriors' character.

Victor fell awkwardly early in the second quarter, and it was a little scary when he took some time to get up. Amazingly, he seemed fine after a minute or two. His resilience is unreal.

Draymond threw Victor the floor a few plays after that, which was clearly not a basketball play, and it was called a flagrant 1 foul, but it should have been an ejection in my opinion. He pushed Vic so hard that he injured his elbow and had to go to the locker room, so there was some poetic justice in the incident. Draymond did return to the game in the third quarter, though.

The Warriors were more physical in the second quarter, while the Spurs kept their foot on the gas, and led 63-33 going into the locker room. A 30 point lead seems pretty safe, except for that one time that they led by 29 and … it's just too painful.

Golden State had a little renaissance at the beginning of the third quarter, as their shot found the hoop and the Spurs went a little cold for a few minutes while the broadcast team of Tobey and Elliott was talking about fried chicken. The visitors were only able to shave the Spurs lead to 24 when they came back with a vengeance and Castle, Wembanyama, and Vassell ballooned the lead back to 33, 35, 40 and more. The Warriors renaissance was over in the blink of an eye.

I'm happy for Charles Bassey. He's found a home in Golden State, and he's really blossoming as a player.

Jordan McLaughlin entered the game with a couple of minutes left in the third, which is usually a good sign that Mitch is feeling comfortable that the game is under control.

Tarris Reed got into the game with 11 minutes left, and played all 11 of them, looking like a monster on the boards. Or maybe some kind of bear.

For the opponents' frame of mind, visit Golden State of Mind. I haven't read them recently, but I expect to see a lot of Wemby isn't fair comments on the game thread. I feel bad for them missing their best player, hopefully he won't miss a lot of the season.

The Spurs play again on Sunday afternoon in Utah against the Jazz. The Jazz are one of three teams in the league who only have one win, and need I say TRAP GAME!?

#2765. SpaceX launches Starship in key test flight of massive rocket

In a key test flight on Friday, SpaceX tried but failed to recover both stages of its massive Starship rocket.

The rocket lifted off from the company's South Texas launch facility at 4:47 p.m. local time, or 5:47 p.m. ET. SpaceX had to abort its plans to catch and reuse the booster, instead diverting it to splash into the Gulf of Mexico, where it burst into flames.

Test Flight 15, the most ambitious Starship mission to date, was the first time Elon Musk's company attempted to catch both the booster and upper stage, or spacecraft portion, of the massive rocket.

Earlier on Friday, SpaceX obtained authorization from the Federal Aviation Administration. Hours later, the FAA issued a nationwide ground stop after reporting communication problems affecting "multiple" air traffic control centers. The SpaceX license wasn't effected by the flight stoppage, a spokesperson for the FAA told CNBC.

The upper stage of the Starship continued its trajectory into orbit after the company failed to catch and salvage the booster. The rocket is expected to fly around the Earth fourteen times in total. If successful, SpaceX will land the upper stage back at its Starbase facility on Saturday.

Starship's success is critical to SpaceX and its ambitions to eventually lift cargo and people to the surface of the Moon and Mars. In the near-term, SpaceX needs to send more satellites into orbit to expands its Starlink satellite internet service, which is currently the company's profit center.

The company's constellation already includes over 11,000 active satellites in low Earth orbit. One Starship rocket has the capacity to carry up to 60 Starlink v3 satellites into orbit, the company has said, representing about 20 times the capacity of earlier Falcon 9 rockets.

In the previous Starship test flight, the company released 26 v3 satellites, and released another 26 in the Friday test flight, with the goal of "Starlink becoming 51% of the internet," SpaceX said in a post on X.

When it comes to launches, "SpaceX is likely a full decade ahead of peers on this front," William Blair analysts wrote in a note out on Friday. "Even if tonight's catch is unsuccessful, a delay by a few months is not that consequential."

Meanwhile, SpaceX is facing fresh criticism over restrictions imposed on its Starshield satellite internet service in Ukraine. Starshield is built on Starlink technology and is the military-tier of SpaceX's satellite internet service.

Earlier this week, Ukraine President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said his country's military forces had experienced limits on Starshield without warning again, harming their ability to defend against Russia's ongoing aerial attacks. Similar limits on Starshield in Ukraine were temporarily imposed last month.

Some U.S. senators have condemned the restrictions, and urged Congress to investigate SpaceX.

#2764. Joe Burrow can join Peyton Manning as only QBs with 4 total TDs in 5 straight games

Bengals quarterback Joe Burrow has been racking up touchdowns recently, and if he continues to do it on Sunday night against the Steelers, he'll join some very exclusive company in NFL history.

Burrow has had at least four touchdowns, combined rushing and passing, in each of the last four games. If Burrow has another four-touchdown game on Sunday night, he will join Peyton Manning as the only quarterbacks in NFL history with at least four combined passing and rushing touchdowns in five consecutive games.

Burrow's last four games:Three touchdown passes and one touchdown run in a 34-17 win over the Falcons.Five touchdown passes and one touchdown run in a 43-36 win over the Titans.Four touchdown passes in a 35-13 win over the Ravens.Four touchdown passes in a 31-24 win over the Dolphins.

Manning became the first quarterback to have five straight games with four touchdowns in Weeks 8-12 of 2004 with the Colts, when he had a six-touchdown game, two five-touchdown games and two four-touchdown games. (All of Manning's touchdowns were passing, not rushing.) Manning was named NFL MVP that season.

And Burrow is an MVP candidate with the way he's playing now. A win over the Steelers on Sunday night would put the Bengals in first place in the AFC North, and a big game with those big stakes would give a big boost to Burrow's MVP chances.

#2762. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.

#2761. Flight disruptions continue after FAA lifts ground stop, some college football games delayed

Flight disruptions continued on Saturday and could extend through the weekend as airlines raced to recover from the Federal Aviation Administration's longest nationwide groundstop since 9/11, a measure the Trump administration blamed on a communications equipment failure in air traffic control.

More than 3,000 U.S. flights were canceled Saturday and close to 1,000 were delayed as of 5:45 a.m. ET, according to flight-tracking site FlightAware. That's on top of about 9,000 U.S. flight cancellations and more than 7,000 delays on Friday, though some disruptions were due to bad weather.

Part of the job of untangling from the ground stop includes getting aircraft where they need to be. The ground stop forced many planes to divert to other airports.

Airlines run one of the lightest schedules on Saturday and are deploying as many spare planes and crews as possible to get travelers and employees where they need to be as they're eager to start fresh for the upcoming Thanksgiving holiday period.

Around 3 million people would typically be scheduled to fly on Saturday, according to aviation data firm Cirium.

The FAA had halted departing U.S. flights around 4 p.m. on Friday, a ground stop that lasted more than four hours. Routers were overloaded with data and started giving air traffic control centers inconsistent information, so the U.S. issued the ground stop, Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said at a news conference late Friday.

He blamed outdated U.S. infrastructure for the problem and said Congress needs to approve more money to overhaul the country's air traffic control. Aging equipment and a shortage of air traffic controllers have angered airline executives for years, who have complained about the cost of resulting disruptions.

It was the second time the FAA has issued a nationwide ground stop since the Sept. 11, 2001 terror attacks. Trump administration officials said it did not appear to be a cyberattack.

The U.S. has the world's busiest airspace.

"Before clearing flights to resume, we thoroughly tested our systems to confirm they were stable and that operations remain reliable," the FAA said late Friday.

The disruption also upended travel for NBA, NHL and college teams, which struggled to reach their destinations in time for games this weekend.

Duffy said college football teams traveling on charter flights could receive priority.

"I would encourage those football teams to reach out to the FAA to schedule their flight times and make sure they have games going off tomorrow," he said during a Friday press briefing.

The Southeastern Conference announced on X that some games had been moved to Sunday due to the flight disruption.

"We are in close communication with our member institutions to assess potential disruptions and coordinate appropriate responses," the SEC said in a statement shared on X.

#2758. South Carolina? USC? Michigan? Will any team finish the women’s college basketball season undefeated?

Currently, 19 of the 25 teams ranked in the AP poll are undefeated. That number will quickly shrink as the days and weeks progress.

However, three programs have as good a chance as any of not only remaining undefeated the longest, but also completing a perfect season.

Here are three teams with the best chance of having such a special year. In the comments, let us know if you agree, while also noting if you think there's another team that could run the table.

Only 10 NCAA women's college basketball programs have had perfect seasons. South Carolina and head coach Dawn Staley were the last to do it in 2023-24.

This season, they could get it done again. SC has a balanced attack with five players currently scoring in double figures. But, Joyce Edwards is the head of the snake and has started off on the right foot, averaging 15.3 points and six rebounds per game.

The Gamecocks will likely be favored in every game they play this season.

After losing last year's title game, they also should have the drive and determination to maximize their potential, win a championship and maybe even finish the season as a perfect team.

If South Carolina is going to be perfect, they'll have to go through the other SC: Southern California.

The second battle to be "The Real SC" will take place on the Gamecocks' home court on Sunday, Nov. 15, with the winner not only coming out on top, but also clearing their path to a lossless year.

If USC earns the result, their next potential loss is difficult to see.

USC only has two other matchups against teams currently ranked in the top five. The Trojans, certainly, have the talent to win every contest. JuJu Watkins is considered by most to be the best player in college basketball. She is averaging 24.3 points, 8.3 rebounds and 4.7 assists per game.

While USC will only go as far as Watkins carries them, that burden isn't hers alone.

Saniyah Hall has been fantastic, averaging 22 points per game. Sophomore Jazzy Davidson is even better than last year. She is scoring 22 points and 6.7 rebounds per game. And let's not forget Kennedy Smith. She's the glue that keeps the backcourt together and is a leader for the team.

Michigan will have something to say about who wins the national title.

Can they get greedy and aim for perfection?

They've already been tested and passed with flying colors, beating the then-No. 2 UConn, 77-66.

The Wolverines have a great mix of elite talents. Olivia Olson is averaging 17.7 points per game, and Te'Yala Delfosse is scoring 14.7 points in every contest. Her monster 22 points and nine rebounds were a big reason why the Wolverines beat the Huskies.

With a soft schedule for the rest of the month, Michigan should remain undefeated until they play some ranked teams in December.

Where things get interesting is in January. If they beat No. 3 USC on Jan. 30, then a perfect season would suddenly go from delusional to doable.

As 2026 comes to a close and 2027 approaches, let's circle back on this. When will these teams lose? Will they keep on winning? Is there another team than can remain unbeaten longer than any of these teams?

#2757. Drew Mestemaker is one win away from crashing the Heisman race

Oklahoma State quarterback Drew Mestemaker has already led one upset over a ranked team at home this season. He's looking to do it again against Texas Tech on Saturday, Nov. 14, although this time he could potentially claim even more than just a win.

Mestemaker, who ranks second nationally with 2,962 passing yards and first nationally with 29 passing touchdowns this season, is near the top of not only NFL draft boards, but also the Heisman Trophy race. There will be lots of eyes on Mestemaker and Oklahoma State in Week 11, especially with it being a night game against a top opponent with Big 12 implications on the line.

Expert picks for every Top 25 game: College football Week 11 predictions

Mestemaker has done it all for the Cowboys and first-year coach Eric Morris this season, leading the program to one of the best turnarounds in college football after winning only one game in 2025. It's a big reason he's expected to be the first quarterback off the board in the 2027 NFL Draft, even if he hasn't declared yet.

The former walk-on from North Texas who never started a game at quarterback in high school isn't thinking about his draft status or what's to come, though, despite a potential win over Texas Tech placing him in the elite tier of Heisman candidates this season.

"No," Mestemaker said when asked if he's thought of the NFL Draft yet. "I think this team is special and this season is special, honestly, and there's no point in thinking about that after because there's nothing I can do about it right now."

The 6-3 quarterback led the country in passing yards (4,379) as a first-year starter last season at North Texas, nearly leading the Mean Green to the College Football Playoff after scoring 39 total touchdowns. Mestemaker and his two top weapons — running back Caleb Hawkins and receiver Wyatt Young — then followed Morris to Oklahoma State, where the trio has emerged as three of the best offensive players in the Big 12 this season.

Mestemaker garnered some hype as a future top-tier prospect after his first season as a starting quarterback since middle school at North Texas last season, but questions still remained. His play against Power Four opponents — including his 426 total yards with three touchdowns in a 39-31 win over Oregon earlier this season — eased those questions, with 17 of 25 anonymous scouts in a recent poll by ESPN favoring Mestemaker as the top quarterback in the upcoming NFL draft.

Mestemaker isn't talking about the NFL Draft anytime soon, a stance that Morris said his other NFL quarterbacks he has coached like Patrick Mahomes and Cam Ward also took in college.

"We already had the discussion we're not going to talk about this stuff until the end of the season," Morris said. "I think that's right for everybody in this program. He owes that to our team. I owe that to this team. So, we'll continue going and chopping this wood every single day and at the end of the year just sit down and have a discussion with him and his mom and figure out what's best for the kid and his future.

"This isn't the first time I've had that conversation and nor will it be the last time I have that conversation."

Texas Tech is coming off back-to-back one-score wins over Arizona and West Virginia, with its offense taking a step forward after changing to Tommy Castellanos at quarterback. Mestemaker and Oklahoma State's offense has had to dig its poor defense out of numerous holes this season, setting up for a potential back-and-forth game on Saturday night.

There's a lot on the line for Mestemaker against the Red Raiders, with Oklahoma State staying in the Big 12 championship game race.

Have a big performance and a Heisman could be within his grasp, too.

"I just love playing football and this week is a dream football game (vs. Texas Tech) you dream about playing and so my close circle and my friends and family we don't talk about that," Mestemaker said. "There might be a decision after the season to make, but that's an after the season decision to make."

Here's a look at where Mestemaker's latest projections for the 2027 NFL Draft:

USA TODAY: No. 4 overall to the Philadelphia Eagles

The Athletic: No. 3 overall to the Miami Dolphins

2024 (North Texas): 30-of-46 passing (65.2%) for 462 yards with two touchdowns to two interceptions; 14 carries for 87 yards with a touchdown

2025 (North Texas): 319-of-463 passing (68.9%) for 4,379 yards with 34 touchdowns to nine interceptions; 57 carries for 89 yards with five touchdowns

2026 (Oklahoma State): 206-of-309 passing (66.7%) for 2,962 yards with 29 touchdowns to eight interceptions; 36 carries for 177 yards with three touchdowns

#2747. Day’Ron Sharpe is making another leap in rare Nets bright spot

MEMPHIS, Tenn. — Day'Ron Sharpe is making the jump from backup bruiser to full-time starter and complete player.

And so far, the transition has been going well.

Sharpe has an impressive plus-16.7 net rating swing for Brooklyn entering Saturday's game in Memphis. Essentially meaning they are an aggregate 16.7 points per 100 possessions better — offensively and defensively — with the big man on the floor.

That's the fourth-best rating in the entire league among all qualified centers.

"Yeah, he's embracing the new role, and he's adjusting," Nets coach Jordi Fernández said of Sharpe. "He's adjusting with playing bigger minutes. … It comes with, its a lot. And I think he's doing a great job. He is being very diligent with his routine, his body, taking care of himself, his treatment, what he eats. And right now, you can see that he can perform in longer stints and for more minutes.

Brooklyn Nets center Day'ron Sharpe (20) shoots a layup against the Charlotte Hornets on Wednesday, Nov. 4, 2026, at Barclays Center in Brooklyn, N.Y. Corey Sipkin for the NY POST

"So, very proud of him, and I think he's impacting the group also. We're playing well and, at times, very well because of him. And what he brings, like yesterday or two days ago, he had huge winning plays at the rim and verticalities and then his passing, rebounding so forth. So I'm very happy and he's got to keep taking steps, keep working."

Over Sharpe's last six games, he's averaged 9.5 points, seven rebounds and 6.5 assists to just 2.2 turnovers — almost a 3-to-1 assist-to-turnover ratio — as he's unlocked his playmaking in dribble handoffs DHOs with Michael Porter Jr. His four straight games with at least five assists are a Nets franchise record for a center.

Brooklyn Nets center Day'ron Sharpe, left, shoots over New York Knicks center Karl-Anthony Towns at Madison Square Garden on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026. Jason Szenes for NY Post

But more importantly, Sharpe — always a powerful rebounder in shorter stints — has provided rim protection, with his career-high tying four blocks in New Orleans giving him 10 over his last five games.

"I'd just say for me, just trusting the work that I always preach, just being patient when I get the ball and just playing hard usual, just trying to make as many winning plays as I can," said Sharpe. "Like I said before the season started, I feel like I'm trying to be one of the best when it comes to the small details of the game. Extra plays, saving plays, it's all about the position game to me. So as many possessions as I can help our team win, that's what matters."

"Nolan just practiced with the group; see how he feels after," said Fernández. "And then Mikel hasn't practiced, he's not here. See how he feels (Saturday)."

Tight end Elijah Higgins has agreed to a three-year extension, according to a joint report from ESPN and NFL Network.

Higgins’ deal is worth up to $26.8 million and puts him under contract through 2029.

A sixth-round pick in 2023, Higgins was waived by the Dolphins at roster cuts as a rookie. But he was claimed by the Cardinals and has been with the club ever since.

Higgins has played 54 games with 23 starts, tallying 82 catches for 798 yards with four touchdowns.

Playing alongside start tight end Trey McBride, Higgins has 18 receptions for 162 yards with a touchdown in nine games so far in 2026.
